Küresel piyasalar Fed haftasına karışık seyirle başladı

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Geçen hafta küresel piyasalar Orta Doğu'da giderek artan jeopolitik riskler, yapay zeka yatırımlarının finansmanına ilişkin soru işaretleri ve bilançolardan alınan karışık sinyallerle negatif seyrederken, söz konusu endişeler yeni haftada da etkisini sürdürüyor.

ABD'de 3 Kasım'da yapılacak ara seçimlerin ardından ABD-İran çatışmasının yeniden tırmanma olasılığı, piyasalarda risk iştahını törpülüyor. Öte yandan gözler bu hafta Fed'in faiz kararına çevrildi. Fed'in politika faizini sabit bırakması bekleniyor. Analistler, toplantıda enflasyon baskıları karşısında bundan sonraki faiz politikasına ilişkin verilecek mesajların önem taşıdığını belirtti.

Diğer taraftan bu hafta ABD'de büyüme ve kişisel tüketim harcamaları verileri ile hızlanan bilanço sezonunda Amazon, Microsoft ve Alphabet'in bilançoları takip edilecek.

ABD Başkanı Donald Trump, İran'da başladığı işi bitirmek zorunda kalabileceğini ifade ederek, "İran'ı tekrar bombalama ihtimalim var." dedi. Katarlı arabulucuların, ABD ile İran arasındaki arabuluculuk görüşmeleri kapsamında İran'ın başkenti Tahran'a gittiğine dair haberlerin ardından aralık vadeli Brent petrolün varil fiyatı yüzde 1,9 azalışla 114,5 dolara geriledi.

Teknoloji şirketlerinin hisselerine yönelik endişelerden dolayı yatırımcıların güvenli liman talebinin artmasıyla ABD'nin 10 yıllık tahvil faizi 3 baz puan azalışla yüzde 4,97'de seyrediyor.

Dolar endeksi ise ABD 10 yıllık tahvil faizindeki düşüşün etkisiyle yüzde 0,2 azalışla 101,7 seviyesine indi. Tahvil faizleri ve dolar endeksindeki düşüşten destek bulan altının onsu yüzde 0,5 artışla 4 bin 15 dolardan işlem görüyor.

Bu arada Brezilya'da devlet başkanlığı seçiminin ikinci turunu resmi olmayan sonuçlara göre Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva kazandı. Bu gelişmeyle dolar/Brezilya reali paritesi yüzde 0,1 azalışla 4,9780 seviyesinde seyrediyor.

Kesin olmayan seçim sonuçlarının piyasa beklentilerinin aksine gerçekleşmesiyle Tokyo'da işlem gören Brezilya hisse senetlerinden oluşan bir borsa yatırım fonu yüzde 15 civarında değer kaybetti.

New York borsası, yapay zeka yatırımlarına ilişkin endişelerle teknoloji hisselerinde görülen satış baskısının diğer sektörlere yayılması ve Orta Doğu'da süren jeopolitik gerilimin petrol fiyatlarını yükseltmesiyle cuma gününü düşüşle tamamladı.

Kurumsal gelişmeler de piyasaların seyrinde etkili olurken, SpaceX'in ABD genelinde kablosuz iletişim ağı kurmak amacıyla hücresel altyapı şirketleriyle görüşmeler yürüttüğüne ilişkin haberlerin ardından telekomünikasyon hisseleri geriledi.

Artan rekabete ilişkin endişelerle AT&T'nin hisseleri yüzde 7,96, Verizon'un hisseleri yüzde 1,18 ve T-Mobile'ın hisseleri yüzde 7,71 değer kaybetti. SpaceX hisseleri ise yüzde 1,89 yükseldi.

American Express'in hisseleri de şirketin gelirinin ve karının bu yılın üçüncü çeyreğinde artmasına karşın küresel iş gücünün yaklaşık yüzde 5'ini azaltacağını açıklaması sonrası yüzde 3,62 düştü.

Makroekonomik veri tarafında ise Michigan Üniversitesince ölçülen tüketici güven endeksi, ekimde yukarı yönlü revize edilerek 48 oldu. Söz konusu revizyona rağmen endeks 5 ayın en düşük seviyesinde kaldı.

ABD'de imalat sanayi Satınalma Yöneticileri Endeksi (PMI), ekimde 56 değerini alarak 53 ayın en yüksek seviyesine çıktı ve piyasa beklentilerinin üzerinde gerçekleşti.

Söz konusu gelişmelerle Dow Jones endeksi yüzde 1,23, S&P 500 endeksi yüzde 0,52 ve Nasdaq endeksi de yüzde 0,21 değer kaybetti.

ABD'de endeks vadeli kontratlar güne pozitif başladı.

Avrupa borsaları, cuma gününü Fransa'da devam eden protestolar ve artan petrol fiyatlarının etkisiyle İtalya hariç düşüşle tamamladı.

Fransa'nın 10 yıllık tahvil faizi yüzde 5,07'ye çıkarak 2002'den bu yana en yüksek seviyeyi gördü.

Uluslararası kredi derecelendirme kuruluşu Moody's, Fransa'nın kredi notlarına yönelik periyodik incelemesini tamamladığını bildirdi.

Moody's'ten yapılan açıklamada, Fransa'nın "Aa3" olan kredi notunun, zengin ve çeşitlendirilmiş ekonomisi ile güçlü kamu kurumları tarafından desteklendiği ifade edilerek, parçalı parlamento yapısı, siyasi ve sosyal istikrarsızlık, yüksek ve artan borç yükü ile borç karşılama metriklerindeki zayıflamanın temel kredi zorluklarını oluşturduğu vurgulandı.

Açıklamada, önceki incelemeden bu yana ekonomik büyümenin yavaşlaması ve bütçe açığının artması nedeniyle Fransa'nın borç yükünün beklentilerin üzerine çıktığı aktarıldı.

Bu sürede, devlet tahvili faizlerinin beklenenden daha hızlı yükseldiğine işaret edilen açıklamada, ayrıca mali konsolidasyon çabalarını kısıtlayabilecek geniş çaplı sosyal protestoların patlak verdiğine dikkat çekildi.

Makroekonomik veri tarafına bakıldığında Avro Bölgesi'nde, ekim ayına ilişkin imalat sanayi Satınalma Yöneticileri Endeksi (PMI) 52,4 ile beklentilerin hafif altında kalırken, Almanya'da ekim ayına ilişkin imalat sanayi PMI 54,2 ile beklentilerin üzerinde gerçekleşti.

İngiltere'de imalat PMI ise 52,8 puanla 5 ayın en iyi performansı olarak kayıtlara geçti.

Fransa'nın ekim ayına ilişkin bileşik Satınalma Yöneticileri Endeksi (PMI) öncü verileri, ülkedeki ekonomik faaliyette daralmaya işaret etti. Fransa'da eylülde 51,1 olan bileşik PMI, ekimde 48,4'e inerek son 4 ayın en düşük seviyesine geriledi.

Bu hafta Avrupa Merkez Bankası'nın (ECB) faiz kararı Avrupa piyasalarının odağında yer alacak. Bankanın 3 temel politika faizini sabit tutması bekleniyor.

Analistler, ilerleyen dönemlerde enerji maliyetlerinin daha da yükselmesi ve Avrupa ekonomisinin büyüme görünümünün zayıf kalması durumunda bankanın enflasyonla mücadele ve ekonomik aktivite arasında ikilemde kalabileceğini söyledi.

Öte yandan İngiltere Maliye Bakanı John Healey'nin çarşamba günü ilk bütçe sunumunu yapması bekleniyor. Küresel borçlanma maliyetlerinin artması ve ABD/İsrail-İran Savaşı'nın yol açtığı yüksek enerji fiyatları İngiltere ekonomisini de kayda değer bir şekilde etkiliyor.

Cuma günü Almanya'da DAX endeksi yüzde 0,75, İngiltere'de FTSE endeksi yüzde 0,32 ve Fransa'da CAC 40 endeksi ise yüzde 0,36 değer kaybederken, İtalya'da FTSE MIB endeksi yüzde 0,36 değer kazandı.

Avrupa'da endeks vadeli kontratlar, güne pozitif başladı.

New York borsasında cuma günü görülen düşüşler yeni haftada Asya piyasalarına da taşınırken, bölgede borsalar yapay zeka temasına ilişkin endişelerin devam etmesiyle satış ağırlıklı seyrediyor.

Çin’de iç talebe ilişkin endişelerin devam etmesi ülke pay piyasaları üzerinde satış baskısının oluşmasına neden oluyor.

Asya tarafında bu hafta Japonya Merkez Bankasının (BoJ) faiz kararı takip edilecek. Piyasa beklentileri bankanın bu toplantıda politika faizini değiştirmeyeceği yönünde şekilleniyor.

Söz konusu gelişmelerle kapanışa yakın Japonya'da Nikkei 225 endeksi yüzde 0,3, Çin'de Şanghay bileşik endeksi yüzde 0,2, Güney Kore'de Kospi endeksi yüzde 0,7 düştü. Hong Kong'da Hang Seng endeksi ise yüzde 0,4 yükseldi.

Cuma günü satış ağırlıklı bir seyir izleyen Borsa İstanbul'da BIST 100 endeksi, günü yüzde 0,92 değer kaybederek 11.116,99 puandan tamamladı.

Borsa İstanbul Vadeli İşlem ve Opsiyon Piyasası'nda (VİOP) BIST 30 endeksine dayalı ekim vadeli kontrat ise cuma günü akşam seansında normal seans kapanışına göre yatay bir seyir izledi.

Dolar/TL cuma gününü yüzde 0,1 artışla 49,6937'den tamamlarken, bugün bankalararası piyasanın açılışında önceki kapanışın yüzde 0,1 üzerinde 49,7580'den işlem görüyor.

Diğer taraftan Uluslararası Para Fonunun (IMF) ülkelerin mali istatistik, bütçe yönetimi, mali risk yönetimi ve hesap verebilirlik gibi kamu mali yönetimi uygulamalarını incelediği Mali Şeffaflık Değerlendirmesi güncellemesi kapsamında Türkiye'ye ilişkin raporu yayımlandı.

Türkiye'nin mali şeffaflık alanında son 10 yılda önemli gelişme kaydettiği belirtilen raporda, ülkenin kamu mali yönetiminde son yıllardaki ilerleme seviyesiyle değerlendirme süreci tamamlanan ve aralarında Birleşik Krallık, Finlandiya, İrlanda, Avusturya ve Portekiz'in de bulunduğu yaklaşık 40 ülke arasında en üst sıralarda yer aldığı kaydedildi.

Analistler, bugün yurt içinde veri gündeminin sakin olacağını, yurt dışında ise Almanya'da Ifo iş dünyası güven endeksinin takip edileceğini belirterek, teknik açıdan BIST 100 endeksinde 11.100 ve 11.000 puanın destek, 11.200 ve 11.300 puanın direnç konumunda olduğunu kaydetti.

Updated - October 26, 2026 09:08 am IST - PORT HURON

President Donald Trump speaks during a campaign rally at McMorran Place Arena in Port Huron, Mich., October 25, 2026. | Photo Credit: AP

President Donald Trump at a Sunday (October 25, 2026) campaign rally ratcheted ‌up his push to link U.S. Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed to Muslim extremists, comparing him to the 9/11 ​attackers while saying Democrats would let violent anti-American terrorists into the United States.

"I'm here to keep a jihadist, ⁠Muslim Brotherhood member from becoming a United States senator," the President told a receptive audience in Port Huron, which is in a Michigan area where 66% of the vote went to Mr. Trump in the 2024 presidential election.

If Mr. El-Sayed wins, he would be the first Muslim elected to the U.S. Senate. ‌Mr. Trump has sought to paint him as an anti-American Muslim radical, and on Sunday (October 25, 2026) he took the rhetoric to a new level, likening Mr. El-Sayed to the attackers who took over United Flight 93 during the September 11, ‌2001, attacks.

Michigan has one of the nation's most competitive U.S. Senate contests going into the November 3 midterm elections, ‌with former ⁠U.S. Representative Mike Rogers, a Republican, and Mr. El-Sayed vying for an open seat that will help to decide ⁠control of the chamber.

The President on Sunday (October 25, 2026) compared voting against Mr. El-Sayed to the actions of Michigan native Todd Beamer and others who fought back against the Flight 93 hijackers.

"Twenty-five years later, Michigan needs to stand up and fight, just like Todd did, to stop a jihadist from walking into the Capitol," said Mr. Trump.

If Mr. El-Sayed ​wins, "he would be the only senator that will have ‌to go through a metal detector before he's allowed to go in the Senate," Mr. Trump said.

Mr. El-Sayed has dismissed the accusations against him, and at a Sunday (October 25, 2026) night event, he suggested that Mr. Trump's frequent emphasis of Mr. El-Sayed's name contains a measure of hypocrisy.

"I get that the President of the United States takes personal offence to my first name," Mr. El-Sayed told an audience ‌in Grand Rapids, Michigan. "He doesn’t mind as much when it’s a shared name from the Saudis who are funding ​his son-in-law’s private equity, doesn’t seem to mind as much when it’s the Qataris who are giving him a $400 million private jet."

Affinity Partners, Mr. Trump son-in-law Jared Kushner's investment fund, has reportedly received billions from a Saudi Government ⁠entity. Qatar gifted Mr. Trump a Boeing 747 jet.

The mostly White crowd at Sunday's (October 25, 2026) rally cheered Mr. Trump’s comments on Mr. El-Sayed. Some attendees yelled “traitor” and others yelled “deport,” although Mr. El-Sayed was born in Michigan.

Terrance Camburn, a 52-year-old disabled Army veteran from Sandusky, ‌Michigan, said he supported Mr. Trump’s criticism because of Mr. El-Sayed’s “political beliefs.”

“I’ve been down to Dearborn and I’ve seen many of the things he agrees with,” Mr. Camburn said when asked about Mr. Trump’s 9/11 comments.

Dearborn, Michigan, has the highest per-capita concentration of Muslims of any U.S. city.

As he left the venue after the rally, Mr. Rogers did not respond to Reuters' questions about Mr. Trump's invocation of 9/11. But earlier on Sunday (October 25, 2026), he said on NBC's "Meet the Press" that he did not agree with the notion that Mr. El-Sayed is a violent terrorist.

"I've never called him a terrorist," Mr. Rogers told NBC.

Mr. Rogers has alleged without evidence that Mr. El-Sayed has ties to the Muslim Brotherhood, one of the ‌Arab world's oldest and most influential Islamist groups.

Mr. El-Sayed has denied affiliation with the Muslim Brotherhood, saying it is "shameful" for Mr. Rogers to question his allegiance to the ​United States.

Mr. Rogers told "Meet the Press" that Mr. El-Sayed had not sufficiently responded to questions about the allegations.

"He's a Democrat Socialist of America-aligned candidate who has these pretty radical support groups that don't like America," he said.

Mr. Trump's speech ⁠in eastern Michigan on Sunday (October 25, 2026) came a day after former President Barack Obama campaigned for Democrats in the State, underscoring its importance in ⁠next week's elections.

At a Saturday (October 24, 2026) rally in Detroit, Mr. Obama framed the attacks by Mr. El-Sayed's opponents as an attempt to make him look "less American than they are."

The former President compared them to his own experience as the target of false claims ‌that he was born in Kenya. "This stuff didn't work on me," Mr. Obama said. "Not in Michigan. It's not going to work on Abdul."

Democrats need to gain just a handful of seats each in the U.S. Senate and House of Representatives to overturn ​Republicans' narrow 53-47 and 218-214 majorities.

Five economic challenges facing Brazil's re-elected President Lula

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published - October 26, 2026 07:24 am IST - BRASILIA

President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva celebrates with his running mate Geraldo Alckmin after winning a fourth term in the presidential runoff in Brazil on October 25, 2026 | Photo Credit: AP

Brazil's economy has beaten expectations in recent years, but mounting debt and entrenched structural problems could complicate leftist President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's fourth ​term, which begins in January.

Here are the five main economic challenges he faces:

Brazil’s public debt has surged ‌more than 10 percentage points during Mr. Lula’s current term to over 82% of GDP, ​driving up borrowing costs.

The root cause is years of rising mandatory spending, leaving under ⁠8% of the federal budget adjustable.

Mr. Lula has sent mixed fiscal signals, at times dismissing debt worries as “nonsense” while arguing that strong economic growth would fix the problem.

His team has pledged to keep the government's current budget rules, which cap spending ‌growth and set primary balance targets — revenues minus expenditures, excluding interest payments on public debt — and hinted at tightening them.

Hitting those targets will be harder given Mr. Lula’s promise to raise ‌the minimum wage faster than inflation, which also lifts pension costs, after already raising welfare benefits and promising ‌new ⁠spending.

Even with interest rates at 13.75%, Brazil’s annual inflation ⁠of 4.6% remains above the central bank's tolerance range of 1.5% to 4.5%.

The central bank delivered its fifth straight rate cut last month on growing signs of an economic slowdown, but markets expect inflation to remain above the official 3% target ​until as late as 2029.

Mr. Lula’s platform promises lower ‌interest rates through fiscal discipline that spares social programs, but most economists say rates will only fall faster if Brazil cuts spending, and it’s unclear how he will square the two.

A record nearly 84 million Brazilians and 9.3 million businesses are behind on debt payments, according ‌to credit bureau Serasa, as high interest rates bite.

That has fueled public dissatisfaction despite strong indicators ​such as record-low unemployment and wage growth outpacing inflation.

Mr. Lula plans a new round of debt relief, this time through discounted purchases of distressed household loans from banks, plus tax ⁠debt forgiveness for some small business owners. Analysts say such measures offer only temporary relief.

The government has also discussed with the central bank ways to rein in heavy use of easy but expensive credit, such ‌as credit cards and unsecured consumer loans, though it has not detailed any proposals.

Mr. Lula will likely struggle to push his economic agenda through Congress after voters elected the most conservative legislature since Brazil’s return to democracy in the 1980s.

Although he defeated right-wing Senator Flavio Bolsonaro in Sunday’s (October 25, 2026) runoff, Mr. Bolsonaro’s Liberal Party and its allies are set to control both chambers.

Mr. Lula’s aides have signalled that he will keep pushing to curtail tax breaks, but lawmakers have repeatedly resisted measures that raise the effective tax burden.

During ‌his current term, Mr. Lula has struggled to name nominees and win Senate approval for them at key institutions, including the Supreme ​Court, central bank, securities regulator, and antitrust watchdog, and that is likely to be even more difficult ahead with the rightward shift of Congress.

Mr. Lula won congressional approval ⁠in 2023 for a sweeping overhaul of Brazil’s consumption taxes, and a new value-added tax is set to ⁠take effect in January.

With under three months to go, the government has still not disclosed the effective rate, which independent analysts estimate will be around 28%.

Fearing a backlash during the campaign, ‌the administration has also held off on sending Congress a bill to regulate a new tax on products considered harmful to health or the environment, such as cigarettes and alcohol.

That delay could ​push back revenue from the tax and has fuelled criticism of the government’s handling of tax policy.

Record-breaking trainer’s warning for punters on Melbourne Cup favourite

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You have reached your maximum number of saved items.

Remove items from your saved list to add more.

You've used all of your gift articles. 3 more will become available on the 1st of every month.

Ciaron Maher says national pride is on the line in next Tuesday’s Melbourne Cup as he prepares a record number of runners to do battle against legendary Irish trainer Joseph O’Brien’s five starters in the big race.

Maher is on track to have eight runners in the Melbourne Cup, including the favourite Point of Law, who beat two O’Brien-trained horses Small Fry and Omni Man in the Geelong Cup.

He not only gained a golden ticket but denied the Irish trainer a chance to have a sixth runner in the great race.

Alongside Point of Law, Maher has Saint George, Juja Kibo, Middle Earth, Mr Monaco, Shockletz, Royal Supremacy and Zahrann sitting inside the top 24 in the order of entry while a ninth entrant Kings Valley is battling for a start.

The previous record was when Robert Hickmott saddled up six runners for owner Lloyd Williams in 2013.

O’Brien will race last year’s runner-up Goodie Two Shoes as well as Defiantly, Sons and Lovers, Trustyourinstinct and James J Braddock.

It means the master trainers from opposite sides of the world are likely to saddle up half the field and have the top six fancied runners in the race.

Maher agreed that national pride was on the line on Tuesday as local trainers battled the international raiders including O’Brien, who he described as “always a force to be reckoned with”.

“We don’t want [the Cup] going overseas. We want it staying here,” Maher said.

Australian trainers have won the past five Melbourne Cups after sharing six of the previous 11 Cups with overseas-based trainers. Apart from O’Brien’s quintet, there are four other horses with international trainers likely to gain a start in the race.

Maher won the race with Gold Trip in 2022 and finished third with Middle Earth last year behind Half Yours and O’Brien’s Goodie Two Shoes. O’Brien won the race in 2017 with Rekindling and 2020 with Twilight Payment.

Despite flying under the radar in the lead up, Maher rates Middle Earth among his best chances because he is a seasoned stayer who he believes is in better form now than he was this time last year.

But Point of Law has caught the attention of the O’Brien stable, their racing manager Kevin Blake saying they are “fearful of him”.

“He has clearly stepped forward since he’s been here and Ciaron will be hoping he steps forward again for the big one which he clearly has the potential to do,” Blake said.

Blake said he wasn’t prepared to declare just yet the best credentialled of O’Brien’s five runners with barriers and weather among the variables to be considered. He described the crop as “a solid bunch of stayers”.

Maher agreed Point of Law was one of his best chances and his ability was undeniable, but he sounded a word of warning for punters, reminding them the Cup will be just his sixth start.

“Whether he is seasoned enough will be the thing,” Maher said.

Maher declared Juja Kibo to be in career best form and King George’s run in the Cox Plate when he “rocketed home” had him excited.

Shockletz, who will carry just 51 kilograms in the Cup, reminds Maher of High Emocean which carried a light weight to run third in 2022, and he said Mr Monaco was a genuine two-miler.

The market has his other runners Zahrann and Royal Supremacy well down in betting.

O’Brien is due to arrive on Monday evening after attending the Breeders Cup meeting on the weekend.

Meantime, Melbourne Cup-winning jockey Jamie Melham will not ride on Derby Day after her attempt to overturn a suspension failed on appeal.

Racing NSW ruled on Monday that her four-meeting ban for careless riding aboard Marhoona would stand. However, she will return to the saddle at Flemington on Melbourne Cup Day.

Melham is yet to finalise a Cup ride but is expected to ride in the Cup, which she won aboard Half Yours last year.

Half Yours was withdrawn from this year’s race after suffering lacerations when he fell in the Caulfield Cup.

Brazil runoff live updates: Bolsonaro says it is ‘impossible to concede’ election result after historic Lula win

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Leftist president Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva defeated the far-right senator and was elected president of Brazil for a fourth non-consecutive term

Full report: Lula wins fourth term as Brazil’s president over Flávio Bolsonaro

Far-right senator Flávio Bolsonaro said it was “impossible to concede the election result” and that his legal team would seek to have the election annulled.

Following in the footsteps of his father, former president Jair Bolsonaro, who four years ago also refused to acknowledge his defeat by Lula – before attempting a coup and being convicted for it – the senator cited a supreme court justice’s decision to extend voting hours as the alleged reason.

Although Bolsonaro had also requested an extension of voting hours, the senator said Justice Cristiano Zanin’s decision to extend it from 5pm to 7pm “was not normal, it was unacceptable” and amounted to “changing the rules of the game while the game was being played”.

Bolsonaro said the decision had come from “Lula’s friends on the supreme court”, pointing out that Zanin had previously represented the leftwing president as his lawyer and had been appointed to the court by him.

The justice ordered the extension minutes after 5pm, the scheduled closing time for polling stations, after fellow justice Kassio Nunes Marques spent hours without ruling on Lula’s campaign request. During the campaign, Marques, appointed by Jair Bolsonaro and current head of the electoral court, had been criticised for bias in rulings favouring the far-right senator.

Lula supporters have poured into Brazilian streets after his election win and the defeat of rival far-right candidate Flávio Bolsonaro.

In São Paulo, the Guardian’s Tom Phillips reports that crowds are celebrating in a street party that is set to go until dawn to mark Lula’s historic victory.

Outside Bolsonaro’s mansion in Brasília, the Guardian’s Tiago Rogero reports that the senator has declared it “impossible to concede Lula’s victory” and vowed to seek the election’s annulment.

See vision from both cities in our video here.

Lula’s reelection win has set up ⁠a test for financial markets, with investors expecting recent bets on a change of government to unwind in a volatile week ahead.

Markets had moved sharply in anticipation of a rightwing shift ⁠since Flávio Bolsonaro unexpectedly finished first in the election’s opening round three weeks ago. Stocks closed on Friday almost 7% above their level going into the first round, while the real ⁠currency was nearly 5% stronger against the US dollar.

Interest-rate futures had indicated expectations that tighter public finances would allow borrowing costs to decline, Reuters reports.

“We expect a sharp market reaction on Monday, with aggressive repricing, a ⁠selloff in assets and interest rate futures likely to hit their upper limit shortly after the open,” said Luis Felipe Vital, head ​of macro strategy at Warren Investimentos.

Over the next four years, ‌Lula must slow the growth ‌of Brazil’s debt while protecting social programs and investment.

“We expect the rally that followed the first round to be largely priced out, and sentiment to stay weak until a clear fiscal plan emerges,” said Alejo Czerwonko, chief investment officer for emerging markets at UBS Global Wealth Management.

The final tally after all votes in Brazil’s presidential runoff poll were counted is:

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva – Workers’ party (PT): 51.79%, 64,256,232 votes

Flávio Bolsonaro – Liberal party (PL): 48.21%, 59,826,041 votes

You can see a state-by-state breakdown here.

I’ve just been speaking to Lula’s overjoyed health minister, Alexandre Padilha.

“This is a huge victory for the Brazilian people who in the second round had the chance to think about what President Lula means for the environment, for the defence of our national health service and our education system and for the Brazilian constitution,” he told me, clutching my hand with emotion as he spoke.

double quotation markAnd [to think about] the risks that the extreme-right candidate [Bolsonaro] entailed.

It’s a massive victory, not just for Brazil. It’s also an important signal to the rest of Latin America and to the world.

Tens of thousands of Lula supporters have poured onto Paulista Avenue at the heart of São Paulo to celebrate his historic election win.

The mood is of absolute ecstasy and relief after Lula managed to stage an unprecedented second-round comeback against his far-right rival Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of jailed former president Jair Bolsonaro.

As fireworks blasted into the night sky, a group of revellers hoisted a sign reading “Dictatorship never again” onto the roof of one of the avenue’s metro stations.

“I feel amazing. Brazil is fucking incredible. Lula is our father,” said Leonardo Machado, a 44-year-old Lula supporter as he hit his city’s most famous street to toast the president’s triumph.

It’s nearly 1am in Brazil now and the party looks likely to go on until sunrise.

Lula’s dramatic win is bad news for former Brazilian president Jair Bolsonaro, who is serving a 27-year prison sentence and was hoping to be freed and given a role in government had his son Flávio Bolsonaro prevailed in the poll.

Jair Bolsonaro was jailed for plotting a coup against Lula after the leftwinger won the 2022 election against him.

Lula’s win in this runoff election is also a blow for Donald Trump, the Bolsonaro clan’s most important international ally.

The US president had been hoping Bolsonaro would become the latest in a series of pro-Trump leaders to take power in Latin America, including Abelardo de la Espriella in Colombia, Daniel Noboa in Ecuador, José Antonio Kast in Chile and Javier Milei in Argentina.

There’s more from Tom Phillips and Tiago Rogero here.

Reactions from political leaders internationally have continued to come in after the news of Lula’s win against Flávio Bolsonaro.

Canadian prime minister Mark Carney said:

double quotation markThe ​relationship between Canada and Brazil has flourished under our partnership.

Let’s seize this opportunity to make it even stronger, together – to create ⁠major new trade opportunities, protect the environment and build greater prosperity for both our peoples.

double quotation markI congratulate President Luiz Inacio ⁠Lula da Silva ... and salute the Brazilian people for this democratic day.

double quotation markOur region has common enemies, shared ‌challenges, and much to gain ‌when we work together.

double quotation markI just spoke with the President ‌of Brazil, Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva. Chile and Brazil have a lot of shared future ahead, and we will continue working on an agenda that strengthens relations between our countries in economic integration and the fight against organised crime in the region.

See here for our earlier post on the reactions of other leaders in the region and beyond – including Mexico’s Claudia Sheinbaum and Cuba’s Miguel Díaz-Canel – also care of Reuters.

There is a mixed mood on the streets of Brazil tonight, a few hours on from Lula’s historic victory was announced. In Rio de Janeiro, cars beeped their horns and a huge roar echoed in the street as the result was confirmed.

But Rodrigo Valentim, a 51-year-old businessman and supporter of Bolsonaro, said that another Lula government would harm the economy. He pointed to financial markets’ positive reaction to the result of the first round, when Bolsonaro bested Lula.

“I don’t understand how people can vote for Lula,” he said. “The country is collapsing.”

Many have focused on the supreme court ruling extending voting by two hours, with Aurelio Junior, 45, a dentist in Brasília, calling it a “disgrace”.

double quotation markWe’ve never had anything like this in Brazil, and now they’ve come up with the idea of extending the election period by two hours.

However, Jacqueline Maria Lima de Franca, 34, a business owner in Rio de Janeiro who backed Lula, said it was “more than fair … everyone should have the right to vote, and that was being neglected”.

The Guardian’s Tom Phillips was in the São Paulo conference hall as Lula addressed a packed crowd of ecstatic supporters.

“I am [nearly] 81 … and I am going to do the best term a president has ever done,” Lula vowed.

double quotation markWe are going to change the way that this country is governed … This election was a defeat for denialism … I want to thank God and to thank you, Brazilian men and women of this country … it was democracy that made this happen.

Lula’s vice-president, Geraldo Alckmin, told the crowd:

double quotation markYou saved Brazilian democracy for the second time. Congratulations president Lula.

The president’s supporters chanted back: “Olé. Olé. Olá! Lula! Lula!”

US senator Bernie Sanders has congratulated Lula, saying he has “stood up for Brazilian workers and democracy”.

“Congratulations to Lula on his extraordinary reelection victory in the Brazilian presidential election,” the senator, who is a key figure on the US left, wrote soon after the results were clear.

The president of Brazil’s electoral court, Kassio Nunes Marques, has recognised Lula’s victory, saying “with more than 64 million votes, President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva has been re-elected and will be the 40th President of the Federative Republic of Brazil, for the term beginning on 5 January 2027”.

Marques added that he had called both candidates.

double quotation markTo President Lula, I express the electoral justice system’s recognition of his participation in yet another democratic process and of his victory at the ballot box. His political career spans different periods of Brazil’s democratic history. He has stood as a candidate in eight elections, winning four of them.

To candidate Flávio Bolsonaro, who contested a presidential election for the first time and received a significant number of votes, I offer my congratulations on his campaign.

In a five-minute speech in which he took no questions, and which appeared to have been deliberately timed to begin only after Lula’s televised victory speech had ended, Flávio Bolsonaro made no mention of the fact that his campaign had also asked the electoral court to extend voting hours.

While Lula’s request concerned cities in at least nine states where voters had not been provided with the free public transport guaranteed by law, Bolsonaro justified his request by citing heavy rain in some states.

As well as refusing to concede, the senator told his supporters:

double quotation markI will not give up on my Brazil, and I hope you won’t give up either.

Quelles sont les pistes étudiées par le gouvernement pour améliorer Parcoursup, dont le mouvement lycéen demande la suppression ?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Recevez l'essentiel de l'actualité et restez à jour avec nos newsletters

Activez les notifications franceinfo et ne manquez rien de l’actualité

Vous pouvez changer d’avis à tout moment dans les réglages de votre navigateur.

Le gouvernement doit dévoiler des mesures pour répondre aux demandes de la jeunesse lundi. Au cœur de leurs revendications figure la fin du système d'accès aux études supérieures, que l'exécutif refuse d'abroger.

Pour sauvegarder cet article, connectez-vous ou créez un compte franceinfo

Le ministre de l'Education nationale l'assure : il va présenter lundi 26 octobre "une première série de décisions" pour répondre aux revendications des lycéens. Parmi le sujet brûlants figure en haut de la liste Parcoursup, système d'orientation pour les études supérieures vivement critiqué par les lycéens.

Les annonces font suite à la mise en place d'une "concertation flash". Trois groupes de travail se sont réunis ces derniers jours pour imaginer, et jusqu'à la fin du mois, des réformes pour la plateforme d'admission post-bac. Représentants de lycéens, d'étudiants et de professeurs ont été conviés aux discussions, non sans tensions. Le syndicat Unsa a aussi quitté la réunion dénonçant "un simulacre de concertation tant les revendications lycéennes y ont été caricaturées et tant le plan de solutions proposé ne laisse aucune marge".

Si Emmanuel Macron a évoqué un "besoin d'une refonte en profondeur de Parcoursup", l'abrogation du dispositif, réclamé par le mouvement lycéen, est écartée par le gouvernement. Dès lors, quelles sont les pistes de l'exécutif pour calmer la colère des jeunes ? Franceinfo fait le point.

En classe de terminale à Nantes, Klara se retrouve confrontée à la difficulté de choisir une orientation à la fin du lycée. "A 17 ans, on se cherche encore. C'est trop tôt pour nous demander de choisir un métier", confie-t-elle à franceinfo. Elle n'est pas la seule à se sentir perdue parmi plus de 25 000 formations supérieures proposées.

Pour les aider à faire leur choix, les lycéens sont censés bénéficier de 54 heures d'orientation chaque année, de la seconde à la terminale. Cependant, un rapport parlementaire de février 2026 explique que ce volume horaire annuel n'est en réalité préconisé qu'"à titre indicatif" en fonction des "besoins des élèves" ou de l'accompagnement déjà proposé dans les établissements. Ainsi, "ces heures ne sont donc pas systématiquement inscrites au service des enseignants".

Laetitia Aresu, secrétaire générale de la CFDT Education, confirme ce constat : "Les professeurs principaux ont une dizaine d'heures de vie de classe à faire, mais (...) cela relève du bénévolat", déplore-t-elle. Pour répondre à cette inquiétude partagée par de nombreux lycéens, le président de la concertation sur Parcoursup, Gilles Roussel, explique qu'"il est question d'introduire plus d'orientation dès la seconde".

Dans les cortèges et les blocus, les lycéens dénoncent un système de sélection profondément inégalitaire. Sur Parcoursup, les établissements des candidats sont précisés : appartenir à un lycée réputé pour ses bons résultats scolaires peut ainsi être valorisé lors des sélections.

En février 2026, un rapport parlementaire a recommandé de supprimer la mention du lycée d'origine dans les éléments du dossier communiqué aux formations, alertant sur un "risque de biais discriminatoire dans l'étude des dossiers". Selon le ministre de l'Enseignement supérieur, Philippe Baptiste, cette possibilité "doit être mise sur la table", mais "comporte plus de complexités que ce que certains veulent parfois faire croire".

Les jeunes manifestants dénoncent aussi la sélection par le contrôle continu, source d'inégalités et de stress pour les lycéens. Les épreuves du bac, dont la notation est similaire sur tout le territoire, n'entrent pas en compte dans le dossier Parcoursup, les vœux étant formulés à partir de janvier pour une fin du processus au printemps. Gilles Roussel, également président du comité éthique et scientifique de Parcoursup, recommande d'avoir des notes qui servent de référence, comme une banque d'épreuves ou des QCM normalisés.

La concertation lancée au ministère de l'Enseignement supérieur et de la Recherche étudie aussi la façon de rendre plus transparents "les critères de classement des candidatures". A l'heure actuelle, pour sélectionner les candidats, les commissions de sélection des établissements supérieurs utilisent des outils automatisés, appelés "algorithmes locaux". Selon le rapport parlementaire de février 2026, leur "fonctionnement au regard des critères de sélection n'est pas assez transparent et nourrit de façon plus au moins légitime des sentiments d'injustice." Les auteurs du rapport recommandent ainsi de publier les algorithmes propres à chaque formation ou d'indiquer comment sont hiérarchisés les critères de sélection.

Le 29 septembre, devant un lycée bloqué à Créteil, Rabah Safsaf, professeur et représentant CGT, fulminait auprès de franceinfo : "Parcoursup crée un désordre sans nom.". Il déplorait la difficulté pour ses élèves d'être acceptés dans la formation de leur choix. "C'est le reflet du manque de places dans l'enseignement supérieur", estimait-il. Un constat partagé par les principales organisations étudiantes, dont certaines réclament la création de 250 000 places.

Reconnaissant "des tensions réelles, en particulier en Ile-de-France [et] pour les élèves issus des baccalauréats professionnels et technologiques", le ministre Philippe Baptiste a ouvert la voie à la création de places. Reste la question du coût. Edouard Geffray a précisé que les décisions se traduiront par "des amendements du gouvernement au projet de loi de finance 2027".

Vous pouvez désormais privilégier l'affichage des articles de franceinfo dans les résultats de recherche Google. Comment ça marche ?

Gilets jaunes, syndicats, lycéens : la mobilisation sociale est-elle partie pour durer ?

La política se cuela en las reuniones de los banqueros centrales en pleno repunte inflacionista

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

El BCE y la Fed se reúnen esta semana, a una semana de las elecciones legislativas estadounidenses y con el BCE pendiente de la crisis francesa

Inflación, primas de riesgo, petróleo, inteligencia artificial y una elevada dosis de política a ambos lados del Atlántico. El menú de las reuniones que afrontan esta semana los banqueros centrales de la zona euro y Estados Unidos apunta a una indigestión por sobredosis informativa. Pese a ello, no se esperan movimientos en el precio del dinero, que los analistas sí esperan en diciembre. La atención de inversores y economistas está puesta no tanto en los números como en las palabras, en un entorno donde la política monetaria cruza su camino con la política a secas.

Con la prima de riesgo francesa en los niveles más altos desde 2011, crecientes dudas sobre la sostenibilidad de sus finanzas, las calles del país sujetas a un toque de queda tras los mayores disturbios en décadas, elecciones presidenciales en 2027 y una exministra francesa en la silla presidencial del BCE (no se sabe por cuánto tiempo), la reunión del Banco Central Europeo está teñida de los colores del azul, blanco y rojo. No se esperan medidas concretas de apoyo a Francia, y el banco recordará, como siempre hace, la necesidad de equilibrar las cuentas públicas. Pero el lenguaje de Lagarde a la hora de abordar el impacto en la estabilidad financiera de esta crisis será examinado al detalle por los operadores del mercado de deuda. “Aún es demasiado pronto para adoptar medidas, pero no nos sorprendería que ya se produjera alguna forma moderada de intervención verbal”, anticipan los analistas de Bank of America. Lagarde podría recordar las herramientas a su disposición o sugerir la suspensión del proceso de reducción de su cartera de deuda soberana, opción que también contempla el analista de la gestora francesa Natixis Mabrouk Chetouane.

En cuanto al alza de la inflación, Ángel Gavilán, exdirector general de Economía del Banco de España y socio de aimTFP Inteligencia Económica, explica que los precios energéticos están más elevados de lo que esperaba el banco en su escenario central, pero ni él ni los mercados de futuros anticipan alzas de tipos. “La desviación es razonable; se puede esperar más. En diciembre sí esperamos una subida y, si no cambia el mapa, otras dos en 2027. El banco no parece incómodo con esta curva”.

Desde ING ligan directamente la perspectiva de tipos con la situación francesa. “El repunte de los rendimientos de los bonos alemanes desde septiembre ya ha tenido el mismo impacto sobre crecimiento e inflación que una subida de tipos de 25 puntos básicos”, explican. “En el caso de Francia, se acerca más a una subida de 50 puntos básicos. El mercado de bonos ha asumido la función del BCE”. La sucesión de Lagarde al frente del BCE también planeará sobre la reunión, con la incertidumbre sobre si la presidenta dará, como publicaba la agencia Reuters este mes, un paso atrás a principios de 2027. Y con la duda de si las capitales europeas pactarán en las próximas semanas un relevo al que aspira el español Pablo Hernández de Cos, exgobernador del Banco de España.

Al otro lado del charco las aguas no están más tranquilas. Estados Unidos celebra elecciones de medio mandato en apenas 10 días y las campañas políticas salpican de lleno la próxima reunión de la Reserva Federal, que se celebrará este martes y el miércoles. El presidente estadounidense, Donald Trump, se ha embarcado en una frenética carrera en la que prevé recorrer 30 estados antes del próximo 3 de noviembre, cuando se celebran las elecciones para renovar el poder legislativo. El mandatario republicano está echando toda la carne en el asador para tratar de revertir la desventaja que le dan las encuestas. La crisis de la asequibilidad, los aranceles, la guerra contra Irán y, sobre todo, la inflación, son los principales temas de la campaña.

La inflación que se resiste a desaparecer. Que, como el cuento de Monterroso: “Cuando despertó, seguía ahí”. Los ciudadanos sufren con la subida de los precios desde hace cinco años. En septiembre, repuntaron hasta el 3,7% en una nueva evidencia de que el bloqueo del estrecho de Ormuz provocado por la guerra de Estados Unidos contra Irán está perjudicando la economía.

Kevin Warsh, el presidente de la Fed, parece que quiere tomarse las cosas con calma y aplazar nuevas subidas de tipos hasta diciembre. Fue elegido por Trump para abaratar los costes de financiación, pero en septiembre subió los tipos por primera vez en tres años. La decisión hizo torcer el gesto a Trump.

“Históricamente, la Reserva Federal se ha caracterizado por abstenerse de modificar su política monetaria en vísperas de elecciones. Sin embargo, como demuestra la subida de septiembre, esta reputación es una tendencia, no una regla”, según Mark Shore, economista jefe de CMEgroup. “La Fed ya subió las tasas en septiembre y ahora todas las miradas están puestas en el próximo miércoles, seis días antes de las elecciones de mitad de mandato. Históricamente, la Reserva Federal suele abstenerse de subir las tasas en octubre. Pero Warsh ya rompió con esa tendencia en septiembre. ¿Romperá también con la de octubre?“, se pregunta Shore.

Los analistas encuestados por FedWatch creen que existe solo un 10% de posibilidades de que la Fed suba tipos el miércoles. Warsh se mostró cauto en el discurso que pronunció hace 10 días en el marco de la reunión de otoño del FMI y el Banco Mundial, celebrada en Bangkok. Aunque el hermetismo de Warsh ya no es noticia.

“A pesar de los altos resultados de inflación en septiembre y de que la Fed ha señalado que probablemente serán necesarias más acciones para contener la volatilidad, los mercados y los economistas están unidos en la expectativa de que el banco central mantenga los tipos de interés en suspenso en la reunión de octubre”, explica James Knightley, analista jefe para Estados Unidos de ING Research.

Por su parte, Michael Pearce, economista jefe de EE UU para Oxford Economics, se muestra algo más prudente. “La Reserva Federal liderada por Kevin Warsh ha dado más sorpresas, así que estimamos que la probabilidad de un aumento es del 30%, algo superior a lo que pronostican los mercados”, escribió Michael Pearce, economista jefe de Estados Unidos en Oxford Economics.

Warsh tendrá que seguir dando pistas para que los mercados empiecen a comprender su enfoque sobre la estrategia de la Reserva Federal con la inflación. Los precios globales del petróleo siguen elevados y volátiles, la inflación se acelera debido a la IA y a la transmisión de precios más altos de la energía, y el mercado laboral sigue pareciendo resiliente, pero la postura Warsh sigue siendo un misterio. Lo que no es un secreto es que el precio del petróleo se ha vuelto a encaramar esta semana por encima de los 120 dólares el barril, una tendencia que añade más presión a la Fed.

Los analistas estarán pendientes de las palabras del presidente de la Fed, un prestigioso economista y millonario (no es incompatible si está casado con la heredera del imperio Estée Lauder), en busca de indicios de su estrategia para contener los precios después de más de cinco años en que la inflación está por encima del objetivo del 2%.

Tassie, Vic gold output clears Kaiser Reef debt as cash hits $40M

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You have reached your maximum number of saved items.

Remove items from your saved list to add more.

You've used all of your gift articles. 3 more will become available on the 1st of every month.

Dual-asset gold producer Kaiser Reef has wiped a key debt from its books after a solid September quarter, a move expected to unchain about $600,000 a month in operating cash flow.

The company banked 6263 ounces of gold during the period, boosting its cash and bullion balance to a healthy $40 million and allowing it to fully repay the senior secured gold loan used to acquire its flagship Henty gold mine in Tasmania.

Production from the company’s two Australian assets continued to build momentum during the period, with total gold output rising to 6263 ounces from 6000 ounces in the prior quarter. The cornerstone Henty mine churned out 5692 ounces of gold and a further 5259 ounces of silver, while its Maldon project in Victoria chipped in another 572 ounces of gold. Impressively, the Maldon operation has now lifted its output for three consecutive quarters, jumping 65 per cent since March.

Total production generated an underlying cash flow of $5.6 million for the quarter, which more than covered $2.8 million in debt and deferred payments, allowing Kaiser to increase its net cash and bullion position by a hefty $2.8 million.

The strong financial position enabled the company to make a final delivery of 104 ounces of gold in October, wiping the slate clean on the 1872-ounce Auramet gold loan. With that facility retired, Kaiser’s only remaining gold delivery obligation is a deferred payment to Catalyst Metals as part of the Henty acquisition, requiring 50 ounces per month through to October 2030. However, the company says repaying the principal acquisition loan from the mine’s own cash flow is an important milestone.

Kaiser Reef managing director Brad Valiukas said: “Kaiser delivered 6263oz of gold across Henty and Maldon during the quarter, while increasing cash and bullion to A$40 million, paying down debt and advancing Maldon. We have completely repaid the Auramet gold loan, on schedule, with the final delivery made in October. The gold loan helped us acquire Henty, and repaying it from Henty’s own cash flow is an important milestone for Kaiser.”

The impressive quarterly report further underscores a remarkable full-year turnaround for Kaiser, which swung from a $20.24 million statutory loss in FY2025 to a $16.8 million after-tax profit in FY2026, backed by an impressive $44.7 million cash inflow from operations.

The Henty mine, nestled in Tasmania’s richly endowed Mount Read Volcanics belt alongside the legendary Rosebery and Mount Lyell operations, remains the company’s gold-producing workhorse. A recent resource and reserve update effectively offset mining depletion, maintaining the mine’s ore reserve at 198,000 ounces of gold and its overall resource at 429,000 ounces. In a shrewd move to sweat its assets, the company is also working with fellow ASX-lister Flynn Gold to process its ore through Henty’s 300,000-tonne-per-annum processing plant. As part of the expansion plan, Kaiser has applied to increase its tailings capacity by two million tonnes.

While Henty provides the cash flow, Kaiser’s Maldon operation in Victoria’s fabled Golden Triangle offers a significant high-grade growth opportunity. The project, with a strong track record of producing 1.75 million ounces at a staggering 28 grams per tonne (g/t) gold, is anchored by the fully permitted 200,000-tonne-per-annum Porcupine Flats processing facility. Its Union Hill deposit hosts an inferred resource of 187,000 ounces grading a solid 4.44g/t gold, with site stockpiles adding a further 11,700 ounces.

All up, the company’s global resource stands at 5.53 million tonnes grading 3.46g/t gold for 615,000 ounces. With FY27 total production targeted at around 32,000 ounces and no hedging in place, the gold producer appears well positioned to capture more upside should the bullion price remain steady.

Another major milestone for the quarter was the start of underground development at the Union Hill mine. The new development will provide access for underground diamond drilling to test the northern extensions of existing workings at Maldon’s Eaglehawk, Linscott’s, Ladies and Sailors Reefs targets.

The underground push adds another dimension to a busy surface exploration campaign aimed at building a pipeline of fresh drill targets. Recent infill soil sampling has outlined strong, continuous gold and arsenic anomalies across multiple structures, with some promising trends extending over 1.5 kilometres of strike. This work has been bolstered by a helicopter-borne magnetic and radiometric survey, providing a clearer picture of the structural architecture, with a surface diamond drilling campaign also ongoing.

With the Henty operation humming along and paying the bills, Kaiser appears to be in an enviable position of being largely debt-free and sitting on a sizeable cash pile as it methodically advances exploration across one of Victoria’s most celebrated high-grade goldfields. With no hedging in place and a production target of 32,000 ounces for FY27, the company looks to be fully exposed to any upside in the gold price. As drill rods turn on both surface and underground hotspots at Maldon, punters will likely be watching closely for the results to start flowing.

Is your ASX-listed company doing something interesting? Contact: mattbirney@bullsnbears.com.au

Venezuelan opposition leader María Machado returns after months in exile

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Venezuelan opposition leader MarÃ­a Corina Machado has returned home to a fraught political landscape after more than 10 months in exile.

"I am here. With you," Machado wrote on X on Sunday. Her return comes after repeated prior efforts to re-enter the country came to no avail.

In January, a month after Machado left Venezuela on a speedboat under cover of darkness to accept the Nobel Peace Prize in Norway, the US seized her opponent, President NicolÃ¡s Maduro.

The Trump administration then backed his deputy, Delcy RodrÃ­guez, as leader and she was sworn in as interim president, later signing an oil deal which granted very favourable terms to the US.

Machado was photographed on Sunday attending Mass at a church in the Venezuelan state of Lara, surrounded by supporters hugging her and clasping her hands.

She then began travelling to the capital Caracas in a caravan of cheering supporters. More supporters have lined the route of her journey to the capital.

She was photographed smiling as she leaned out of the sunroof of a car as she entered Caracas late on Sunday night.

"Night of reunion in our Caracas. For the reunion of each family, for the Freedom of each political prisoner, for the Freedom of Venezuela," she wrote on X.

Venezuelan opposition leader Maria Corina Machado greets supporters as she arrives in Caracas

Her ally, former presidential candidate Edmundo GonzÃ¡lez, confirmed her arrival, writing in a statement: "I am thrilled to announce to you that our leader, my dear MarÃ­a Corina, is on Venezuelan soil."

"Today is a very important day for Venezuelans. Our sovereign voice returns with her to lead and guide this process!" GonzÃ¡lez said.

He added that he will soon join Machado in Venezuela to advance the country "toward freedom".

An advisor to Machado said in September that over the previous few weeks Machado had attempted seven times to return to Venezuela, but was denied each time. There have been reports that the US was involved in delaying her return, which it has denied.

It's unclear what Machado plans to do next now that she's back, but she has previously said she would run for president again if an election were held. Her ability to return to her country was always contingent on approval from the Trump administration, which it appears has now been granted.

A staunch supporter of democracy, Machado won an independent presidential primary election - held outside the Maduro government's official election authority - in 2023, earning more than 90% of the vote.

Despite winning the primary, Maduro's government barred Machado from running for office, accusing her of fraud and tax violations. Machado denied the allegations, calling them politically motivated. She spent her final days in the country hiding in safehouses before her fleeing to Norway to accept the Nobel Prize.

In the 2024 election, Machado's ally GonzÃ¡lez ran in her place against Maduro.

The electoral authority, which is closely aligned with the government, declared Maduro the winner without making detailed voting tallies public.

The opposition argued that GonzÃ¡lez won the election, not Maduro, uploading copies of the voting tallies as evidence.

Maduro was removed from the country by US forces in January, and is currently jailed in New York where he faces drugs, weapons and torture charges. He has pleaded not guilty.

A spokesman for Machado called her return "a major step toward a democratic transition and free, fair, and verifiable presidential elections in the near future".

"We thank President Donald Trump, Secretary of State Marco Rubio, the US Government, Congress, and allied governments for their continued leadership and support," her spokesperson, David Smolansky, added.

Speaking to reporters on Sunday, Trump said, "I don't know anything about" her return, and added that he hoped she was "safe".

"I think she's a very nice woman. She gave me her Nobel Prize, so how can I say anything else? And I hope she's safe," he said.

In January, Machado visited the White House and gifted her Nobel prize award to Trump. The committee that issues the prize had said "it cannot be revoked, shared, or transferred to others".

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio praised his country's role in securing her return through political reconciliation talks.

"The conditions allowing for the safe return of Ms Machado to Venezuela is the direct result of the US supported reconciliation talks which has already seen hundreds of political prisoners freed and hundreds of political opposition figures return from exile," he posted on X.

He added: "We expect all political actors to support these talks which represent the best chance Venezuela has had in thirty years to peacefully achieve not just a free and fair election but an enduring democracy."

Oil slides over 2% as traders weigh Saudi attack risks, demand destruction

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

U.S. crude oil fell over 2% on Monday as investors weighed the threat of further attacks on Saudi Arabian energy infrastructure against concerns that elevated prices could eventually erode demand.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures slid 2.19% to $101 a barrel, while Brent crude futures declined 1.89% to $114.51 a barrel.

The pullback followed a sharp rally on Friday after attacks by Yemen's Houthi militants on Saudi Arabia reignited fears of disruptions to global energy supplies.

Tensions remained elevated over the weekend as the Houthis said they had targeted Saudi Arabia's main airport in Riyadh and oil facilities on Saturday. Saudi authorities said Sunday that they had intercepted additional missiles and drones.

The kingdom partially resumed operations at its main airport on Sunday despite damage from earlier attacks, even as the Houthis threatened further strikes.

The continued hostilities have heightened concerns about the vulnerability of Saudi Arabia's energy infrastructure, with traders increasingly focused on the risk of a major disruption to production, refining or export facilities.

Speaking to CNBC, Michael Alfaro, chief investment officer at Gallo Partners, estimated that geopolitical uncertainty had already added roughly $20 a barrel to oil prices.

A substantial strike on Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline or additional refining infrastructure could send prices sharply higher, he added, but further gains could eventually undermine consumption.

"What we would likely see if oil pushed above 120, 130, 140 dollars a barrel for Brent, that is, you're going to see more demand destruction," Alfaro said.

Beyond the immediate threat to crude supplies, damage to refining infrastructure could pose a more persistent challenge to global energy markets.

"The real challenge is refining capacity," he said, warning that the disruptions could develop into a longer-term structural problem extending into 2027 and beyond.

Meanwhile, Saudi Arabia plans to begin storing crude oil in Singapore from the second half of 2027, according to energy ministers from both countries, in a move aimed at strengthening the resilience of its Asian supply chain.

In the U.S., the Energy Department on Friday authorized the release of up to 400,000 barrels from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve to address a supply disruption at a Louisiana refinery.

Still, the prospect of additional supply from American producers remains uncertain, with companies reluctant to commit to major new investments amid volatile prices and geopolitical risks.

Alfaro added that U.S. producers were adding some drilling rigs, but remained cautious about substantially increasing capital expenditure.

"We're not really seeing major capex additions to bring new rigs on or bring new production online," he said.

Israel’s election: Five key things to know

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

High-stakes vote comes as Israel is mbroiled in multiple years-long conflicts, faces growing international criticism and grapples with deepening domestic polarisation.

Israelis will head to the polls on Tuesday for legislative elections.

The vote, the first since the Hamas-led attacks on Israel on October 7, 2023 and the subsequent genocidal war against Palestinians in the Gaza Strip, is being called one of the most important in its history.

It will determine the direction of Israel’s government as the country remains embroiled in multiple years-long conflicts, faces growing international criticism and grapples with deepening polarisation at home.

Here are five things to know about the upcoming election:

Any party clearing an electoral threshold of 3.25 percent enters parliament, whose term lasts four years.

There are two main blocs vying to lead the next government.

The first is the pro-Netanyahu coalition, led by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party and joined by several far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties. These include United Torah Judaism (UTJ) and Shas – both conservative religious parties – and Otzma Yehudi and Religious Zionism-Zehut, led respectively by far-right ministers Itamar Ben-Gvir and Bezalel Smotrich.

The main opposition coalition comprises four Jewish-led opposition movements – the Yashar party, led by former Israeli military chief Gadi Eisenkot, the Democrats, led by former deputy military chief of staff Yair Golan, the Yisrael Beiteinu party, led by former Finance Minister Avigdor Lieberman and the Together alliance, led by former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett and joined by Knesset opposition leader Yair Lapid.

The latest opinion polls published before a ban on new surveys took effect on Friday showed that neither bloc was on course to secure a parliamentary majority of 61 seats.

A poll by public broadcaster KAN projected that the opposition alliance would win 52 of the Knesset’s 120 seats, compared with 50 for Netanyahu’s bloc, with the remaining seats going to unaffiliated parties, including the country’s two main Arab-majority political lists – Ra’am, also called the United Arab List, and the Joint List, an alliance of Hadash, Balad and Ta’al parties.

However, both the pro-Netanyahu and anti-Netanyahu blocs have effectively ruled out joining forces with the Arab-led parties for now.

The fractured landscape means Israeli parties could be in for tough post-election negotiations to cobble together a governing majority. If those efforts fail, Israel could end up with a minority government reliant on support from parties outside the governing coalition – or potentially head to another round of elections.

Israelis are grappling with a series of domestic and international crises this election cycle.

On the domestic front, there is growing demand for accountability for reported security failures, including those leading up to the October 2023 attacks, anger over the growing ultra-Orthodox community’s military exemption and concern over Netanyahu’s repeated efforts to push judicial reforms that critics say undermine Israel’s liberal democratic order.

“The secular and liberal opposition factions in Israel feel this election is the last chance to save Israeli democracy and restore the institutions that have eroded over Netanyahu’s successive terms,” Mairav Zonszein, an Israeli-American commentator who is a senior analyst at the International Crisis Group, told Al Jazeera.

A war-weary public is also weighing how Israel should proceed with its multiple military conflicts, with many voters seeking security and economic stability while remaining resistant to perceived concessions in Gaza, the occupied West Bank and Lebanon.

It is Israel’s first election since the October 2023 attacks and comes amid a surge in far-right attitudes and growing divisions over the direction of the country.

“Strains of Israeli society had always been given to violence and right-wing rhetoric, but the trends that had always been present had accelerated under the current government,” prominent Israeli sociologist Yehouda Shenhav-Shahrabani told Al Jazeera.

The election also comes as Israel’s international standing has deteriorated, with a host of Israel’s allies sanctioning its illegal settlements in the occupied West Bank, and many other nations denouncing the country’s continued attacks in Gaza and Lebanon.

When Netanyahu addressed the United Nations General Assembly on September 24, dozens of envoys from around the world walked out, some jeering and booing.

The outcome of the upcoming vote could determine whether Israel’s next government maintains its approach to the international community or seeks to repair ties.

Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving prime minister who has led the country for more than 15 of the last 17 years, and a total of 19, faces growing scrutiny at home and abroad over alleged security failures leading up to the October 7 attack, as well as corruption charges he has been battling since 2019.

An electoral defeat would also leave Netanyahu more exposed to his ongoing corruption trial, which could ultimately result in a prison sentence if he is convicted.

A new government could also affect the prospects of a potential commission of inquiry into the October 2023 attacks and Netanyahu’s political standing as he faces an arrest warrant for war crimes issued by the International Criminal Court.

About 7.3 million Israeli citizens are eligible to vote in Tuesday’s election, including Palestinian citizens of Israel, who number about 1.2 million.

Meanwhile, roughly three million adult Palestinians in Gaza, the occupied West Bank and occupied East Jerusalem have no say in the government that controls their borders, movement and legal status.

Recent polling suggested that the turnout among the Palestinian citizens of Israel could reach 60 percent, with the majority of the votes expected to go the Arab lists, which are the principal representatives of Palestinian citizens of Israel. If turnout reaches 65 percent, these parties could potentially win as many as 15 seats in the Knesset. Campaigners are aiming for 70 percent turnout, which would be the highest in decades and raise the threshold, keeping smaller parties out of parliament.

Last month, Israel’s Central Elections Committee voted to bar the Joint List and Ra’am from the election.

Israel’s top court quickly reversed that decision, enabling the Arab parties to stay in the race. However, the court did force Balad leader Sami Abu Shehadeh to withdraw from the race over an article in which he described the October 7 attacks as “an important historical event”.

The role of Arab parties is going to be significant. Both major coalitions have ruled out joining forces with them for now, but without them, neither coalition might be able to form a majority.

Netanyahu has repeatedly sought to portray his opponents as potential partners of the Arab parties, claiming that an opposition-led government will seek their support and that only his Likud party can prevent this.

Joseph Mansour, a former aide to Israeli Knesset member Ayman Odeh, said politically alienating Arab parties is part of Netanyahu’s political strategy, which could allow him to remain in office as a caretaker prime minister if neither coalition forms a majority.

“If Palestinian-led party support becomes politically toxic and the opposition falls short of 61 seats, the deadlock itself becomes the latest win for Netanyahu,” wrote Mansour for the Arab Center Washington DC.

Labour urged to plug predicted £400m shortfall in overseas aid funding in 2027

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Official forecast indicates shortfall below 0.3% of national income in promised official development assistance

The UK is on course to miss its target of spending 0.3% of national income on international aid by an estimated £400m by the end of next year.

Calculations from the Center for Global Development (CGD) found the UK was on course for a large shortfall in promised official development assistance at the end of 2027.

The researchers said the central forecast from the Treasury’s comparison of independent experts implied UK aid would be £410m less than anticipated – at only 0.29% of national income.

However, they said it was “very plausible” that the shortfall would be £580m, at only 0.28% of national income. Even the most conservative forecast implies a £250m shortfall, according to the estimates.

Ian Mitchell, a senior fellow at the CGD and co-director of its Europe programme, described these as “material shortfalls in the spending power of UK aid”.

Making up a shortfall of even £400m would be enough to halve cuts to Africa that are likely to cost thousands of lives and mean the UK’s de facto exit from the south and east of the continent, he said.

The CGD attributed the shortfall to higher than expected inflation, which means national income will be higher than forecast at the spending review, and a 2025 policy change around the Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office (FCDO) budget.

The researchers called on ministers to consider allocating additional funds to international aid in this week’s budget, to avoid the embarrassment and criticism from missing a target.

The 0.3% spending target for 2027 is far below the 0.7% promised in Labour’s election manifesto, having been downgraded by Keir Starmer to boost spending on defence.

Fleur Anderson, a former minister who sits on the foreign affairs select committee, said she would like to see higher spending on development aid in the budget.

She said: “I’d like to see this government going further and reversing these cuts altogether in time, but at the very least we should be hitting the reduced target that was set.

“Falling short isn’t an abstract accounting issue. It means communities not having clean water, girls pulled out of school, vaccines that don’t get delivered, peace programmes in war zones cut.

“The upcoming budget presents real opportunity for the Treasury to address this and for the government to show that its words on Britain’s place in the world are matched by action.”

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

Charities have repeatedly said that the government’s deep aid cuts will hit children’s education and increase the risk of disease and death in some African countries.

The latest figures released by the FCDO in September showed that British aid cuts under Starmer’s government hit the world’s poorest countries the hardest, while higher-income countries’ share of aid increased. The data showed a 6.7% decrease in funding to the least-developed and low-income countries, amounting to cuts of £118m.

The UK’s overall official development assistance (ODA) budget in 2025 decreased to 0.44% of gross national income, compared with 0.5% in 2024, a drop of £987m.

The government has prioritised spending through multilateral agencies such as the World Bank’s development arm, the International Development Association, and the global vaccines initiative Gavi. It has also continued to fund humanitarian support.

The Conservative party and Reform UK have pledged to slash aid even further, from about £9bn a year to £1bn – a level that would leave the UK unable to cover existing contributions to the UN and World Bank.

An FCDO spokesperson said: “The publication of the FCDO multi-year ODA allocations in March 2026 set out our spending plans and priorities for the next three financial years based on GNI [gross national income] at the time of the 2025 spending review.

“Moving to a fixed budget reduces volatility and provides the necessary stability to deliver high-impact aid programmes.”

Bayeux tapestry becomes pop culture hit as young people more keen to see exhibition than top films

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Monzo says 55% of cardholders’ British Museum payments in first hour of latest ticket release were by people under 35

A 1,000-year-old embroidery may be an unusual candidate for a pop culture moment, but young people are seemingly more willing to shell out for tickets for the Bayeux tapestry than spend money on Hollywood blockbusters.

In the first hour that the second round of tickets to view the tapestry were available last Wednesday, payments from Monzo cardholders to the British Museum reached £14,100 and were 48 times that of a normal Wednesday morning. More than half (55%) of those buyers were aged under 35.

This was double the opening mornings for this year’s blockbuster films: the critically acclaimed The Odyssey, at £7,000, and the box office smash hit Spider-Man: Brand New Day, at £6,300, according to the bank’s spending data for its 16 million customers.

Tickets for the Bayeux Tapestry, which is on display in England for the first time since it was taken to France in the 11th century, are significantly more expensive than the average cinema ticket, at £33 for standard adult peak tickets and £27 for off-peak. However, adjusting for the more expensive entrance fee – as well as the limited availability of tickets compared with a blockbuster movie – highlights the huge appeal of the tapestry.

Get our weekly pop culture email, free in your inbox every Friday

As a result, buyers spent five times more on average at the checkout than on Spider-Man’s opening morning, at £97 versus £19. By the time the Bayeux tickets had been on sale for two hours, Monzo customers had spent £30,500. Enthusiasm was such that many buyers were willing to travel from across the country for the once-in-a-lifetime event, with almost half of the buyers based outside London, and the furthest purchases coming from Glasgow.

Thousands joined an online queue to secure tickets when they went on sale at 10am on Wednesday, with spots selling out within three hours – beating the first wave, which sold out in 24 hours.

The frenzy has drawn comparisons with big pop cultural events such as Glastonbury festival and the Oasis reunion tour. The British Museum said the response to the tapestry going on display last month had been “extraordinary”, leading it to make changes to address lengthy queues for ticket holders, including banning visitors from taking photos.

The exhibition has received rave reviews for its meticulous display of the woven record of the Norman Conquest, which is treasured for its impressive technical prowess, skilful storytelling and historical value. The Guardian described it as “a stupefying, thrilling, horrifying masterpiece that shakes the earth”.

Indonesia strengthens measures against stock manipulation after market rout triggered by MSCI

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto told his Cabinet last week that MSCI was trying to "intimidate" Indonesia, but said the country would not be afraid.

Workers walk past an electronic board displaying stock movement at the Indonesia Stock Exchange (IDX) in Jakarta, Indonesia on Oct 16, 2026. (Photo: Reuters/Ajeng Dinar Ulfiana)

JAKARTA: Indonesia on Monday (Oct 26) introduced more measures to address stock market manipulation, including adding independent parties to a committee determining stocks with concentrated ownership structures, after MSCI said the country could be demoted due to concerns about opaque trading.

The measures came after MSCI announced earlier this month that it could demote Indonesia's stock exchange to "standalone" market status, saying not enough had been done to arrest coordinated trading practices in its stock market.

That was more severe than the global index provider's January warning that it could downgrade the country to "frontier" status. At present, MSCI still classifies Indonesia as an "emerging" market.

The Indonesia Stock Exchange (IDX) plans to expand the membership of a committee that comes up with a list of stocks with high shareholder concentration to include independent parties like Indonesian academics and F Chapman Taylor as a representative of foreign investors, chief Jeffrey Hendrik said at a press conference.

Taylor is a former partner with investment company the Capital Group and is currently an adviser to Indonesia's sovereign wealth fund, Danantara."We encourage the IDX to strengthen the mechanism and methodology of trading supervision, including its capacity to identify patterns of irregular transactions, detect coordinated trading and strengthen the mechanism to respond to such events," said Hasan Fawzi, the financial regulator's capital market supervisor.Last week, the IDX said it would exclude stocks with high shareholding concentrations from its main Jakarta index starting on Nov 2. The current list includes 80 companies.The MSCI downgrade warning spurred Indonesia's deepest-ever two-day stock selloff on Oct 16 and Oct 19, sending the main index to its lowest point since 2020. It has since rebounded.At 1.15pm Singapore time on Monday, the index was down around 1 per cent.

Previously, the committee members were officials from capital market authorities. Taylor is a former partner with investment company the Capital Group and is currently an adviser to Indonesia's sovereign wealth fund, Danantara.

"We encourage the IDX to strengthen the mechanism and methodology of trading supervision, including its capacity to identify patterns of irregular transactions, detect coordinated trading and strengthen the mechanism to respond to such events," said Hasan Fawzi, the financial regulator's capital market supervisor.

Last week, the IDX said it would exclude stocks with high shareholding concentrations from its main Jakarta index starting on Nov 2. The current list includes 80 companies.

The MSCI downgrade warning spurred Indonesia's deepest-ever two-day stock selloff on Oct 16 and Oct 19, sending the main index to its lowest point since 2020. It has since rebounded.

At 1.15pm Singapore time on Monday, the index was down around 1 per cent.

The exchange also plans to launch investigations when it detects possible coordinated trading to manipulate share prices and freeze suspicious investor accounts, Hendrik said.

The new measure, to take effect in November, is more proactive than the IDX's previous response. The IDX previously warned public investors of unusual market activities when a stock price moved sharply and requested an explanation from the company. The process could also lead to a temporary freeze in share trading.

A policy that allows share buybacks without requiring approval from a general shareholders' meeting has also been extended until June 2027, Hasan said, adding that any buyback must consider rules about the minimum free float, or shares available to trade in the market.

Hasan said Indonesia has been communicating with MSCI and other global index providers like FTSE Russell to reiterate its regulators' commitment to market reforms.

The market showed resilience following MSCI's recent announcement, Hasan said, describing local investors as "mature" and "wise" for buying stocks in companies with good fundamentals.

President Prabowo Subianto told his Cabinet last week that MSCI was trying to "intimidate" Indonesia, but said the country would not be afraid, striking a combative tone in his first public reaction to the downgrade warning.

Since MSCI's January warning, Indonesia has launched a series of reforms including releasing more details on investor accounts and ownership data, increasing the minimum free-float level and unveiling record fines against parties involved in market manipulation.
