Obama unloads on Trump over the Iran war and mocks the president’s diplomatic strategy

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Obama bashed Trump for causing fuel prices to surge and what he saw as the unnecessary deaths of service members in a war that has raged on for nearly eight months

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Former Democratic President Barack Obama has unloaded on Donald Trump for the Iran war, mocking the Republican president’s diplomatic strategy.

“These are not serious people. They’re not serious people. And the problem is you end up having to pay the price for their lack of seriousness,” Obama said Saturday while stumping for Democrats in Ohio.

The U.S., alongside Israel, started launching strikes against Iran in late February, with Trump claiming that Americans were in imminent danger over the Middle Eastern country’s nuclear ambitions.

But Obama argued that the issue had already been dealt with under his administration — until Trump unraveled it.

“Let’s think about this Iran war. The original rationale was, we’re going to go in there and get them to give up their nuclear program, their nuclear weapons,” Obama said. “Now, the thing was, Iran didn’t have nuclear weapons, because I put a deal in place that everybody acknowledged — kept them from getting nukes. And I didn’t have to fire a missile to do it; I just had to engage in diplomacy.”

Obama was referring to the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action, commonly called the Iran Nuclear Deal, which the Middle Eastern country signed in 2015 and Trump withdrew from during his first term.

The deal restricted Iran’s nuclear program to ensure it was only using those materials for peaceful purposes in exchange for sanctions relief.

But Trump argued when withdrawing from the deal in 2018 that it “allowed Iran to continue enriching uranium and — over time — reach the brink of a nuclear breakout.”

Trump has insisted throughout the war that it would be a quick operation, but it has now dragged on for nearly eight months. Soon after the strikes began, Iran effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, blocking oil tankers from passing through the vital passageway and causing fuel prices to skyrocket.

A total of 21 American service members have been killed and 865 wounded in operations related to the war, according to the Pentagon.

“So seven months later, how’s that war going?” Obama asked.

“Our allies and our bases have been attacked; Americans have been killed; hundreds of Americans have been wounded; we have, we’ve run down our stock of missiles,” the former president said, though Trump has denied reports of munitions shortages. “And Iran now has veto power over the Strait of Hormuz, where oil goes through to the world market.

“This is the reason why the price of oil is up. Nobody disputes that. This is reason the price diesel is up. This is why the reason the just about everything is up.”

Trump is hosting daily rallies across the country to boost Republicans’ chances of keeping control of Congress in November’s midterm elections — all while dealing with his deeply unpopular war.

Forty-seven percent of Democrat voters and 56 percent of Republican voters think ending the war in Iran should be one of the highest priorities for the next Congress, according to a new AP/NORC Center for Public Affairs Research poll.

Obama was in Cleveland Saturday to support Amy Acton, who is running for Ohio governor against Trump ally Vivek Ramaswamy, and former Senator Sherrod Brown, who is running against Republican Senator Jon Husted.

Ramaswamy saw his betting odds plummet over a controversial ad using his opponent’s mother, whom Acton has accused of abuse, to call her a “liar” and urge Ohians not to vote for her.

Acton has an 83 percent chance of winning the race compared to Ramaswamy’s 18 percent on the prediction market Polymarket as of Saturday evening.

Ramaswamy was also trailing Acton by seven points in a traditional poll. Acton had 50 percent of support among likely Ohio voters compared to Ramaswamy’s 43 percent, according to a recent survey conducted by Marist Poll before the campaign ad was released.

In that same poll, Brown was up four points with 48 percent of voter support compared to Husted’s 44 percent.

Two men killed by falling trees as Storm Boelo leaves 90,000 properties without power

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Storm Boelo floods town centre of Largs, Scotland

Two men have been killed by falling trees on Sunday as Storm Boelo continues to bring severe weather to the UK.

A man in his 20s was pronounced dead in north London after the car he was driving was hit by a falling tree. It came hours after a 60-year-old man died in Dumfries and Galloway.

The harsh weather battering the UK had left more than 90,000 properties without power as of 19:00 GMT on Sunday, the Energy Networks Association (ENA) said.

Those still without electricity have faced delays as the dangerous conditions were "limiting network engineers' opportunities to safely start the work", the ENA added.

Roads across Scotland have been flooded, including part of the A82 at Dumbuck Quarry in Dumbarton

The Metropolitan Police were called to Dollis Road in Finchley at around 12:40 GMT to reports a tree had fallen onto a moving car, and the man was pronounced dead at the scene.

Chief Inspector Guy Ellwood, from the North West Command Unit, said: "This is a deeply tragic incident and our thoughts are with the loved ones of the young man who lost his life."

A London Ambulance Service spokesperson said it sent incident response officers, paramedics from its hazardous area response team, and a trauma team from London's Air Ambulance to the scene.

Earlier, Police Scotland said a man died after being hit by a falling tree at at Mid Kelton near Castle Douglas. Meanwhile, two women were also taken to hospital after being hit by a another falling tree in Aberfeldy.

During Storm Boelo and the earlier Storm Austen, which hit the UK on Friday, 838,700 properties across the country faced power cuts, according to the ENA.

Wind gusts peaked on Sunday morning at 85mph in Machrihanish, in Argyll and Bute, and 84mph in both Stornoway in the Outer Hebrides, and Capel Curig in north Wales.

An amber wind warning issued by the Met Office was in place until 21:00 on Sunday across Scotland and parts of northern England.

A yellow warning that also covered other areas of the north of England, including Liverpool, Manchester, Leeds, Sheffield and Newcastle-upon-Tyne, as well as parts of the East and West Midlands and North Wales, also expired at 21:00.

The Met Office urged people to "stay indoors as much as possible", adding: "If you do go out, try not to walk, or shelter, close to buildings and trees."

The strongest gusts have continued to spread eastwards across the UK on Sunday.

The sea wall at New Brighton promenade in Merseyside took a battering over the weekend

Four flood warnings have been issued for parts of England and Wales on Sunday, while there are 17 flood warnings in place in Scotland.

High rainfall totals have been recorded in the Scottish Highlands, peaking in Cluanie Inn which received 130mm.

Scottish government ministers and officials gathered on Sunday afternoon to discuss the impact of Storm Boelo.

In a statement, the Scottish government said while conditions may ease later on Sunday, there is potential for adverse weather to return in the coming days.

In Aberfeldy, Perth and Kinross, two women were taken to hospital after being struck by a fallen tree at around 12:50 GMT on Sunday, police said.

A person is covered in spray as waves crash in Largs, near North Ayrshire, Scotland

The towns of the Clyde coast in Scotland have been hit particularly hard by the severe weather. The main Skelmorlie to Largs road has been badly damaged and a number of businesses in Largs were flooded overnight on Saturday.

In Gourock, a boat ended up on the side of the road and there have been reports of power being out in Girvan all throughout Sunday.

In Dumbarton, on the north bank of the River Clyde, Muhammad Zubair Ashraf's mobile phone shop was flooded overnight.

He estimated between Â£5,000 and Â£10,000 of damage was caused and said he was "heartbroken" at the scale of the losses.

Travel has been affected across the country, with police in Scotland warning against unnecessary road journeys.

Ferry services have been impacted, some flights to and from Belfast, Glasgow, Aberdeen and Dublin airports have been cancelled, and train services across the UK have faced disruption.

Kushner Lawyer Demands VANITY FAIR Pull Book Excerpt Over 'Defamatory Allegations'...

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Chad Mizelle, an attorney representing Jared Kushner, demanded that Vanity Fair take down a recently published book excerpt alleging that President Donald Trump's son-in-law had access to secret foreign bank accounts during his first term.

"We formally put Vanity Fair and Condé Nast on notice regarding the false and defamatory allegations published about our client, Jared Kushner," Mizelle wrote in a statement on X Saturday morning. "Serious questions are now being raised about the credibility of this reporting, including by independent journalists."

He continued: "Despite repeated requests, the author has failed to produce a single bank statement, wire transfer record, account number or other verifiable evidence supporting these extraordinary accusations."

As Mizelle went on, he claimed that they "have seen no indication that Vanity Fair conducted any independent verification of these claims before publishing them."

"We call on Vanity Fair to clarify publicly whether it independently verified these allegations and, if so, what evidence supports them. Or did it simply republish the author's claims without conducting its own fact-checking?" he concluded. "We have demanded that Vanity Fair remove the article and preserve all records relating to its reporting, fact-checking, editorial review and publication."

Mizelle also attached the letter he sent to Vanity Fair over the article in-question, which claimed the outlet has exposed itself to "serious legal risk" by sharing the excerpt from investigative journalist Matthew Cole's forthcoming book, "Princes and Thieves."

In the excerpt, which was published Thursday by Vanity Fair, Cole alleged that Dutch intelligence officials gained access to information from Kushner's compromised phone and discovered evidence of secret financial arrangements involving leaders in the United Arab Emirates and Saudi Arabia. Investigators allegedly found that Kushner could access a UAE bank account that received hundreds of millions of dollars through Azerbaijan.

Cole further alleged that Saudi and Emirati officials arranged for at least $2 billion to be placed into accounts that Kushner could access, including one holding roughly $750 million where he was allegedly a signatory. The allegations have not been independently verified by TheWrap.

Kushner publicly denounced the allegations on X Thursday, writing, "[Vanity Fair] posted an excerpt from a book containing outrageous and categorically false allegations. The allegations made by this author are completely insane and never happened. We denied every one of these claims on the record, yet the author failed to provide a single shred of evidence to substantiate any of them."

"No bank statements, wire transfer records, account numbers, transaction dates, or other verifiable details were provided to support these extraordinary accusations," he continued. "During the period in question, I was one of the most scrutinized people in the world, subjected to intense media coverage, multiple congressional investigations, and a special counsel investigation. I voluntarily cooperated with these investigations, providing over 30 hours of testimony. None of these allegations ever surfaced, because none of these events ever happened."

Before concluding his statement, Kushner blasted Cole's claims as "outrageous fabrications being presented as investigative journalism."

"This is not journalism," he said. "It is a mixture of fantasy, fiction, and advocacy, written by someone who is either deeply disturbed or acting with sinister intent."

Mizelle followed suit with his own statement, adding on X, "Before publication, we sent Simon & Schuster a detailed 13-page letter responding to and categorically denying the extraordinary allegations made against my client, Jared Kushner, and challenging the total absence of supporting evidence."

"Vanity Fair has now published an excerpt amplifying these false and defamatory allegations," he continued on Thursday. "We have seen no credible evidence substantiating any of these claims, and we question what independent verification Vanity Fair conducted before publishing them. We intend to pursue legal action against those responsible and hold them accountable."

Representatives for Vanity Fair, Condé Nast and Simon & Schuster did not immediately respond to TheWrap's request for comment.

The post Jared Kushner's Lawyer Demands Vanity Fair Pull Book Excerpt Over 'False and Defamatory Allegations' appeared first on TheWrap.

TRUMP: DON'T 'GIVE A DAMN' ABOUT GAS PRICES...

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump has said he does not "give a damn" about potential criticism of his remarks boasting that the United States is making a "fortune" from high oil prices, as American motorists continue to face elevated gasoline costs.

Speaking at a rally in Georgia on Saturday, October 24, Trump said advisers had warned him against highlighting the financial gains associated with rising oil prices, suggesting the comments would invite criticism over the cost of fuel for ordinary Americans.

In footage of the rally shared online, Trump claimed the US oil and gas industry was making billions of dollars amid the conflict in the Middle East, presenting higher oil prices as a source of financial gains for the country.

President Donald Trump speaks during a midterm campaign rally at the Guardian Centers on October 24, 2026 in Perry, Georgia.

"Our country is making hundreds of billions of dollars because of what's happened in the Middle East, because they're coming to Texas and they're coming to Oklahoma and they're coming to Louisiana and they're taking that oil," Trump said.

"My people say, please don't mention that, Sir, people are paying a little higher," Trump said, adding that he expected gas prices to fall.

"I can just see the fake headlines," Trump said. "President Trump is talking about all the money they're making while people are suffering at the pump."

"But I said, 'I don't give a damn, I'm gonna say it: We're making a frickin fortune,'" he added.

Trump went on to claim that Americans were "paying lower than Biden and Obama and they don't have nuclear weapons," apparently referring to Iran's nuclear capabilities.

Under Obama, the U.S. national average gas price peaked at $3.93 per gallon in May 2011, and under Biden, it peaked at $5.06 in June 2022, according to govargus.com.

There has been widespread backlash to Trump's comments online. California Governor Gavin Newsom reposted footage of Trump's remarks at the rally commenting: "Congratulations to Big Oil on their very successful, record-profits presidency."

Newsweek contacted the White House via email Sunday for comment.

The national average price of regular gasoline stood at approximately $4.29 per gallon on October 25, according to the American Automobile Association (AAA).

That was down from approximately $4.49 a month earlier but significantly higher than the $3.06 average recorded a year ago. The latest figures represent an increase of around $1.23 per gallon over 12 months, equivalent to roughly 40 percent.

AAA's reported national averages for other fuel types were approximately $4.80 per gallon for midgrade gasoline, $5.20 for premium and $6.35 for diesel.

Prices vary considerably across the country, with motorists in some states paying substantially more than the national average. The figures also illustrate the distinction between the financial benefits Trump attributes to the energy sector and the costs facing households at the pump.

Although gas prices had eased from their September levels, the year-over-year increase meant drivers were still paying considerably more to fill their tanks than they had a year earlier.

Trump has repeatedly pointed to the potential financial benefits of higher oil prices for American energy producers and oil-producing states.

But higher crude oil prices can also put pressure on consumers by increasing the cost of gasoline, diesel and transportation. The extent to which domestic producers benefit depends on factors including production costs, market prices and the wider economic effects of more expensive energy.

There have been numerous protests across the country protesting the Iran war and its impact on gas prices.

Signs protesting the Iran war and its impact on gas prices are seen during a protest organized by VoteVets outside of the U.S. Capitol on May 20, 2026 in Washington, DC.

As previously reported by Newsweek, nearly three-quarters of likely voters disapprove of how President Trump has handled gas prices, with the midterm elections days away and his overall approval rating at the lowest point of his second term.

A New York Times/Siena poll found that 73 percent of likely voters disapprove of Trump's handling of gas prices, compared with 24 percent who approve. The poll surveyed 3,494 voters, October 12-19.

No issue in the poll scored worse for the president, and the frustration runs well past Democrats: 77 percent of independents disapprove, along with 42 percent of Republicans and 43 percent of people who voted for him in 2024.

Massachusetts Senator Elizabeth Warren posted on X on Friday: "I'd like to report a fraudster in the Oval Office. Trump promised to lower costs on Day 1. But instead he launched a war that increased the prices of everything from gas to groceries."

NFL House closes as riots disrupt game weekend...

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The NFL shut down its NFL House fan hub in Paris on Saturday as France braced for another day of mass protests, the latest disruption ahead of the league's first regular-season game in France. The Pittsburgh Steelers and New Orleans Saints kick off Sunday at 9:30 a.m. ET in Saint-Denis.

The league notified fans of the closure through its OnePass app Saturday morning, citing "potential accessibility issues," a day after it shut its city-center pop-up shop for the same reason.

MORE: NFL insists on playing Steelers-Saints Paris game despite ongoing riots

A general overall view of the Stade de France, the site of the inaugural NFL Paris Game between the New Orleans Saints and the Pittsburgh Steelers. | Kirby Lee-Imagn Images

The Saints also delayed their own fan gathering Saturday, announcing that "Saints House has been postponed until Sunday." The Steelers canceled their Steelers House event at a bar in the Bastille neighborhood on Thursday.

"Following recent events in the Bastille neighborhood, and after careful consideration, we felt it appropriate not to move forward with the planned Steelers House fan event in Paris," the team told fans.

That same day, Nicki Ewell, the NFL's vice president for global events, said no league fan events had been affected. Dani Watt, the wife of Steelers linebacker T.J. Watt, questioned the decision to play in Paris on her Instagram Story earlier in the week.

MORE: Week 7 fantasy football RB start-sit: Dowdle, Mason, Pollard, Wilson

French Interior Minister Laurent Nuñez said as many as 70,000 officers would be deployed nationwide Saturday. Paris police approved one march from Bastille to Nation and banned all other demonstrations in the city. Authorities closed 24 metro stations, the Louvre and the Orsay Museum.

The unrest began in mid-September with high school students protesting overcrowded classrooms, teacher shortages and outdated facilities, then spread as trade unions and the yellow vests movement joined over the rising cost of living. The Oct. 17 demonstrations drew more than 500,000 people nationwide, and authorities arrested more than 2,000 people in Paris.

The U.S. State Department issued a security alert this week urging "Americans in Paris for the NFL game" to "exercise caution and be aware of their surroundings."

MORE: Week 7 buy-low trade targets: Dak Prescott, Saquon Barkley discounts

Oct 23, 2026; Paris, France. New Orleans Saints coach Kellen Moore during press conference at Stade Montbauron. | Kirby Lee-Imagn Images

NFL Commissioner Roger Goodell said Wednesday that he was comfortable with the teams playing safely in Paris. "We've made all the preparations," Goodell said. "Protests are not unusual in France, as you know, and that's something that we will be aware of and make sure that the appropriate arrangements are made."

Peter O'Reilly, the NFL's executive vice president for club business, international and league events, said there was never serious consideration of canceling or moving the game. French President Emmanuel Macron hosted Goodell, Saints owner Gayle Benson and Steelers president Art Rooney II at the Élysée Palace on Friday to "discuss Sunday's game and the long-term future of the NFL in Paris."

The Steelers (4-2) won 26-19 in overtime at the Tampa Bay Buccaneers last week, while the Saints (2-4) snapped a three-game losing streak with a 34-27 win at the New York Giants.

When to buy Apple’s accessories (and which ones to buy cheaper elsewhere)

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dropping $1,200 on a brand-new iPhone only to have an Apple Store salesperson casually hand you a $49 silicone case and a $39 charging brick feels like a minor mugging.

Yes, Apple’s accessory margins are legendary. The company has built a multibillion-dollar side business selling $19 polishing cloths and $39 plastic dongles, banking on the assumption that you will blindly buy official gear for everything just to keep your new device working safely.

But that assumption is wrong.

At the same time, going bottom-barrel cheap for certain power components can result in slow charging speeds, excessive heat, or degraded battery health. Knowing where to spend your money and where to save comes down to a few clear rules.

Here’s a practical breakdown of which Apple accessories justify the official markup, and which ones you should buy third-party for a fraction of the price.

Buy official

MagSafe wireless chargers and power banks

There are a few specific categories where buying official Apple gear is genuinely worth the price tag, mostly where precision engineering, thermal safety, and input latency come into play.

MagSafe wireless chargers are a prime example. The official $39 Apple MagSafe charger contains proprietary hardware modules that communicate directly with iOS thermal management. It delivers fast wireless charging up to 25 watts while throttling output if your phone gets warm.

Most $15 magnetic knockoffs on Amazon are merely basic 7.5-watt Qi chargers with a magnetic ring glued inside. They generate excessive heat, charge your phone at half speed, and degrade long-term battery health.

Apple Pencil and iPad Magic Keyboard

The same rule applies to the Apple Pencil and iPad Magic Keyboard. An official Apple Pencil offers sub-millisecond latency, tilt sensitivity, and pressure tracking that generic $20 active styluses simply cannot replicate.

For the $300 (and up) Magic Keyboard, Apple’s Smart Connector passes power directly through the hinge without requiring separate Bluetooth pairing or charging dongles, and the glass trackpad remains unmatched in daily feel compared with plastic third-party alternatives.

Buy elsewhere

USB-C charging cables

When it comes to physical protection, textiles, and basic copper wiring, however, skip the Apple Store and head straight to a third party.

Standard USB-C charging cables are a classic case of Apple overcharging for a fragile product. Apple’s white cables are notorious for yellowing, fraying at the collar, and splitting after a year of travel.

Trusty third-party brands like Anker, Ugreen, and Spigen sell double-braided nylon USB-C cables rated for 100-watt power delivery and 30,000 bends for less than half the price of an Apple cable.

Cases and screen protectors

Protective cases and screen protectors are another area where third-party manufacturers consistently win. Apple’s official $49 silicone cases famously peel at the corners after a few months in a pocket, and their clear cases are notoriously slippery.

Third-party case makers offer air-cushioned corner protection, grippy textures, and built-in MagSafe rings on the cheap. Furthermore, paying Apple $40 in-store for a Belkin glass screen protector is painful when a $10 three-pack on Amazon includes an auto-alignment tray that guarantees a bubble-free installation in seconds.

Watch bands

Apple’s $50 woven nylon and silicone watch bands carry a criminal markup. Third-party woven nylon bands match the stretch, comfort, and lug fit of official bands so closely that you can buy a four-pack of different colors for a fraction of the cost of one official strap.

AirTag keychains and mounts

As for AirTags, paying $35 for a leather holder for a $29 tracking puck makes no financial sense. Third-party retailers are flooded with rugged, waterproof, screw-lock AirTag mounts that protect the tracker far better than Apple’s open-face leather loop—and most cost around $10.

The golden rule for outfitting your Apple gear is straightforward: Buy official for internal power management and precision inputs, but buy third-party for physical protection, textiles, and charging cables. Sticking to vetted tier-one brands like Anker, Spigen, and Ugreen allows you to fully kit out your new setup while keeping a substantial chunk of change in your bank account.

Indonesia cuts gas exports to Singapore amid row over haze

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The cut follows a week of warnings from Jakarta

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Indonesia has cut natural gas exports to Singapore, escalating a row over the haze from its wildfires.

The cut began on Saturday and totals 31 million standard cubic feet a day, with 15 million from the Natuna field in the Riau Islands and 16 million from Sumatra, the head of the state agency that supervises the oil and gas sector said in a statement.

Djoko Siswanto of SKK Migas said the move was made "to maintain the reliability of domestic gas supply for electricity”, according to AFP.

"We will continue to monitor developments on a daily basis," he said. Only Singapore is affected for now.

Singapore's Energy Market Authority said the reduction is less than two per cent of the city-state's total daily gas demand and that it has contingency plans in place.

"EMA understands that our importers will pursue all relevant recourse in their contracts for the gas supply disruption," it said in a statement to AFP.

The cut follows a week of warnings from Jakarta.

Indonesia's energy minister, Bahlil Lahadalia, said on Thursday that Indonesia could stop supplying countries that do not respect it, at the opening of gas facilities off East Kalimantan, according to Eco-Business.

"I will carry out an evaluation, and I will not hesitate to consider halting exports to countries that disrespect Indonesia," he said.

"The direction is clear: our natural resources will be used for domestic needs first. This is survival mode." He did not name any country.

The day before, president Prabowo Subianto ordered police, prosecutors, and the forestry ministry to investigate the companies behind the fires, and rebuked critics abroad.

"For certain countries that like to insult the Indonesian nation, in time we will reveal the companies," Mr Prabowo told local media.

Singapore has served legal notices under its haze law on 10 Indonesian companies it suspects of contributing to the fires, and has promised legal action against those responsible. Its stock exchange has also questioned seven listed agricultural companies about possible links to the fires.

Singapore relies on gas for about 95 per cent of its electricity and imports Indonesian gas mainly by pipeline under contracts that expire at the end of 2028, according to Singapore's trade ministry.

Liquefied natural gas made up more than half of its gas imports in 2024, and a second terminal for it is being built. Indonesia supplied 145.3 billion cubic feet to Singapore in 2024, according to US Energy Information Administration data.

The fires have consumed more than 1.9 million hectares in the nine months to September, according to the deforestation monitor Nusantara Atlas. More than 333,470 people have suffered acute respiratory illnesses across seven Indonesian provinces since mid-August, according to the health ministry.

The haze has also reached Malaysia and East Timor, and the ASEAN regional bloc has called a meeting over the crisis.

Malaysia's environment minister Arthur Joseph Kurup said his country reserved the right to demand effective action from Indonesia and would raise the haze at a meeting of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) in Manila.

Anger has spread on both sides of the border. Thousands of students have protested across Indonesia over the government's response, including more than 500 outside the central Kalimantan governor's office on Friday, demanding a national disaster declaration.

In Malaysia, some consumers have begun boycotting Indonesian goods after Indonesia's government communications chief Syahganda Nainggolan said the oxygen in Singapore and Malaysia comes from Indonesia's forests, the Straits Times reported.

First international flight takes off from new Sydney airport

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Western Sydney International Airport offers the first alternative to the city's main airport, which has been operating for more than a century.

An Air New Zealand plane takes off as the first international flight to depart the newly-opened Western Sydney International Airport in Sydney on Oct 26, 2026. (Photo: AFP/Saeed Khan)

SYDNEY: The first international flight took off on Monday (Oct 26) from a new, multi-billion-dollar airport in the fast-growing western suburbs of Sydney.

After more than four decades of planning, Western Sydney International Airport offers the first alternative to the city's main airport, which has been operating for more than a century.

An Air New Zealand plane from Auckland landed and soon afterwards flew out of the airport in the morning, launching a three-flights-a-week service to Sydney's largely working-class western suburbs.

"It's not every day you get to be part of opening a brand-new international airport," said Air New Zealand chief operations officer Kate Boyer.

"Western Sydney is home to more than 2.5 million people and it's a further gateway for Australian visitors to discover everything our country has to offer."

The new single-runway airport, Australia's first new major aviation hub in more than five decades, began domestic flights on Sunday, and freight operations in July.

Sydney's main airport, Kingsford Smith, is Australia's busiest, its three runways handling more than 42 million passengers last year.

Lying in Botany Bay just 9km from the city centre, planes using the airport roar over many city homes, and a curfew bars passenger aircraft from operating during the night.

The new airport in western Sydney has no such restrictions.

"Through our 24-hour operations, Western Sydney International Airport gives airlines greater flexibility to connect into global hubs and travellers more choice in how they reach the world," said airport chief executive Simon Hickey.

Qantas' budget carrier Jetstar is now the main domestic carrier, flying to the Gold Coast, Melbourne and Brisbane.

The airport says other international carriers are coming, including Qatar Airways from December 2027, Fiji Airways from March 2027, and Vietjet Air from January 2027.

Built at a cost of A$5.3 billion (US$3.7 billion), excluding surrounding transport works, the airport is reachable by road but is not expected to have a direct rail link until at least December 2027.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said the new airport marked a "wonderful new chapter" for Australia.

It would also drive economic and jobs into western Sydney, an area often overshadowed by the city's famed harbour and central business district (CBD), he said at the weekend.

"For too long, Sydney has looked towards the harbour and the CBD. What this does is turn it around to where people live, to where jobs occur, to where investment occurs in western Sydney."

Caribbean seeks reparations and questions role of monarchy before King Charles III visits

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The royals are coming, and the Caribbean wants to be heard.

Islanders are seeking slavery reparations from Britain, a formal apology for its role in the slave trade and greater independence from the former colonial power.

The Bahamas National Reparations Committee said it already knows what awaits the Caribbean as King Charles III and Queen Camilla prepare for this week's trip: “Schools emptied of children who will stand in the sun and wave for the Crown that has taken everything from their ancestors and comes back smiling.”

Charles is expected to visit the Bahamas on Tuesday to begin the nine-day trip to the Caribbean. His last official visit to the archipelago was in July 1973 as a prince who granted the Bahamas independence, ending 300 years of British rule. But the Bahamas remains a Commonwealth realm, along with six other Caribbean nations that recognize Charles as their king and head of state.

While in the Bahamas, Charles is scheduled to visit Clifford Park, where the archipelago’s black, turquoise and yellow flag was raised for the first time more than half a century ago.

His visit will raise important questions, said Niambi Hall Campbell-Dean, chair of the Bahamas National Reparations Committee.

“Why do we still have a king? What does this person really represent to us? Why is he coming and how will that benefit us?” she said.

A psychology professor at the University of the Bahamas, Campbell-Dean said she recently mentioned Charles’ upcoming visit to her students, and they were not excited. She responded: “I know y’all might not care about this man who is coming, but here’s why this is of importance.”

Leaders across the Caribbean have long been pushing for an apology and reparations from Britain.

In early September, Jamaica took its case for reparations to Charles, and the case has since been referred to the United Kingdom’s highest court of appeal for the Caribbean island. It was an unprecedented legal maneuver, even though the British government has consistently rejected demands for either an apology or reparations.

A visit to Guyana more than 200 years after a slave revolt

After three days in the Bahamas, Charles is scheduled to fly to the South American country of Guyana. The former British colony was the site of one of the biggest slave revolts in the region during the colonial era, with thousands of slaves uprising during the 1823 Demerara rebellion on the plantation owned by the father of former British Prime Minister William Gladstone.

More than 200 slaves were killed as a result; several were decapitated and their heads placed on poles.

In a recent interview with ITV News, Guyanese President Irfaan Ali said Charles has “rightfully recognized slavery as the darkest days in human history.”

“Moving to the apology is the next step,” he added. “You exploited the region. You destroyed lives. You created underdevelopment.”

While in Guyana, Charles is expected to meet with Ali and elected leaders of Amerindian villages known as “toshaos.”

Nikita Miller, toshao of Chinese Landing, a rural Amerindian community in northern Guyana, said she was not invited.

She noted that her community has been mired in a decades-long land fight with miners seeking to extract gold from the area, and she would have liked the opportunity to talk about an issue that affects other toshaos as well.

“We have been suffering,” she said. “We want the government to respect Indigenous rights. … We have long-standing issues with our land.”

Miller said she believes the king’s visit might help improve things for Guyana overall, but not for Amerindian villages.

After two days in Guyana, Charles is scheduled to fly to Antigua and Barbuda for the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting and meet with leaders of 56 member states. Talks are expected to focus on climate, trade and development, but some Caribbean leaders have said slavery reparations should dominate conversations.

The royals to skip Jamaica as it seeks more independence

The British royals have long visited Jamaica, but it’s not on Charles' itinerary this time.

It’s just as well, said 51-year-old Rastafarian Steven Bennett.

“We don’t need any royal wave or photo-taking while the wounds of our ancestors are still being ignored. He still refuses to formally apologize for chattel slavery,” Bennett said. “Until the British monarchy is ready to stand on Jamaican soil, look us in the eye, apologize, and talk about actual repair and restitution, he has no business here. We want justice, not a parade.”

Jamaica in recent years has been pushing to oust Charles as head of state and become a republic like Barbados did in 2021.

Antonio Williams, a 25-year-old university student, said young Jamaicans have zero connection to the Crown.

“I think Buckingham Palace has read the room after the absolute PR disaster of William and Kate’s Caribbean tour a few years back,” he said, referring to the 2022 visit of the Prince and Princess of Wales to the region, where they faced protests and calls for reparations.

“We are actively moving toward becoming a republic and breaking these colonial ties,” Williams said. “Having the king visit now would just be incredibly awkward. Skipping us is a sign that they know their influence here is expiring.”

But 68-year-old retired civil servant Hermine Reid recalled with fondness how Charles’ mother, Queen Elizabeth II, used to visit Jamaica.

“It was always such a grand, respectful occasion, and people would line the streets in their Sunday best,” she said. “Things are very different now. It’s a little sad for my generation to see how much the relationship has fractured, but I suppose this is simply the modern era. Jamaica is growing up and charting its own course, and the king clearly recognizes that.”

Myers Jr. reported from Kingston, Jamaica.

US military says it helped Gulf states export 1.5 billion barrels of oil

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

US Central Command says more than 3,000 commercial vessels have transited the Strait of Hormuz with its support.

US military forces have helped Gulf countries export more than 1.5 billion barrels of oil since June, despite Iran’s efforts to control the critical waterway, according to US Central Command (COMMAND).

The US military command said on Sunday that more than 3,000 commercial vessels had transited the Strait of Hormuz with its support over the past six months.

It added that more than 95 percent of ships navigating the channel had sailed through safely in recent months despite Iranian threats, “clearly demonstrating that the United States and regional partners control the international waterway”.

CENTCOM announced the figures as Commander Admiral Brad Cooper made a visit to the region, during which he observed the “current operational environment” in the strait while flying in a US Army helicopter.

“We continue to stand shoulder to shoulder with our Gulf partners and the shipping industry,” Cooper said in a statement.

“This visit underscores our strong commitment to continued freedom of navigation through the Strait of Hormuz.”

Cooper said the primary transit routes for vessels in the strait were free of Iranian mines due to the “incredible efforts” of personnel who conduct mine clearance operations.

“We remain intensely focused on keeping commercial traffic flowing as we work with our teammates in the US interagency, Gulf Cooperation Council, and shipping industry,” he said.

Crude oil flows have largely returned to levels seen before the US and Israel launched their war on Iran in late February, despite the ongoing threat of attacks on commercial vessels by Iranian forces.

At least 16.5 million barrels of oil per day left the region in September, equal to the pre-war average, excluding Iran, according to market intelligence firm Kpler.

Analysts have attributed the rebound to a combination of offshore ship-to-ship transfers, US military escorts, and increased use of oil pipelines.

However, oil prices remain far higher than before the start of the war.

Brent crude futures stood at about $114.50 a barrel on Sunday, after easing about 2 percent from last week’s nearly six-month high.

Brent settled at $72.48 per barrel on February 27, the day before the US and Israel began strikes on Iran.

→ Baca artikel asli

Netflix relocated me to Japan. After I left the job, I found a new way to stay.

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Matthew Kelson, now 43, who was transferred by Netflix from Los Angeles to Tokyo and is now the founder of CineYama, an independent cinema in Uenohara, Yamanashi Prefecture, Japan. His words have been edited for length and clarity.

Japan isn't new to me. In middle school, I spent two weeks living with a host family as part of an exchange program. I returned a couple of times as an adult.

I was working at Netflix in Los Angeles when the company relocated me to Japan in 2020. I was based in Tokyo for two years before moving to Uenohara, a small town about an hour and a half by train from Tokyo. I still spent time in the capital for occasional meetings and traveled extensively across Asia for screenings. Otherwise, I worked remotely from home. After a company restructuring, I left Amazon in 2024.

I wanted to stay in Japan. My daughter was six months old when my wife and I moved, and my son was born three years later. We thought it was a great place for them to grow up. I started thinking about how I could utilize my expertise in cinema and give back to the community.

While scouting for locations, I came across an abandoned kindergarten. In 2024, I started plans to turn the old school into a cinema. And in December, I got my permanent residency in Japan through the highly skilled professional fast-track program. A year later, I formed my company and spent another year renovating before opening a cinema in March.

I spent most of my childhood in Michigan, in the Detroit area. My father was a professor of international business. He traveled for work often. It got me curious about the world around me.

At 23, I came across the Detroit Film Center and took a filmmaking course. A year later, I became a teacher and worked as a projectionist there. Through that, I started working at an independent art theater in the Detroit suburbs. I started as an usher, then became the theater manager.

Within the two years of working there, I fell in love with the film world. At 25, I renovated an old school's auditorium into a theater in Michigan using my savings. The theater ran for two years, but after disputes with the landlord, it closed.

I was looking for a fresh start, so in 2011, I moved to Los Angeles. At first, I knocked on the doors of all the film distributors I'd worked with until I met some people at an independent film distribution company. I joined as an intern and worked my way up. I landed a job on Netflix's creative team two years later.

Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.

Getting the permit to renovate the abandoned kindergarten was a long process. I had to negotiate with officials at city hall and the residents about how I'd use the building, establish the company, deal with fire and health regulations, and construction in a language I don't speak fluently.

I learned that pushing harder is not necessarily what gets things done in Japan. It was about patience, building trust, and showing people that I was serious and that the project wasn't going to disappear in six months.

There were days when I wondered why I had chosen to make my life so difficult. But every milestone, from getting the building, the inspection, installing the projector, and finally opening the doors, made me more determined.

When the cinema first opened, the elderly residents seemed cautious. After a preview, they told me they were impressed to see the place cleaned up and put back into use. It's important for me to have screenings that kids and families can come to and enjoy the space.

Tickets cost 1,500 Japanese yen, or about $9.50. We offer a discount to seniors and residents. I stick with showing content that is harder to find elsewhere, so that it gives viewers a perspective on other ways of life and culture.

It's mostly family-run. I manage the operations, and my wife helps out sometimes. My kids stamp the tickets when they're around.

Opening the theater in the US was different from doing it in Japan. I was in my 20s, had little money, and was just figuring things out in Detroit. The one in Japan came after 15 years of working in film distribution, creative production, and international screenings.

It's been exciting living over here, having the cinema, and meeting people from all over the world. While life in the US would've been much easier, I like the challenges in Japan. That's why I was at Netflix for so long, because it wasn't an easy job either. I learned life in hard mode.

Baby orangutans in India expose illegal wildlife trade as experts question a billionaire’s zoo

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The villagers who first spotted the five baby orangutans in a casuarina forest in India in early September were puzzled by their reddish hair and unfamiliar faces.

The three males and two females, aged six to eight months — were left on a plantation in eastern India’s Odisha, over 2,700 kilometers (1,700 miles) from their native rainforests in Borneo and Sumatra, evidence of a growing illicit trade of apes being trafficked into India.

They're now at an Indian zoo, where keepers reportedly have been playing classical music to soothe them.

Wild orangutans are native only to the islands of Borneo and Sumatra, where they sustain rainforests by spreading seeds across vast areas, allowing trees to regenerate and store carbon that would otherwise contribute to global warming. The species is critically endangered, threatened by severe habitat loss. There are about 119,500 orangutans left in the wild, according to conservation organization World Wildlife Fund.

The global illegal wildlife trade is estimated to be worth $34 billion annually, according to a 2026 study by National University of Singapore researchers, and India is emerging as an important market. Experts say seizures reflect a fraction of a booming trade in exotic or non-native animals sold to wealthy Indian collectors.

India’s wildlife laws were mainly designed to protect native species such as elephants, rhinoceroses and tigers from being trafficked overseas. Gaps in controls on imported animals complicate efforts to trace the origins of exotic wildlife.

In 2022, two juvenile Sumatran orangutans were found in boxes at a checkpoint between the northeastern states of Assam and Mizoram. Another was rescued in Mizoram in 2023, and a young orangutan was found in an apartment near Mumbai in November 2024. At least 23 gibbons were seized at Indian airports in 2025, according to an analysis by nonprofit Wildlife Trust of India. In May 2026, Egyptian authorities seized three India-bound, sedated baby chimpanzees.

“India has become the number one destination for the live trade of primates,” said Sinan Serhadli, a wildlife trafficking expert at the Orangutan Information Centre in Indonesia’s North Sumatra province.

India’s exotic wildlife trade and Southeast Asian trafficking routes

Wealthy collectors who prize rare animals as “status symbols” are driving the trade in trafficked animals from Southeast Asia, a regional hub for trade in species from elsewhere, said Uttara Mendiratta, a wildlife trafficking researcher in India.

Expanding tourism has led to more direct flights to India through cities like Bangkok and Kuala Lumpur, giving traffickers routes that are faster, albeit riskier, than overland border crossings.

“The profit margins are so high that they can afford to lose a few (animals) and still make a profit,” Mendiratta said.

Questions also surround legal trade. Most commercial trade in orangutans is banned, but exceptions exist for captive-bred animals. India imported 41 orangutans between 2021 and 2024, according to CITES, the global treaty regulating trade in endangered wildlife.

Sinan Serhadli, a wildlife trafficking expert at Indonesia’s Orangutan Information Centre, questioned whether the animals were legitimately bred in captivity.

“It just shows the loopholes. And ... corruption as well,” he said.

The CITES Secretariat declined to comment, saying the matter was under review

Loopholes in India’s wildlife laws enable wildlife trafficking

Concerned about diseases spreading from animals to people during the COVID-19 pandemic, India allowed people to register their exotic animals in 2020 without proving their origins.

They can report births, deaths and breeding of non-native species through an online portal. But the government lacks enough staff and expertise to adequately inspect collections and identify global species. The system also leaves room for trafficked animals to be misrepresented as registered ones, Mendiratta said.

India tightened its wildlife laws in 2022, giving authorities clearer powers to seize illegally traded exotic animals and prosecute traffickers. But by then, it already had “opened the floodgates,” she said.

India’s exotic pet trade “sits at the crossroads of biodiversity loss, unbridled community demand, and organized criminal activity,” said a 2026 report by Humane World for Animals India.

India's forest ministry didn't respond to AP's request for comment.

Indonesia renews push to repatriate orangutans from India

Indonesia’s Ministry of Forestry said it was taking “active steps” to bring home the five baby orangutans found in Odisha, sending experts to verify their origins and assess their health.

Their mothers were likely killed, traumatizing infants that normally would depend on them for up to nine years.

Conservationists have successfully returned orangutans to Indonesia from Thailand, but so far only one has been repatriated from India.

When one orangutan was transferred last year to a private wildlife facility known as Vantara, run by billionaire Mukesh Ambani’s family, India’s federal wildlife officials and India’s CITES authority were not informed, a federal wildlife official to New Delhi wrote in a report.

Officials concerned over the orangutan's care at a local public zoo ordered it transferred to Vantara, which describes itself as a wildlife rescue and rehabilitation center. The orangutan was treated for a respiratory infection and pneumonia before it was returned to Indonesia on Oct. 19 after 19 months at the private facility.

India’s CITES authority didn’t respond to AP’s request for comment.

Questions over the world’s rarest great ape in Indian zoo

Vantara, a 3,500-acre wildlife facility in western India, has over 150,000 animals and over 2,000 different species.

It has faced international scrutiny over its animal acquisitions. In November 2025, CITES questioned shipments to Vantara that included snow leopards from Germany, a bonobo said to have been bred in Iraq and a gorilla reportedly from Haiti. The primates are not native to those countries.

India’s Supreme Court cleared Vantara of wildlife trafficking and animal mistreatment allegations in 2025.

In September, 27 wildlife conservation and animal welfare organizations issued a letter urging the facility to end imports of wild animals, raising concerns about endangered species and animal welfare.

The groups said they had “never encountered anything comparable to the scale and diversity” of Vantara’s imports, citing more than 60,000 animals acquired in a short period.

One controversial acquisition, according to Vantara's 2024-25 inventory, is a Tapanuli orangutan, the world’s rarest great ape. Recognized as a distinct species only in 2017, it's only found in a small area of Indonesia’s Sumatra island, where 716 remain in the wild.

“The Tapanuli orangutan is hovering just above extinction,” said Serhadli, whose organization is one of the few that works with the species. The one at Vantara would be the only Tapanuli orangutan in captivity.

CITES trade data shows that the animal left Indonesia in 2024 and arrived in India via the United Arab Emirates.

“That can’t happen legally,” Serhadli said.

Indonesia’s Ministry of Forestry said it was taking “seriously” reports that a Tapanuli orangutan might be outside the country. It said it couldn't confirm the animal’s species, origins or how it got to India. An investigation would require checking permits and transfer records, along with scientific testing, the ministry said.

Neither Vantara nor Indonesia’s CITES authority responded to requests for comment from AP.

Milko reported from Islamabad, Pakistan. Associated Press journalist Edna Tarigan contributed from Jakarta, Indonesia.

The Associated Press’ climate and environmental coverage receives financial support from multiple private foundations. AP is solely responsible for all content. Find AP’s standards for working with philanthropies, a list of supporters and funded coverage areas at AP.org.

Good Food Guide Awards LIVE updates: Crowd gives standing ovation to Sydney icon Peter Gilmore as he wins Legend Award; Peter Fiander takes out Young Chef of the Year as all the winners, hats revealed

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You have reached your maximum number of saved items.

Remove items from your saved list to add more.

You've used all of your gift articles. 3 more will become available on the 1st of every month.

For a moment there, things were looking grim for NSW dining. The closure of two three-hatted restaurants – Quay and Oncore by Clare Smyth – in February was followed by two-hatted Bentley calling it a day in the CBD, and much-loved Ursula’s pulling up stumps in Paddington.

It was looking unlikely we would have 10 cracking spots to nominate as the Good Food Guide’s New Restaurant of the Year.

But then, bang! A volley of new bars, cafes and restaurants opened in June, some led by veteran operators pouring decades of experience into passion projects, others led by young guns making sure NSW will be the best Australian state for eating and drinking for generations to come.

Marrickville’s Ester Spirits is on martini duty today, but when it comes to the stiff drink, competition is stiff.

A normal martini ideally should be between -5C and -10C, but people have been getting competitive. The general rule of thumb is “as cold as humanly possible” but some bartenders are now taking that to extremes.

Take Alfie’s, for example. The high-energy chophouse in Sydney’s CBD served us the coldest martini of the year – a positively freezing -18C. Could have been colder, in my humble opinion.

(Today’s martinis are of the two-sip variety!)

From backstage: Carlisle Castle Hotel executive chef Cameron Votano and head chef Jimmy Walker held up their Pub of the Year frame high before a short speech on the podium.

Walker was still clutching it when we caught up with the duo once they walked off the stage.

A stunned Walker echoed his colleague, “It’s just crazy. I can’t believe how quickly it’s happened… we’ve only just opened and six weeks ago. The recognition’s insane in this amount of time, it’s just unbelievable.”

The 150-year-old Newtown pub, which had been family-owned-and-run for the past 60 years, was recently sold to Universal Hotels, who also owns Redfern’s Bat & Ball Hotel. The revamped, pokie-free community hub opened in September.

From the bar: Mark Best, asked who he thinks will win Chef of the Year, says it’s a tight race between Nicholas Hill of Porcine and South End’s Alex Tong and Hussein Sarhan. But he thinks the duo from Newtown will bring it home.

South End was awarded two hats earlier this year in its opening review by Sydney Morning Herald Good Food Guide editor Callan Boys. Sarhan previously cooked at produce-forward Fred’s in Paddington; Tong was sous chef at fire-licked Ester. Together, that’s one powerhouse package.

Best has picked up a few awards himself over the years, including the Guide’s Chef of the Year in 2009, and mentored last year’s Young Chef of the Year winner Jen Kwok Lee. Will his tip be the winning one? Stay tuned to find out.

When Quay served its final course on February 14 after four decades in business, it went out on top with the Good Food Guide’s highest ranking of three hats, a level it maintained since 2002, soon after Peter Gilmore took on the role of executive chef.

No other Sydney restaurant has come close to equalling that three-hat run, but Quay’s real legacy is its influence on Australian cuisine, produce and hospitality.

See the full timeline of Quay, and Legend Award winner Gilmore’s influence on its success, right.

The Trailblazer Award, founded in memory of Bill Granger and presented by SevenRooms, recognises an individual, team or business driving Australian food forward. The winner should have a unique point of view, embrace hospitality in its truest sense, and forge a path for others.

This year’s winner, Ahana Dutt, takes out the award for her work at her Newtown restaurant, Kolkata Social, which aims to flip the expectations of Indian cooking on their head.

The diner, which began as a social enterprise with Plate it Forward but is now under Dutt’s ownership, is less an Indian restaurant than a Bengali one, committed fiercely to the flavours and food of her heritage, yet also grounded firmly in place, spinning Kolkata through a third-culture lens. Think Davidson plum sharpening alu’r dom Hasselback potatoes (as served to tonight’s attendees), or kosha that sees retired Meredith Dairy goat cooked in a slow braise, dark and deeply spiced.

Trailblazing? Definitely. Australian diners may be increasingly fluent in the flavours of regional Italy or China, but Dutt still fends off questions about why she refuses to serve butter chicken. The chef recognises that there’s plenty of diversity in Sydney’s Indian food scene, but the broader public’s expectations, she says, can be paralysing.

Sticking to her guns at Kolkata Social is one more step to the food of Australia’s largest migrant population being celebrated in all its depth, nuance and glory.

The winner of the Legend Award has received a rock star’s reception inside Carriageworks.

As Peter Gilmore shook hands with fans and friends while approaching the podium, the entire room stood in applause.

A video played to the audience featured hundreds of photos of his food, including the famous Snow Egg dessert dish. “Seeing it like that is pretty crazy,” he said.

He thanked Leon Fink, and said he was lucky to work with hundreds of chefs in his restaurants. “God, it’s been a ride, absolutely. What can I say? This industry has given me so much, so much joy and so much love from everyone that works in this industry.”

“It hasn’t been work. It’s been my life … and an amazing amount of good fortune. And I’ve been lucky to work with some of the best people in this industry over the years.”

Gilmore paid special thanks to his wife, Kath Gilmore, “who has been my biggest support, and without her, I wouldn’t have had a normal life at all”.

In the year 2000, The Sydney Morning Herald published what could now be described as a historic food review. A young fellow running a restaurant in the furthest reaches of the northern beaches caught Terry Durack’s eye. This chap, wrote Durack, had “a real talent for sending out beautifully structured food with innate simplicity.”

With the benefit of 26 years of hindsight, we now know that Durack was onto something. Said young chap was Peter Gilmore, who went on to transform Australian dining with his tenure at Quay and Bennelong.

After earning three hats for 23 consecutive years, and winning Restaurant of the Year six times, Gilmore is now the winner of the 42nd Sydney Morning Herald Good Food Guide Legend Award.

As we are sure the hundreds of chefs who have passed through his kitchens and now carry on his legacy would agree, he has more than earned it. Congratulations to Peter Gilmore, whose new honour earns a standing ovation from the crowd.

Peter Fiander has applied for the Young Chef of the Year award before, so his origin story was already familiar to judges – and former winners – Lauren Eldridge, Dan Puskas, Louis Tikaram and Phil Wood: after leaving high school early, he started his career in Castle Hill at the age of 16 before going on to work at LP’s Quality Meats, Ragazzi and Porcine.

What impressed the panel this time around, however, was how he has evolved into a leader since stepping into the role of head chef at Osteria Luna.

Anyone can cook, after all, but not everyone can lead a team through the heat of service, and still find time to source whole beasts so his chefs can upgrade their butchery skills.

Fiander is a chef obsessed with the small stuff, a cook-it-from-scratch, do-it-yourself, learn-it-then-teach-it kind of cook, who wants all those steps to come together in a way that appears effortless on the plate.

Lula wins in Brazil but faces hostile Congress and divided country

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Watch: How President Lula turned it around to beat FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro

Brazil's veteran left-wing president, Luiz InÃ¡cio Lula da Silva, has been re-elected after a bitterly fought campaign, beating his right-wing rival FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro, who has not conceded.

With all the votes counted, Lula had 51.79% of the votes, compared Bolsonaro's 48.21%, Brazil's Superior Electoral Court announced.

But Bolsonaro has refused to recognize the result, citing the extension of polling by two hours as "inexplicable" - even though his campaign team, as well as that of his rival, had requested that polling stations be kept open longer.

He told his supporters in Brasilia that he would seek the annulment of the election.

However, this has not stopped Lula and his supporters from celebrating what they describe as a "historic victory" and an surprise turn-around.

It is Lula's fourth win in a presidential election, having served two terms as president from January 2003 to December 2010, and again from January 2023.

But the road to his win in Sunday's run-off was not a smooth one.

Bolsonaro had beaten Lula in the first round on 4 October and many had not thought it possible for him to turn the result around in just three weeks.

He has also bucked a wider trend in Latin America, where voters have punished incumbents and turned to the right. Across the region, large countries such as Argentina, Chile and Colombia all now have conservative leaders at their helm.

Lula greets supporters after casting his ballot on Sunday

Lula's campaign sprang back to life following his first-round defeat driven by street demonstrations and a massive social media push.

The former metalworker, who has been the dominant figure on Brazil's left since he first ran for president in 1989, nevertheless faces the most challenging political landscape since first taking office in 2003.

His new term, set to begin on 5 January 2027, will start amid significant constraints at home and abroad, including animosity from US President Donald Trump.

While Trump did not endorse Lula's rival, FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro, directly â something he did with the right-wing candidates who went on to win presidential elections held in Colombia and Honduras â there is little doubt his administration would have preferred to see a conservative at the helm of this country of 213 million people.

The White House regards Latin America as an area of US influence with the Trump administration keen to have leaders in power who support its policies, including on defence, security and trade.

It is also keen to reduce China's influence in the Western Hemisphere.

Brazil, which is oil-rich and holds the world's second-largest reserves of rare earth minerals, is the only big country in South America not governed by a right-wing leader, with Lula largely seen as China-friendly.

FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro had promised to align Brazil more closely with Washington while Lula had warned the US not to interfere in the election and insisted that Brazil would "never fit into anyone's backyard".

But while there may be disappointment in the White House, Lula's victory will be welcomed by environmentalists who had feared that a win by Bolsonaro would have given Brazil's influential agribusiness and mining sectors more power.

They had pointed to record levels of deforestation and forest fires in the Amazon during the presidency of his father, Jair, arguing that under the younger Bolsonaro laxer environmental policies could again lead to destruction in this key ecosystem.

An aerial view from a Greenpeace flyover shows a fire burning in the Amazon rainforest during the UN Climate Change Conference (COP30), held near Centro Novo in the state of Maranhao, Brazil, in 2025

While Lula's supporters are ecstatic about his victory, the result shows that the country remains deeply divided, with more than 48% of voters choosing Bolsonaro.

In addition to the challenge of uniting such a deeply polarised country, Lula will have to govern alongside the most right-wing Congress in Brazil's democratic era.

Bolsonaro's Liberal Party (PL) was the party that made the biggest gains in the election.

And yet, Lula's Workers' Party managed to organise huge rallies ahead of the run-off, showing the strong grassroots support it commands.

Before the election, Lula had described the poll as "a parting of the waters for Brazil".

He promised to bridge the gap between Brazil's opposing political poles with "a new way of governing", adding in a WhatsApp livestream before the election that "this country will never be the same again".

While he provided little detail as to what the new way of governing would entail, he insisted he would "remain firm in the fight for a more just country, with opportunities for all and policies directed at the needs of the population".

With a largely hostile Congress, this fight just may prove much harder for the veteran leader - who will be 85 by the end of his fourth term - than in his three previous terms in office.

Forty Iranian sailors rescued off Sri Lanka coast after medical emergencies

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sri Lanka's foreign ministry says it has coordinated efforts to treat and repatriate 40 Iranian sailors suffering from unspecified medical emergencies in international waters off the island's southeastern coast.

Ministry spokesperson Thushara Rodrigo told BBC Sinhala they received a distress call on Saturday from Iranian ships "regarding an urgent medical need".

The rescue operation was planned by the Iranian Embassy in Colombo, he said.

Iranian ships have been stranded at sea due to a US naval blockade. Sri Lanka stresses it is neutral in the conflict, but has at times offered help to Iranian sailors in what it says is an obligation to international law.

A BBC Sinhala report last month found that there were 21 Iranian vessels located in international waters near Sri Lanka.

The 40 rescued sailors served on different ships - though Sri Lankan authorities do not have information on how many ships they belonged to, Rodrigo said.

The sailors were brought ashore and taken to Colombo for medical treatment before being sent back to Iran by plane on Saturday night, he added.

He said that several agencies were involved in the operation, including local shipping companies.

According to Rodrigo, Sri Lanka was obliged under international law and humanitarian grounds to provide the necessary facilities for medical emergencies.

"This does not mean that we will provide assistance to those ships," he said. "It is generally an internationally accepted tradition to respond to a distress call after receiving it."

"We have dealt with such cases before. This is a normal process that continues to be implemented."

The ongoing conflict in Iran started when the US and Israel launched wide-ranging strikes on the country in February. Iran responded by attacking Israel, US bases and allied states in the Gulf, and effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, a critical international trade route.

Attacks by both sides have persisted as negotiations stalled.

Sri Lanka has sought to remain neutral in the conflict, having rejected military access requests from both the US and Iran and urged both parties to prioritise dialogue.

It has also offered assistance to Iranian ships caught in the conflict on what it argues are humanitarian grounds.

In March, Sri Lankan authorities said they responded to a distress call from the Iranian ship the Iris Dena, which had sunk off its coast after a US torpedo attack.

More than 80 crew members were killed in the attack, and injured survivors were taken to a hospital in the Galle Harbour.

The following day, Sri Lankan authorities allowed another Iranian naval vessel to dock at one of its ports, with President Anura Kumara Dissanayake saying that they would "never hesitate to protect humanity".

Sri Lankan authorities said earlier this month they had no immediate plans to send aid to 19 Iranian ships near its coasts which had reported shortages of food, water and fuel. The authorities said they had to prioritise national interests, the Associated Press reported.

Singapore’s estimated geothermal resource potential more than 60 times annual electricity consumption: EMA

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A non-invasive geophysical survey found high-temperature geothermal resources several kilometres underground that may be suitable for electricity generation.

Visitors having a foot bath at the new Sembawang Hot Spring Park on Jan 4, 2020. (Photo: CNA/Jeremy Long)

SINGAPORE: Singapore's geothermal resource potential is estimated to be more than 60 times its annual electricity consumption, according to preliminary findings from a survey, the Energy Market Authority (EMA) said on Monday (Oct 26).

The non-invasive geophysical survey, conducted from 2024 to 2026, found high-temperature geothermal resources several kilometres underground that may be suitable for electricity generation. Singapore's current annual electricity consumption is around 60 terawatt-hours.

Further studies will be needed to validate the estimates of Singapore's geothermal technical potential and determine how much geothermal energy can be feasibly extracted.

Speaking at the Singapore International Energy Week 2026 on Monday, Minister for Energy, Trade and Industry (Energy and Industry) Tan See Leng described the findings as "a very good start", adding that if realised, geothermal energy could power Singapore for the next 60 or so years.

The nationwide survey was conducted by a consortium of leading global consultants, including Chevron, Metatek Group, and Surbana Jurong.

It used two non-invasive techniques to develop three-dimensional geological and heat resource models of Singapore.

Metatek Group undertook the data acquisition and processing, while Chevron used the processed data to develop a 3D geological and heat resource model of Singapore.

The survey assessed geothermal resources at depths of up to 10km for the purpose of power generation.

The consortium also assessed that there are lower-temperature geothermal resources at shallower depths, which could potentially support industrial heating and cooling using heat pumps and absorption chillers.

CNA Games Guess Word Crack the word, one row at a time Buzzword Create words using the given letters Mini Sudoku Tiny puzzle, mighty brain teaser Mini Crossword Small grid, big challenge Word Search Spot as many words as you can Show More Show Less

Following the preliminary findings, EMA said it would proceed with the next phase of studies to further assess the feasibility of harnessing geothermal energy for electricity generation, cooling and heating.

It has appointed a consortium formed by Surbana Jurong and GLJ Ltd to study the potential deployment of next-generation geothermal plants in Singapore.

The study will cover safety considerations, technical viability and the cost of geothermal energy production. It is scheduled to start by the end of this year and is estimated to take around 15 months.

Dr Tan noted that the process would not be straightforward, referring to how geothermal resources are located several kilometres underground.

The amount of electricity that can eventually be generated will depend on the cost and technical feasibility of extracting these resources, as well as the pace of advances in deep drilling technology, Dr Tan said.

"Our dense urban environment will also affect how many geothermal power plants we can eventually or feasibly deploy, and most importantly, we must keep safety as a top-of-mind consideration," he added.

EMA chief executive Puah Kok Keong said geothermal energy was an "indigenous source of clean energy that can provide constant power, and can potentially play an important role in our energy mix".

"If proven to be viable for electricity generation, Singapore’s geothermal energy resources will strengthen our energy resilience and support the decarbonisation of the power sector,” he added.

Dr Tan said that Singapore has not made a decision on whether to deploy geothermal power.

"It could take many years before we get to that decision. But nevertheless, as I've said many times before, no option is off the table for us here in Singapore. We will therefore explore this option seriously," he added.

The warning Pierce Brosnan gave his son, 25, ahead of his acting debut

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Actor said his son has the ‘strength and stamina’ to survive in the ‘capricious business’

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Pierce Brosnan warned his 25-year-old son about the realities of Hollywood as he embarks on a career in acting.

The 73-year-old James Bond star was speaking at the premiere of his son Paris’s new film, Seniors, when he shared the advice he gave his youngest on how “tough” the entertainment industry is.

“No, it's a very tough business, it is a very capricious business at times, but Paris has the strength and the stamina, he has a talent there, he's got a charisma, and he's got good sense and intuition, he really has a talent there that will need to be nurtured and worked on,” he told Fox.

“The work that you need to do, the discipline it takes to be an actor, to work on your voice, to work on your body, to work on your mind and the discipline of reading and studying...So he has all of that,” he said.

The Mamma Mia star explained that he and his current wife, Keely Shaye Smith, raised their sons Paris and Dylan with a sense of normalcy despite the Hollywood life they led.

“We kept them close... We had family dinners, drive them to school, pick them up from school,” Brosnan said. “We are very close, the whole family.”

In Seniors, Paris portrays a teenager performing community service at a retirement home, where he bonds with one of the residents, played by John Rhys-Davies, his father’s friend since the 1970s.

Paris, who is also a painter and model, said on the red carpet that it was a “full-circle” moment working with Rhys-Davies.

“My father started his career working with John, and I got to begin mine, and the experience was nothing short of a master class, truly,” he explained.

Brosnan and his late wife, the actress Cassandra Harris, had three children, Christopher, Sean and daughter Charlotte, who died in 2013 aged 41 from ovarian cancer.

Brosnan and his second wife had their two sons, Dylan, 29, and Paris, who they raised in Hawaii.

The actor has previously spoken about his close-knit family, and shared his secret to a long-lasting marriage. He has been married to Smith for 25 years.

“You know, it's just solving one problem after the [other], whether it's the washing machine or how far the finances are going to go or what your dreams are. But, ultimately, we just enjoy each other's company enormously,” he said last year.

“She's given me wings to fly and lets me, you know, gallivant around the world here making movies,” he said.
