Simba faces possible fines, licence cancellation over allegations of intentional spectrum misuse

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Simba's unauthorised spectrum use was allegedly intentional and approved by senior management. The telco is also accused of deliberately failing to fully comply with an order to produce documents.

A Simba shop at Canberra Plaza in Singapore on Aug 18, 2025. (Photo: CNA/Ooi Boon Keong)

SINGAPORE: Telecommunications operator Simba could face fines, suspension or cancellation of its licence over alleged unauthorised spectrum use and other regulatory breaches, according to new details of an ongoing investigation disclosed by its Australian parent company Tuas on Monday (Oct 26).

In a filing to the Australian Securities Exchange (ASX), Tuas said the Infocomm Media Development Authority (IMDA) had issued Simba a notice to show cause, setting out six allegations involving unauthorised spectrum use, breaches of telecommunications regulations and concerns about the company's governance and compliance practices.

These include allegations that Simba's unauthorised use of radio frequency bands was intentional and approved by its senior management, and that the telco deliberately failed to fully comply with an order to produce documents.

The filing also outlined potential regulatory actions being considered by IMDA, including financial penalties and the suspension or cancellation of Simba's spectrum rights or telecommunications licence.

CNA Games Guess Word Crack the word, one row at a time Buzzword Create words using the given letters Mini Sudoku Tiny puzzle, mighty brain teaser Mini Crossword Small grid, big challenge Word Search Spot as many words as you can Show More Show Less IMDA previously disclosed in May that it was investigating Simba over possible unauthorised spectrum use, prompting it to suspend its review of the telco's proposed S$1.43 billion (US$1.11 billion) acquisition of rival operator M1. The deal was subsequently terminated.

IMDA previously disclosed in May that it was investigating Simba over possible unauthorised spectrum use, prompting it to suspend its review of the telco's proposed S$1.43 billion (US$1.11 billion) acquisition of rival operator M1.

According to Monday's filing, IMDA alleged that Simba deployed dynamic spectrum sharing technology at about 133 sites for intermittent periods in 2022 and 2023.

This allowed the telco to use the 2100 megahertz (MHz) frequency band for both 4G and 5G services, even though it was authorised to use the band only for 5G.

Dynamic spectrum sharing is a technology that allows different generations of mobile networks, such as 4G and 5G, to share the same frequency band.

IMDA also alleged that Simba used spectrum in the 2340MHz to 2360MHz band at about 92 sites for intermittent periods between 2023 and 2026, despite not having authorisation to do so.

The regulator alleged that both instances of unauthorised spectrum use were intentional and approved by Simba's senior management.

Separately, IMDA alleged that Simba deployed 5G indoor radio access network equipment at two locations in 2023 before receiving authorisation to do so. Approval was only granted on Nov 14 that year.

The regulator also alleged that Simba did not fully comply with an order to produce documents, and that its failure to do so was deliberate and intentional.

IMDA further raised concerns about deficiencies in Simba's governance and compliance culture, which it considers should be addressed through additional regulatory obligations.

IMDA could impose financial penalties of up to 10 per cent of the annual turnover of the relevant part of Simba's business or S$1 million, whichever is higher.

Other possible actions include suspending or cancelling Simba's spectrum rights or telecommunications licence, shortening its licence period, or imposing additional regulatory obligations to address governance deficiencies.

Simba has until Nov 16 to respond to the show cause notice.

Tuas noted that IMDA has not reached any conclusions on whether regulatory action should be taken against Simba or what form such action might take.

"Simba intends to respond to the Show Cause Notice," the company said.

"As the matter remains under consideration by the regulator and no findings have been made or confirmed, Tuas does not intend to provide further comment on the matters raised in this announcement."

IMDA first disclosed its investigation into Simba in May, saying it had uncovered possible unauthorised spectrum use by the telco.

The regulator suspended its review of Simba's proposed S$1.43 billion acquisition of M1, saying the findings of its investigation could be material to its assessment of the deal.

The proposed acquisition, which would have consolidated two of Singapore's four mobile network operators, was subsequently terminated after the deadline for its completion passed.

Monday's filing sets out more specific allegations, including that certain instances of unauthorised spectrum use were intentional and approved by senior management.

Radio frequency spectrum refers to the electromagnetic waves used to carry wireless communications, including mobile phone calls and data.

IMDA regulates the allocation and assignment of spectrum to telecommunications operators, which are authorised to use specific frequency bands for their services.

These rules are intended to prevent interference between wireless services and ensure that operators use spectrum in accordance with their assigned rights and licence conditions.

Peranakan food stall Kokoyo Nyonya Delights shutting down in November after S$50,000 in losses

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

“It’s quite a pity, it’s like I failed. But I gave it my best shot,” said the hawker, who opened Kokoyo to honour his Peranakan grandmother’s recipes.

Kokoyo Nyonya Delights will shut down in November 2026. (Photos: 8Days, Kokoyo Nyonya Delights)

Kokoyo Nyonya Delights, a stall selling Peranakan food including blue pea nasi lemak, ayam buah keluak and beef rendang, is closing after two years in business. Its last day at Hougang Mall’s fourth-floor food court is on Nov 2.

Co-owner Ernest Ang, 25, told 8days.sg that the business has accumulated an estimated S$50,000 (US$39,100) in losses since February this year. Sales have also fallen by about half, with business becoming particularly difficult since September.

While the Kokoyo brand is closing for good, Ang and his brother, co-owner Irwin Ang, 29, are staying put. They plan to launch a seafood porridge concept in the same space called The Porridge Stall. The siblings hope this comforting dish will help turn things around.

The brothers announced Kokoyo’s closure in a video posted on the brand’s Instagram page on Oct 21.

They explained that footfall at the food court was initially healthy when there were fewer dining options elsewhere in Hougang Mall. Following renovations and the arrival of more eateries on other floors, they found it increasingly difficult to draw customers up to the fourth floor.

“It’s been rough these past few months, foot traffic to the food court has dropped drastically compared to when we first entered,” said Ernest Ang.

With monthly rent of S$11,000, the drop in sales has been difficult to sustain.

“The rent is high, so the breakeven point is high,” Ang told 8days.sg. “There has to be a benchmark we need to hit every day, but then the footfall is not good, so it’s directly related.”

Kokoyo first opened as a standalone eatery in Serangoon Central in 2024, before relocating to Jelita Mall as a food court stall on Holland Road, then to Hougang Mall in February this year. Ernest Ang said the business had already begun incurring losses at Jelita Mall, and earlier earnings had gone towards covering those losses.

So why take another risk at Hougang Mall? Ang, who lives in Hougang, said he was initially confident about customer traffic there. He estimated that food establishments made up only about 30 per cent of the mall when Kokoyo moved in.

What he hadn’t anticipated was the extent of the mall’s refurbishment under an asset enhancement initiative. As more eateries gradually opened, competition for diners increased.

When 8days.sg first featured Ernest Ang in 2024, he was a fresh electronics engineering diploma holder who had stopped job hunting after six weeks to open his own eatery.

His grandmother contributed S$40,000 – half the initial investment – while he funded the rest with savings from part-time jobs. She also taught him her closely guarded Peranakan recipes, tasting and approving dishes before they went on the menu.

Her involvement extended beyond sharing recipes: when Kokoyo first opened, she made around 200 pieces of nonya kueh daily from home for the eatery. Ang shared that she has since stopped supplying them due to mobility issues.

Even the brand’s name was a tribute to her: Kokoyo came from the way she pronounced “coconut”, while the cartoon logo was also modelled after her.

His older brother Irwin, who has a degree in marketing, has also been involved in the business since the start. Hougang Mall is their only remaining outlet, and the brothers work at the stall daily.

For Ernest Ang, giving up the brand means letting go of a business he started to honour his grandmother’s cooking.

“It’s quite a pity. It’s like I failed, but I gave it my best shot, so that’s all that matters,” he shared.

His grandmother has taken the decision in her stride. According to Ang, she feels that if the business isn’t working, they should “stop the bleeding”.

For those hoping to visit before the closure, Kokoyo’s current menu centres on blue pea nasi lemak. Its Nasi Lemak Kosong (S$4.90) comes with a fried egg, ikan bilis, peanuts, cucumber, achar and sambal.

Other options include the Inchi Kabin Berempah Set (S$7.50), featuring spiced fried chicken, and Ayam Buah Keluak Set (S$7.50). There’s also a Fried Pork Belly Set (S$8.50) and Beef Rendang Nasi Lemak (S$11.50), featuring beef slow-cooked in a spiced coconut gravy.

À la carte dishes include Homemade Ngoh Hiang (S$5.80) and Nyonya Chap Chye (from S$4.80), while sharing sets for two start at S$20.50.

The Porridge Stall will take over the space after Kokoyo closes. Staying at the same spot is a practical decision – their existing lease runs until February 2027.

He settled on seafood porridge after visiting eateries in Malaysia in search of a new concept. He describes his version as Guangdong-style porridge, with a consistency somewhere between thick Cantonese congee and the looser Teochew style.

The base will be a broth made with chicken and pork bones. The opening date has yet to be confirmed.

Ang hopes the switch will help the brothers reach a wider customer base.

“I just want to go for a very mass market dish. I don’t want to go for something very niche like Nyonya food anymore,” he said. “I believe for many people in Singapore, porridge has a childhood connection.”

He also saw a gap in the mall’s food offerings. According to him, there are four nasi lemak sellers, including Kokoyo, but no dedicated porridge stall.

“I feel the success rate will be higher, and I think it’s worth a gamble rather than not doing anything about it,” he said.

If the new concept does well, the brothers are open to continuing the business beyond their current stall’s lease.

Would Ang consider getting a corporate job if things don’t work out?

“I will study the numbers and make a decision,” he mused. For now, he prefers to stay in F&B and may try his luck at another location.

Why stick with a notoriously punishing industry that hasn’t been kind to him?

“I feel like this shouldn’t be the end of my journey. I’m not the type of person that gives up easily,” he reasoned.

He added that building a brand is something both he and his brother enjoy.

And what do his dad, a technician, and mum, who works in HR, think of their sons’ rocky F&B ventures?

“My parents are pretty chill about it [despite our losses],” said Ang simply.

Kokoyo Nyonya Delights’ last day of operation is on Nov 2. #04-11 Hougang Mall, 90 Hougang Ave 10, Singapore 538766. Open Mon–Fri, 11.30am–8pm; Sat & Sun, 11am–8pm.

A simple liquid can stop cavities without drilling, major study confirms

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A cavity doesn't always have to mean drilling, injections, or an uncomfortable visit to the dentist. A major clinical trial has confirmed that a simple, inexpensive liquid can stop tooth decay in young children, potentially preventing painful infections and the need for dental surgery.

The treatment, called silver diamine fluoride (SDF), takes only seconds to apply to each affected tooth. Rather than removing damaged tooth material and placing a filling, a dentist uses a tiny sponge tipped applicator to paint the liquid directly onto the cavity. The process requires no drilling, injections, or sedation.

The findings could have enormous implications for childhood dental care. Every year, thousands of young children in the United States visit emergency departments because of untreated cavities. Emergency physicians generally cannot provide the dental procedures needed to address the underlying problem, leaving some children with ongoing pain and infections. Others ultimately need dental surgery under general anesthesia, which makes them completely unconscious during the procedure.

Now, researchers led by the University of Michigan have produced compelling evidence that a much simpler approach can stop many cavities from getting worse.

Major Clinical Trial Confirms Silver Diamine Fluoride Works

Published in JAMA Pediatrics, the Phase III clinical trial included 830 children younger than 6. Participants were recruited through dental clinics, pediatric medical practices, and Head Start and Early Head Start programs in Michigan, New York, and Iowa.

Head Start and Early Head Start are federally supported programs that provide early childhood education and related services to eligible families.

The results showed that a solution containing 38% silver diamine fluoride successfully stopped decay in more than half of the affected baby teeth when treatment was provided at six month intervals.

Dentists refer to this as arresting tooth decay, meaning the damaged area stops deteriorating. Unlike a traditional filling, the treatment does not replace lost tooth structure. Instead, it helps prevent existing decay from progressing.

SDF works through the combined effects of silver and fluoride. Silver helps combat bacteria associated with tooth decay, while fluoride helps strengthen the mineral structure of teeth and resist further damage.

"This is a very effective and safe treatment -- even in children as young as 1," said Margherita Fontana, professor of dentistry at the University of Michigan School of Dentistry and the study's lead investigator.

A Simple Treatment Could Help Millions Avoid Dental Pain

Tooth decay is the most common chronic disease among children, affecting more than 40% of American children. Although cavities may initially cause few noticeable symptoms, untreated decay can eventually lead to severe toothaches, infections, problems eating and sleeping, missed school days, and repeated medical appointments.

For young children, conventional dental treatment can also be particularly challenging. Some are too young to remain still throughout a procedure, while others experience significant fear or anxiety.

SDF offers an alternative for many of these patients because it can be applied quickly without the equipment and procedures required for a traditional filling.

Fontana said the benefits could extend well beyond young children. Older adults, people with developmental or physical disabilities, individuals with severe dental anxiety, and patients who have difficulty obtaining conventional dental care could also benefit.

There is, however, one important tradeoff. SDF permanently turns the decayed portion of a treated tooth dark, which can be especially noticeable on front teeth. The discoloration is primarily cosmetic, but it may influence whether patients and families choose the treatment.

For many people, avoiding pain, drilling, or more invasive procedures may outweigh that drawback.

Why This Decades-Old Treatment Could Soon Reach More Patients

Silver diamine fluoride is not a newly invented substance. Dentists have successfully used it to manage cavities in numerous countries for decades.

In the United States, the FDA approved SDF as a medical device for treating tooth sensitivity in 2014. Dentists have also used it off-label to stop cavities, meaning they apply an authorized product for a purpose beyond its original approved indication.

What has been missing is large-scale clinical evidence from U.S. populations establishing its safety and effectiveness for treating tooth decay, a key step toward obtaining FDA approval for that specific use.

The University of Michigan trial was designed to help fill that gap.

"If we want more children and families to benefit from this treatment, we need rigorous evidence showing both that it works and that it's safe. From a public health perspective, if we want broader implementation across the United States, including in medical settings, we need carefully collected data in U.S. populations, and we now have that," Fontana said.

The investigation began in 2018 and continued despite the major disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic.

The researchers believe stronger clinical evidence could make SDF more widely available, including in pediatric medical settings where children often receive care long before their first dental appointment.

"In medicine, clinicians want high-quality evidence before changing practice," Fontana said. "It is important to have data they can refer to because young children often see pediatricians years before they ever visit a dentist, broader acceptance could allow many more cavities to be treated while a referral to a dental home is successful, and before they become painful, infected or require surgery."

A dental home refers to an ongoing relationship with a dental care provider who can deliver preventive care, treatment, and follow-up services.

Expanding access to SDF could allow more children to receive early intervention before relatively manageable cavities develop into serious dental problems.

FDA Approval Could Expand Access to Cavity Treatment

The trial brought together researchers from the University of Michigan, New York University, the University of Iowa, and Indiana University. The National Institute of Dental and Craniofacial Research, part of the National Institutes of Health, provided more than $12 million in funding.

The product tested was Advantage Arrest 38% SDF, supplied by Elevate Oral Care.

The findings provide clinical trial evidence that the manufacturer can use in an application seeking FDA approval of SDF as a drug for arresting dental caries, the medical term for tooth decay.

Amr Moursi, a professor of pediatric dentistry at New York University College of Dentistry and a co-principal investigator, emphasized the potential importance of that regulatory change.

"Our results support FDA approval of SDF for managing arrest of tooth decay in young children. Removing SDF from off-label status would be an important innovation which could lead to increased utilization by providers, enhanced payments by insurers and more consistent product quality," said Moursi, a co-principal investigator on the study.

For some children, receiving another application of SDF every few months could be sufficient to keep a cavity under control until the affected baby tooth naturally falls out.

Adults could also benefit, either by using SDF to manage decay over a longer period or as a temporary solution until a filling or another restorative procedure becomes affordable or practical.

The treatment is not suitable for every cavity, and stopping decay does not necessarily eliminate the need for future dental work. Nevertheless, the findings suggest that a procedure requiring only seconds to perform could help many patients avoid more complicated treatment.

"For almost anyone, this can arrest the decay and stop the infection and the pain it causes," Fontana said. "This could benefit many people."

Materials provided by University of Michigan. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Lula beats Bolsonaro to win Brazil presidential election

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brazil President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva was elected to a fourth, nonconsecutive term in the nation's presidential election, according to early results released Sunday evening.

Why it matters: Lula's victory deals a setback to Brazil's right-wing populist movement and preserves a major counterweight to President Trump's influence in Latin America, with tensions over trade and tariffs threatening to persist.

  • Lula, the incumbent candidate, was facing Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of former Brazilian President Jair Bolsonaro. Flávio was widely seen as the heir to the nation's right-wing populist movement.

The latest: Lula thanked the Brazilian people after his election win, but Bolsonaro told reporters he would not accept the results and said they should be annulled, according to translations.

The big picture: The election has been shaped by the impact of U.S. tariffs, climate change and the Amazon rainforest, 60% of which is in Brazil.

  • Bolsonaro campaigned on public security and tackling corruption, presenting himself as a continuation of his father's political legacy, while Lula emphasized defending Brazilian sovereignty against U.S. pressure.
  • Yet substantive policy debate was limited, with both candidates focusing instead on corruption allegations levied against the opposing camp, wrote Matthew Taylor, a Brazil politics expert at American University.

Zoom in: After the U.S. imposed additional 25% tariffs on some Brazilian goods this summer, Lula accused Bolsonaro of being "Trump's candidate" and the U.S. president of wanting to "colonize" Brazil.

  • Lula has enacted climate policies aimed at reversing environmental deregulation under the previous administration, while Bolsonaro denied climate change and pledged to continue his father's political legacy.
  • Amazon deforestation has fallen 71% since 2022, the year before Lula returned to office, reaching its lowest annual level on record in 2026. Yet Lula's environmental record is complicated by his support for expanded oil development.
  • Lula defeated Jair Bolsonaro in the previous election. The elder Bolsonaro is now serving a 27-year prison sentence for plotting a coup to overturn the 2022 election, though he remains under house arrest due to health problems.

Go deeper: Brazil's markets rally after Bolsonaro's first-round lead

Editor's note: This story has been updated with comment from the presidential candidates.

Two storms slam northern Europe, cut power to 150,000 homes

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Storms Boelo and Austen kill a man in Scotland and cut power to thousands of homes in the UK, France and Germany.

A pair of storms battering northern Europe have killed one man, cut power to more than 150,000 homes, and disrupted flights and phone networks.

More than 82,000 homes in the United Kingdom, nearly 60,000 in France, and at least 10,000 in Germany had no electricity on Sunday after storms Boelo and Austen passed through the region within 24 hours, according to authorities in the countries.

A falling tree killed a man, 60, in southwestern Scotland on Sunday, while another falling tree injured two women in central Scotland, the Press Association reported.

Winds of up to 135 km/h (84 mph) were recorded in the Argyll and Bute regions of western Scotland. Trees and power cables brought down by the storm blocked roads and rail lines across Scotland and northern England.

In Brighton, on England’s southern coast, Austen blew away an unused pier on Friday.

Authorities have warned of flood risk in regions of both Scotland and England.

The Met Office, the UK’s national forecaster, warned that Boelo would bring a danger to life from flying debris and crashing waves and said people should stay indoors as much as possible.

Across the English Channel, Europe battled its first big storm of the autumn, with strong winds inflicting widespread power cuts.

French energy company Enedis said that at Austen’s peak on Saturday, some 288,000 homes had no electricity. Some 59,000 remained without power at midday Sunday, mainly in Normandy and Brittany.

A marathon runner in the midst of a race in northern France was lightly injured by a tree knocked over in strong winds.

In Germany, Austen cut power to 10,000 homes in and around the southwestern town of Freiburg-Breisgau on Sunday. The town’s main hospital said it had to limit service to emergency cases because of the blackout, which also cut the mobile phone network.

German police said two people were seriously hurt on Saturday by scaffolding blown away by Austen’s strong winds at a market in the town of Nastatten.

The storms also hit the Netherlands and Belgium, where falling trees injured four people in Brussels on Saturday.

In the Netherlands, north of the island of Schiermonnikoog, three crew members on board a freight ship were injured in the storms, according to the country’s coastguard. Two of them were winched off and taken by helicopter to a hospital on the mainland.

The storms caused hundreds of flights to be cancelled or diverted across northern Europe, including at Amsterdam’s Schiphol airport, one of the continent’s busiest hubs.

Experts have warned of more extreme weather events due to climate change.

Thomas Markle's alcoholic ex-wife, the wayward son he 'cut off financially' - and how the grubbiest royal scandal in history unfolded: GUY ADAMS

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published: 01:55 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 02:26 GMT, 26 October 2026

Shortly after his daughter Meghan married Prince Harry, Thomas Markle was the subject of a cover story on America's most notorious supermarket tabloid, The National Enquirer.

Published in October 2018, it gave a compelling insight into why his absence from that summer's royal wedding might, in the great scheme of things, turn out to have been a blessing in disguise.

The article was based on a tape recording of the retired TV lighting director discussing his time in Hollywood, where he'd won three Emmy awards.

Two excerpts stood out. In one, Markle brazenly talked about having dabbled in Class A drugs, confessing: 'In the industry I worked in back in the 70s and 80s there was cocaine around a lot. And I'm sure that I had an occasional sniff of cocaine.'

In the second, he gave a bizarrely equivocal answer when asked if he'd ever consorted with prostitutes in South-East Asia, where he'd holidayed on a number of occasions.

'Have I ever paid for a sex worker? Uh, there was a Playboy Club in the top floor of a hotel in the Philippines [but] I never paid for sex. I would go upstairs and enjoy myself in the Playboy Club, but… not for sex. I didn't get sex out of it,' Markle said.

But at the weekend he admitted to the Mail on Sunday that he had paid for sex in the Philippines.

The tape recording turned out to have been secretly made by Markle's son, Thomas Jr.

The Duchess of Sussex has been estranged from her father, Thomas Markle Sr, 82, since 2018. Pictured: The couple together on the day of Meghan's marriage to film producer Trevor Engelson in 2011

Roslyn Loveless, the first wife of Meghan Markle's estranged father Thomas, pictured outside her home in Albuquerque, New Mexico

Meghan Markle's half-brother Thomas Markle Jnr pictured hand-in-hand with a woman outside the apartment building where he lived with his father in the Philippines on May 10, 2025

In a separate interview, around the same time, he discussed his father's love life, claiming that in between divorcing his mother, Roslyn Loveless, in 1975, and marrying Meghan's mother, Doria Ragland, in 1979, he'd enjoyed a lengthy relationship with a lady from the Philippines named Marty.

'There's a certain type of woman he likes. He likes Asian women a lot,' said Thomas Jr, before adding enigmatically: 'He likes to go on vacation to Thailand, Vietnam. He likes their culture a lot.'

Fast-forward eight years and Meghan's father, now 82, is once more the subject of a toe-curling sex scandal. The principal setting is, again, the Philippines.

And the most explosive details are, as previously, being made public by Thomas Jr, one of two children from whom he appears to be irreparably estranged.

The whole thing kicked off at exactly 10.04pm on Thursday, when it emerged that Filipino police were investigating Thomas Sr over a huge stash of child sex images that had allegedly been found at his home in Cebu, a tourist island some 350 miles south-east of the capital Manila.

It had purportedly been discovered by Thomas Jr, who told The Sun newspaper that he'd found 'hundreds if not thousands' of images in four cardboard boxes that had been shipped there from Rosarito in Mexico, where father and son had lived before moving out to the Philippines for a 'fresh start' in January 2025. 'When I found this material, I was horrified and I had no option but to report my dad,' he said.

'I have supported, cared for him and helped him for years – but that stops now. I won't speak to my father ever again unless I'm called to give evidence against him.'

Thomas Jr, 60, claims to have come across the material in July, while searching for a mobile phone handset he could give to the father of his fiancee, a 28-year-old Filipino woman named Rose.

Mr Markle faces child sex allegations which he denies and told The Mail on Sunday he was 'not buying' the idea that his first wife, who died two years ago, wrote the letter to the Met Police in early 2019

He says he charged up an old LG device, which had previously belonged to his Dad, only for explicit video of 'naked young girls' to flash up on the screen.

Other images, of what The Sun described as 'victims appearing to be aged no older than ten or 11,' were discovered on a Lenovo ThinkPad laptop.

There were also alleged to be explicit DVDs and photographs, some of them secreted in the pages of a couple of coffee table books that belonged to Thomas Sr. One was called Lewis Carroll Photographs Of Children: Four Nude Studies, the other Hollywood Lolitas: The Nymphet Syndrome In The Movies.

According to Thomas Jr, he promptly reported the discovery of the material to the FBI, hoping they would launch an investigation into his father.

Thomas Sr had moved back to the US in May after suffering serious health difficulties, including a stroke and a blood clot, which resulted in the amputation of his left leg below the knee.

Yet three months passed, without an FBI probe ensuing. So Thomas Jr instead decided to report the matter to police in the Philippines.

On Friday morning, he transported the four cardboard boxes to the police headquarters in Cebu City in a Toyota HiAce people-carrier under the watchful eyes of a Sun reporter.

Thomas Jr, who had until now sided with his father in various public spats with Meghan, then used the newspaper to issue a mea culpa to his estranged half-sister, saying yesterday: 'I'm so, so sorry… I now know I have been protecting the wrong person for the past nine years.'

The Duchess of Sussex had by then set a second hare running. On Friday, she issued a statement declaring herself 'deeply distressed at the news that has come to light in the last 24 hours'.

Thomas Sr recovering in a hospital bed after surgery in the Philippines last December

Meghan added that in 2018, the Metropolitan Police had shared with her details of a letter which 'made troubling and disturbing allegations against my father'.

She was 'given assurances that these were being investigated', and subsequently decided to prevent her children from having contact with their grandfather, in order 'to protect them'.

It later emerged, the letter had in fact been sent in early 2019, shortly after The National Enquirer's revelations about Thomas Sr's previous exploits in an 'X-rated jiggle joint' in the Philippines. The letter's author was Thomas Sr's alcoholic ex-wife Roslyn Loveless, who died in 2024.

She alleged that he had admitted going to the Philippines and Thailand to abuse children, according to sources who have seen the letter's contents.

The Met is now 'reviewing its handling of the matter' and may yet refer itself to the police watchdog.

One odd wrinkle is that the letter seems to have ended up not with officers specialising in child sex abuse but with the Force's 'Fixated Threat Assessment Centre', a unit frequently referred to as its 'stalker and obsessive unit', which deals with people who threaten royals, celebrities and other public figures.

It's unclear whether this means officers concluded that claims made by Ms Loveless suggested she was a potential threat to Meghan, or whether they were instead concerned about Thomas Sr. Either way, an informed source said last night that the Met would almost certainly have had no jurisdiction to carry out further inquiries since Mr Markle was not in the UK and neither was his alleged offending nor the purported victims.

Another tricky factor for the law enforcement agencies might be the fact that the age of consent in the Philippines was only upgraded to 16 in 2022. Prior to that point it was just 12.

Quite how seriously the Duchess of Sussex took the 2019 letter from Ms Loveless is, at this stage, unclear. In February 2019, shortly after it arrived, Meghan authorised five friends to brief her favourite outlet, People magazine, about her estrangement from Thomas Sr. The resulting article suggested the Duchess wished to repair the relationship.

Later, in a 2022 Netflix documentary, Meghan declared that she had been 'a daddy's girl my whole life'. She only appears to have categorically cut him off in late 2025, when Thomas Sr was gravely ill in hospital in the Philippines.

At that point, a representative was dispatched to hand-deliver a letter in a scene witnessed by the Mail's Caroline Graham.

Prince Harry and Meghan Markle at The Community Recording Studio in Nottingham earlier this month, their first official engagement since returning to the UK

None of which is, of course, to suggest that the Duchess is anything but a victim of this week's appalling developments. One might argue, in fact, she is the only member of her troubled American family who emerges from recent developments with her reputation enhanced.

Thomas Jr's motives are, to this end, very much open to question.

A twice-divorced window-fitter, with two grown-up sons (one is a marijuana farmer in Oregon, where the activity is legal), his chaotic personal CV includes an arrest for allegedly putting a gun to a girlfriend's head during a drunken row (charges were filed in 2017 but later dismissed after they reconciled) and a court appearance for drink-driving.

He also appeared on Australia's version of Celebrity Big Brother in 2022, alleging that he and Meghan had once been close, but that 'money changed her' after she achieved fame.

In recent years, the 60-year-old has been almost entirely financially supported by his father. The duo moved out to the Philippines together in January 2025, and Thomas Jr remains there.

Thomas Sr, for his part, returned to the US in May to recuperate from the health crisis that saw his leg amputated.

He left behind his most recent girlfriend Rio Canedo, a local nurse who he'd met when she treated him in hospital.

At 46, she is three and a half decades his junior.

Meghan's father initially spent time in Pennsylvania but has lately been in a nursing home in Lakeland, Florida, where he is being supported by Samantha Markle, the Duchess's somewhat outspoken second half‑sibling, who is the eldest child of Ms Loveless.

Samantha, a former actress and model who has given a number of unflattering interviews about Meghan over the years, remains very much in her father's camp.

She told the Daily Mail at the weekend that her brother's allegations about their father are 'disgusting', saying they have 'no merit in them whatsoever'.

She described Thomas Jr as a 'liar' who is fabricating the claims to make cash.

Furthermore, she has alleged that his original complaint to the FBI in July followed a family row over money: shortly after returning to the US in May, Thomas Sr changed passwords to a bank account his son had access to.

'My brother first came out with these lies days after the cash from my Dad was cut off. He has used Dad as a cash cow for years, and once the money stopped he made up the most outrageous lies he could to make money from the media,' was how Samantha put it.

She further described her brother as a 'delusional alcoholic' and said the devices said to contain illicit material had been untouched by her father since he'd arrived in the Philippines, where they had been kept in storage.

'Dad had no access to those computers for two years. Who knows who could have had access to them? Anyone could have set Dad up. There is no merit in these disgusting allegations whatsoever and my brother is disgusting for making them up.'

In remarks which may do little to improve her father's reputation in the court of public opinion, Samantha added that one of the photographs at the centre of the investigation, which purportedly shows her father on a bed with a young-looking Asian woman, 'may be one of his girlfriends'.

She went on: 'Dad dated a wonderful Japanese woman called Carolyn who I loved. She was in her 30s but she looked like a teenager. Asian women have great skin and are small and petite. They look much younger than they are.

'Dad had another girlfriend called Gail. She was Asian and gorgeous but looked very young.'

And what of Thomas Sr? He is purportedly in severe ill health, but has declared himself prepared to do 'whatever it takes' to clear his name, saying that the computers and phones at the centre of this week's events haven't been in his possession for more than two years so: 'If there is anything bad on there it was put there by someone else.'

As we have seen in an interview with the Mail on Sunday, he admitted that when he was single he travelled to the Philippines at least twice and 'slept with prostitutes', adding, perhaps ill-advisedly, that: 'I like Asian women but that is not a crime'.

Is 'ferritin face' a real thing - and can you really spot low iron from looks?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lucy says she noticed changes in her skin and hair alongside symptoms including fatigue and hair loss when her ferritin levels were low

"Do you have ferritin face?" a young woman asks on TikTok, pointing to dark circles, washed-out skin and brittle hair, alongside before-and-after style photographs. The content creator puts these features down to her iron levels.

It is one of a growing number of videos and posts on TikTok and Instagram claiming that low ferritin - a protein which stores iron in the body - can leave visible clues on your face.

Users list pale or grey-looking skin, dark under-eye circles, dry or cracked lips, hair shedding and weak nails, while others share portraits claiming stark visual differences.

The phrase "ferritin face" has spread widely across social media, with videos attracting millions of views.

But doctors say appearance alone cannot diagnose iron deficiency.

"Ferritin face is not a medical diagnosis," said GP Dr Aziza Sesay, who is based in the West Midlands. "However, it may be a clue to tell us that something is going on."

Pale skin, hair shedding or brittle nails can accompany low iron, but can also be signs of other conditions, she told BBC News.

Your ferritin levels show how good your body's iron stores are, while haemoglobin is the protein in red blood cells which carries oxygen. When haemoglobin is low, the blood cannot carry as much oxygen - a condition known as anaemia.

But iron stores can become depleted before that happens, meaning someone can be iron-deficient and have symptoms without being anaemic.

Can you tell your ferritin levels from your face?

"You've got no stored iron but you're maintaining your haemoglobin. That's the big disconnect," said consultant haematologist Dr Drew Provan.

Dr Sue Pavord, also a consultant haematologist, said iron deficiency is common and often under-diagnosed in women.

UK guidelines from the British Society of Gastroenterology say ferritin below 15 micrograms per litre indicates absent iron stores, while below 30 generally indicates low iron stores.

Social media users have shared posts and videos claiming that low ferritin can cause visible changes to the face

Lucy M, 32, who posts about hair loss as @marvluceskin on Instagram and TikTok, said "ferritin face" seemed to describe changes she had noticed in herself.

In 2021 she developed heart palpitations, fatigue and hair loss. After being told her blood tests were normal and being diagnosed with generalised anxiety disorder, a later test showed her ferritin was 12 micrograms per litre.

Lucy said she looked "grey" and her hair had become dull and brittle.

She shared photographs with the BBC from when her ferritin was low, compared to when it had recovered, and said the difference between how she felt and looked was "night and day".

Videos about "ferritin face" often encourage viewers to look for a combination of physical signs - from dark circles and pale skin to dry lips, thinning hair and brittle nails - and compare them with their own appearance.

"You're not ugly, your ferritin is low", one creator says, pointing to the camera.

Provan said it was "impossible" to determine someone's ferritin levels from how their face looks.

People who are significantly anaemic may look pale, he explained, while fatigue can contribute to dark circles, but these visual signs are "subjective and unreliable".

In people with darker skin, changes in skin colour may be less obvious, Sesay added, meaning someone looking specifically for the signs described online could miss iron deficiency.

Doctors say appearance alone cannot tell you whether your ferritin is low, and that iron deficiency should be confirmed with a blood test

For doctors, symptoms and how someone feels are more useful than appearance.

Blood tests matter not only to confirm iron deficiency, Provan and Sesay said, but to establish the cause and guide treatment.

Both said treatment should also address the cause. Heavy periods, low dietary iron, gastrointestinal blood loss and problems absorbing iron through the gut can all play a role.

Iron deficiency is commonly treated with oral iron. Pavord said iron tablets are best taken on an empty stomach, ideally first thing in the morning, and advised avoiding tea or coffee around the time they are taken because they can inhibit iron absorption.

Iron can also be obtained naturally from foods including meat, beans, lentils, leafy green vegetables and fortified cereals.

Taking iron supplements without a diagnosis can be harmful, while self-treating could delay the investigation of an underlying problem.

Lucy said social media has nevertheless helped women take symptoms more seriously, and has given some the confidence to ask questions when something feels wrong.

"There are so many women saying, 'Oh my gosh, this is me," she said, adding that she is not a doctor and presents her content as her own experience.

For her, doing so has been empowering - giving women information with which to advocate for themselves.

Sesay called social media a "double-edged sword" - it can encourage people to seek help, but can also lead them to incorrectly diagnose themselves from a short video.

For Lucy, the useful part is not diagnosing yourself from your appearance, but knowing when something doesn't feel right, and when to ask questions.

If you'd like to include more iron in your diet, try some of these BBC Food recipes.

Sharon Osbourne: 'I visit Ozzy's grave every day'

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sharon Osbourne spoke to BBC News as the family prepares to release a film about Ozzy's final concert with Black Sabbath

It's been 15 months since Ozzy Osbourne died, at the age of 76, at his home in Buckinghamshire.

Although he had suffered from health problems for years, including Parkinson's disease and a fractured vertebra, his death was a shock.

Less than three weeks beforehand, he had been on stage in Birmingham, playing a triumphant farewell concert.

"He was just so, so happy," recalls his wife Sharon.

"He was online, going through the reviews and saying, 'There's something terribly wrong', and I'm like, 'Why?'

"He goes, 'I didn't get slagged by anybody. Why are people being nice to me?'

On the morning he died, Ozzy had woken up early and gone downstairs for his usual morning workout. Shortly afterwards, he had a heart attack. An air ambulance was called and paramedics tried to save him. But it was too late.

"We'd spoken in the night. Ozzy had woken in the night and he asked me to kiss him twice, and I did.

"And then he said, 'Hold me, hold me tight,' and I did.

"And then the next thing I know, it's early in the morning, and someone comes rushing into the room saying, 'Come down now'.

Sharon and Ozzy Osbourne, pictured in 2017 after renewing their wedding vows

"You've lost the love of your life, so you don't just wake up and go, 'I'm fine now. I'm cured'.

"You never get over it. But you learn to live with it, and that's what I'm trying to do. So you either sit in a room and don't come out, or you accept it, befriend it, and that's it."

The affection among Ozzy's fans was apparent before the singer's funeral. Thousands of people lined the streets of Birmingham, laying floral tributes and singing Black Sabbath songs as Ozzy's body was transported through his home city in a hearse topped by a purple floral tribute in the shape of a cross.

Sharon says he would have been mystified by the outpouring of love.

"He'd have been like, 'What's the matter with you guys? Go home!'" she says.

But for the family, the public response was a huge source of comfort.

Daughter Kelly said last year: "I never could have imagined it to be as helpful as it has been to know that we're not alone in our grief and our sadness, and that the rest of the world loved him as much as we did."

Sharon says the fans' cards and messages were all collected, sorted and photographed; while the flowers were composted for the family garden, where Ozzy is buried.

"There was no way I could put Ozzy in a public graveyard," she says.

"I've seen what happens to other people in the public eye. There's no peace because people want to come and they want to pay their respects. That's an ongoing thing, and I wanted Ozzy to rest and just be."

"Of course, every day. I can see him from my window."

She still talks to him regularly, she says. Asked what she discusses, she puts a finger to her lips and gives a gentle "shh".

Ozzy's family - including children Jack and Kelly - stopped to read the tributes during his funeral procession last July

Ozzy and Sharon's relationship spanned more than 50 years.

They met in 1970 and married in 1982. Their time together could be turbulent - he was arrested for attempting to murder her in 1989 (she dropped the charges), and they split briefly in 2016 amid rumours of infidelity - but the couple maintained that they were soulmates.

"It was an unusual relationship because we had such highs and such terrible lows," she admits. But at the end of his life, "he knew" how much he was loved.

You can see proof of that love - from his family, his fans and his bandmates â in a new documentary about his final gig at Birmingham's Villa Park last year, when he reunited with heavy rock legends Black Sabbath.

Titled Back To The Beginning, it features more than 25 of the songs played at that star-studded concert, including Ozzy's solo set and Sabbath's entire performance.

In backstage footage, fellow A-list performers discuss the star's influence in awestruck tones.

"If there was no Sabbath, there's no Metallica," says the band's frontman James Hetfield. "It's as simple as that."

Yungblud, whose version of Changes was one of the concert's emotional high points, talks about how, as a kid who didn't fit in, Ozzy changed his life.

"The fact he was celebrated for his madness and his individuality gave me hope."

Watch: Black Sabbath blast out Iron Man at final gig

Sharon says the concert was Ozzy's final wish: the one thing that would make him happy after years of deteriorating health.

Six months before the show, Ozzy fell and fractured his vertebrae. In recovery, he caught pneumonia three times. After surgery on his back, he caught sepsis and nearly died.

Two weeks before the show, doctors urged him to call it off.

"They came to see us at the house and they said, 'You can't do this. We're advising you not to do this. Your heart's too weak'," Sharon recalls.

But the singer was determined to see it through. In the film, it's hard to believe he's so ill. Although he performs seated on a throne, his eyes are full of life and his voice is powerful. As he looks out at the crowd, he seems elated.

Backstage, Sharon admits, "the whole family was terrified". But something changed when Ozzy entered the stadium.

"He was sat in his dressing room, watching everybody on a screen performing, and he was like, 'I just can't believe they're all here'.

"It's amazing what support and love and determination can do for you, because [before the show] he was somebody that couldn't breathe. He had a lung condition. He had a heart condition. He had terrible, terrible agony from his spine. He had blood clots and he had Parkinson's.

"Some days just to even talk was an effort, let alone sing. And I don't know how he did it, but he did it.

"And you saw it in his eyes, in his face. It just made him feel like he was alive again."

Ozzy is survived by Sharon, their three children (left-right: Kelly, Aimee and Jack) and three children from his previous marriage

The effect was so galvanising that, when he came off stage, Ozzy declared he wanted to go on tour.

"He was raring to go," Sharon laughs. "He was like, 'I can do this, and the fans don't mind if I'm sat down'."

It's an incredible and rare thing, I suggest, to be able to grant your partner their last wish.

Ozzy may have suffered immense pain in his final years, but he was able to say goodbye in style.

"To quote one of his songs, he was flying high again," Sharon says.

"He was just floating for those last 17 days. It was great. It was joyous."

JD Vance sees Bengals making 'a run' but that's understandable because he's a fan, especially of Ja'Marr Chase

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Vice President JD Vance is greeted by Cincinnati Bengals head coach Zac Taylor outside of the team's locker room while visiting a recent practice.

We know by now that Vice President JD Vance is a big fan of the Cincinnati Bengals so, of course, he had something to celebrate on Sunday when his team beat the Baltimore Ravens.

Vance did not attend Sunday in Baltimore, but the Bengals punished the Ravens by scoring all their 35 points in the second half, mostly thanks to four touchdown passes by quarterback Joe Burrow.

JD VANCE VISITS CINCINNATI BENGALS PRACTICE AND QUARTERBACK JOE BURROW IS CONVINCED IT WAS MOSTLY ABOUT HIM

JD Vance and Dexter Lawrence chat at Cincinnati Bengals practice. (X/@VPRapidDirector)

It was the type of performance from the Bengals that everyone expected from a potential Super Bowl contender and particularly the offense.

And that had Vance feeling pretty good about his club.

"Huge win for Cincinnati," Vance posted on X.

Can you imagine? The second-most powerful man in the country posted on X about his NFL team. Let that sink in a second.

Anyway, the Veep continued ... "It's early, but I think the Bengals are going to make a run. Who Dey!"

The Bengals have won two consecutive games, but they do indeed need that run Vance is predicting.

Cincinnati Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow (9) throws a pass against Baltimore Ravens linebacker Kyle Van Noy (53) during the second half at M&T Bank Stadium in Baltimore, Md., on Nov. 27, 2025. (Mitch Stringer/Imagn Images)

They've been a roller coaster of a team, winning two in a row after losing two in a row after winning two in a row. If they continue that pattern, you know what comes next — some frustrating football.

But Vance, the fan, apparently doesn't buy that.

Speaking of being a fan, Vance loves Bengals wide receiver Ja'Marr Chase.

Cincinnati Bengals wide receiver Ja'Marr Chase catches a touchdown pass during the first half of an NFL game against the Houston Texans in Houston on Sunday, Sept. 20, 2026. (Ashley Landis)

During Vance's visit to the Bengals training facility last week, Chase apparently noticed that Vance's six-year-old son, Vivek, was holding Bengals football gloves.

Celebrations in Serbia after veteran ruler Vučić loses election to student-led protest movement

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Watch: Joy and disbelief on Serbian streets as VuÄiÄ concedes election defeat

Serbia's long-standing leader Aleksandar VuÄiÄ has admitted defeat to the student-led protest movement in snap parliamentary elections.

"We still do not have final results... it is clear that we have lost these elections," the right-wing populist leader said as he conceded late on Sunday.

Preliminary figures from Serbia's election commission indicated a record high turnout, which commentators had suggested would favour the student movement. Celebrations erupted after VuÄiÄ conceded.

VuÄiÄ called the snap elections after two years of protests triggered by the collapse of a train station canopy in the city of Novi Sad, which killed 16 and was seen as emblematic of corruption and negligence.

VuÄiÄ said early results made it "clear" that his party had lost

VuÄiÄ had spent two terms as president and could not seek a third term, so was leading his Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) with the aim of returning to the post of prime minister, which he left in 2017.

The Student List's lead candidate Ilija SrdanoviÄ told BBC Serbian: "This will be a free state."

He said the anti-corruption movement, which had attracted anti-government voters across Serbia, hoped to "free the country from tyranny".

There were celebrations on the streets of the city, with people dancing and chanting "victory", cars honking, and fireworks set off. "Serbia is free," one student told the BBC. "It's a miracle."

During a celebration speech, Student List candidate Radomir Saicic said Serbians had shown "no obstacle was greater than their will", Reuters reported. "No force can forever overpower a people who have decided to take their destiny into their own hands," he added.

Amid scenes of people dancing in the streets Belgrade, Selena Lebovic said the result was "really fabulous".

"We are all crying, and what to say. We couldn't believe that it would be so easy," she told Reuters.

An aerial photo shows the size of the celebrations by Students' List supporters at Slavija square in Belgrade

VuÄiÄ made his announcement at the headquarters of his Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) in the capital Belgrade.

The centre of Novi Sad - the northern city that triggered the student-led protests - was crowded. "Everything is going to get better now," David VasiÄ, one of those celebrating, told the BBC Serbian.

The Student List - which has no obvious leaders and whose members are not used to politics - had attracted anti-government voters across Serbia.

It decided to field candidates to the 250-member parliament from across Serbian society including doctors, actors, academics and sports personalities.

Ilija SrdanoviÄ, Student List's leading candidate, pictured during celebrations late on Sunday

The anti-corruption movement campaigned on a manifesto that included purging officials who broke rules cracking down on protesters, stopping party loyalists from leading public enterprises, and freeing the public broadcaster of political control.

VuÄiÄ and the SNS party have repeatedly denied corruption.

VuÄiÄ, who backs Serbia's candidacy for the European Union while refusing to impose sanctions on Russia over its full-scale invasion of Ukraine, had pledged to increase pensions and public sector salaries, and investment in state healthcare.

Preliminary figures provided by Serbia's election commission suggested a record high turnout, with 64.9% having cast their vote by 18:00 local time (17:00 GMT), two hours before polls were set to close.

Earlier in the day, long queues were seen outside polling stations in cities and towns across Serbia.

This was also the case outside booths in a number of countries with sizeable Serbian communities - including Slovenia's capital Ljubljana, Germany's Munich, and Los Angeles and Chicago in the US.

In France, Serbian voters waited in line in heavy rain in Paris.

The election was held amid high tensions across Serbia, with police sealing off streets around the Republic Electoral Commission (RIK) in Belgrade.

Gina Hinojosa says there's "deep disappointment" in both parties

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Updated on: October 25, 2026 / 9:03 PM EDT / CBS News

Washington — Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa, the Democrat running for governor in Texas, said on Sunday that there's "deep disappointment in both political parties" in the Lone Star State, prompting a new path forward that she argued is "post-partisan in some ways."

"Right now nobody wants politics as usual. Most of us see that it is broken," Hinojosa said on "Face the Nation."

Hinojosa, 52, is taking on Gov. Greg Abbott in Texas. A civil rights and labor lawyer who's represented parts of Austin in the Texas House since 2017, Hinojosa would be the first Democratic governor and the first woman to lead the state in the governor's mansion in three decades. A recent CBS News poll had Abbott leading Hinojosa, 52% to 48%.

But in a state like Texas, which has more registered Republican voters than Democrats, Hinojosa will need support from both sides of the aisle to pull off a victory. She argued that the deep disappointment she described in both parties has made Texans forge "a new way forward."

"There is bipartisan anger about what Greg Abbott has done to our public schools," she said. "Saving Texas public schools is a key part of my agenda, and that's why this campaign has resonated amongst Texans."

Hinojosa said her agenda "is not a Democratic agenda," but rather a populist one aimed at delivering for Texans. She cited healthcare access for children and combating high insurance premiums as key focus areas.

She also pointed to data centers as an issue where Texans can find agreement. She said, "Nobody's pushing back against the data centers harder than MAGA because these data centers are mostly moving into our rural communities that are unincorporated."

"This part of Texas is being taken advantage of by these data centers, and the people of Texas and rural Texas are being taken advantage of," she said. "There are big water concerns, there are big electricity cost concerns, property rights concerns, and so we need to visit all of it and make sure Texans are protected, and first and foremost have a seat at the table."

The comments come as Hinojosa has filed a lawsuit to obtain emails between Abbott and Elon Musk, who's given millions to Abbott's campaign. She said, "We need to know what exactly is going on," pointing to the relationship between Abbott and Musk, the CEO of Texas-based Tesla and SpaceX.

"Governor Abbott is focused on putting Texas on a path to becoming number one in education, codifying his data center guardrails, eliminating sales tax incentives for data centers, and lowering costs for hardworking Texas families," Abbott's press secretary, Eduardo Leal, told CBS News in a statement. "Under Governor Abbott's leadership, Texas public education funding is at an all-time high and average teacher pay has risen by over 30%."

"While Gina Hinojosa continues to run from her disastrous record, Governor Abbott will continue crisscrossing the state to talk directly to voters and to keep Texas, Texas," he added.

Hinojosa expressed optimism about her chances in next week's election, despite Texas being a reliably red state for over 30 years.

"Turnout is high, enthusiasm is high on our side, so we feel really good about where we are," she said.

At 42, I finally got pregnant via an egg donor. Then my husband told me he didn't want the baby. What he did next cut even deeper

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Earlier this year I fulfilled a lifetime ambition, owning my very own campervan. Forgive me if I sound like a cliche, as a single, childless woman aged 51, with two sausage dogs for company. I’ll often tie my paddleboard to the roof and take off at the weekend.

And why shouldn’t I? This is not the life necessarily I planned for myself, but I’m making the most of it.

A decade ago I spent over a year trying to conceive naturally, suffering a devastating miscarriage in the process. When I then became pregnant via donor egg IVF, my husband freaked out at the idea of a child that wasn’t all ‘ours’, forcing me to undergo a termination. And, even after all that, he still dumped me, later settling down with a young, fertile woman who could have his child ‘as nature intended’.

I like to think I’m over it and embracing the second part of my life, but there are times when I think of my ex and the howling injustice. He can keep on reproducing until his final breath and yet women have a fertility window of 20 years (if we’re lucky). In that time, if we’re sensible, we try to ensure we’re in a loving relationship and in decent enough financial circumstances to raise a child.

I spent my 20s and 30s working in the antiques industry. Fluent in Spanish and Italian, my job took me all over Europe and I embraced every aspect of it, working all hours with no intention of settling down. I was far too busy enjoying life and all it had to offer.

Before I knew it I was 40 and still single. While I had enjoyed three semi-serious relationships, I knew I’d never met The One. On a whim I bought a holiday home in the Dolomites, a part of northern Italy I’d come to know well. It was there in 2015 that I met Mateo, who, like me, was in the antiques business.

What started as a fling grew serious pretty quickly. We fell in love. Within a year I found myself regarded as part of his family and the community, so I left my home in West Sussex to move lock, stock and barrel to Italy.

I was ten years older than Mateo, but to look at us as a couple you’d never know – I’ve always looked after myself (with a bit of tweakment help) and we shared the same zest for life and sense of humour. The age difference simply never seemed to bother him. So when Mateo asked me to marry him, I didn’t hesitate in saying yes. I knew he wanted to become a father and I wanted to become a mum.

'There are times when I think of my ex and the howling injustice. He can keep on reproducing until his final breath', writes Carrie Darbyshire

I was 42 and two months pregnant when we got married in 2017, three months after he’d proposed. A fortnight after exchanging vows, I miscarried. We hadn’t told anyone about the pregnancy and so we faced the grief alone. My gynaecologist assured me there was no reason to worry, now my body knew what to do, we should try again.

Mateo rallied around, looking after me and we had lots of fun trying. Every weekend we’d have lunch with his huge family or drinks with his wide circle of friends.

Occasionally I’d be asked if there was any ‘news’; I’d assure people we were trying and close down the conversation.

A year passed without a positive pregnancy test. By then I was about to celebrate my 43rd birthday so I booked another visit to the gynaecologist. When hormone tests showed my eggs weren’t viable, she mentioned that we should think about assisted fertility treatment with a donor egg.

While this option is common in many countries, it is less so in Italy and so she suggested we research clinics in Spain, where the procedure was widely available. When I explained this to Mateo, he was unsure at first but, after sleeping on it, assured me he was on board.

Within days I’d found a clinic with a glowing reputation. All of the private Facebook groups I was in said it was expensive – yes – but would guarantee results. It was €7,000 (£5,800) for each attempt. Fortunately, I had a small inheritance from my grandmother. Throughout it all, Mateo assured me that he was committed to the treatment we were signing up for.

The private Spanish clinic redefined the meaning of luxury. It was set in a renovated farmhouse, and the walls in the lobby were covered with testimonials to the clinic’s success and beautifully framed, handwritten thank-you notes. Our gynaecologist rattled off the patter about Spanish law, anonymity and the checks involved. We’d never know the identity of our donor and neither would our child. We went through the characteristics of the sort of donor we wanted – ideally someone who matched my hair colour, eye colour, height. Before we left, Mateo gave a sperm sample and we were told to wait to hear about a match.

The matching process happened very quickly. While the donor started to take drugs to stimulate her egg production, I was on medication to ensure my body would be ready to accept the fertilised embryos once her eggs had been removed and fertilised using my husband’s sperm.

All went to plan, resulting in four embryos of sufficient quality. The plan was to transfer two in our first round, and freeze two for use later. I’d been sent images of the embryos – admittedly at that point just round blobs – and I spent hours looking at the pictures. Whether fertilised in a Petri dish or in my womb, they were my babies. I’d secretly given them names (Sofia and Valentina) and talked to them every day. I was going to become a mum. I just knew it.

We travelled to Spain for the transfer, which went seamlessly and I left feeling certain that one day we’d be back for the other two.

During the two-week wait to see if the transfer had been successful, I’d sing to the embryos in my womb, gently willing them to implant and grow. Mateo wasn’t as excited as me, but I put that down to him being preoccupied with his work, running his family’s busy antiques restoration company.

'When I became pregnant via donor egg IVF, my husband freaked out at the idea of a child that wasn’t all "ours", forcing me to undergo a termination'

Even before we got the positive pregnancy test, I’d had an inkling we were expecting. My breasts felt heavier and I developed a metallic taste in my mouth. The first eight weeks were tough, I felt nauseous for most of the time but I stayed focused on me and not me and Mateo. Is that where I went wrong? I do wonder.

We paid for a private eight-week scan, and I burst into tears on hearing the sound of a heartbeat – I just knew it was Sofia who had implanted and taken. Mateo looked bewildered. Mentally I dismissed his reaction as the enormity of fatherhood hitting him.

Back at home I circled the date when we were going to announce the news to everyone.

A few days after the scan I asked Mateo if, when we broke our big news to family and friends, he wanted to also explain we had conceived via a donor egg. It was then that my world fell apart. He got very upset telling me we shouldn’t have gone about it this way. He was, I suspect, frightened of how his family would react. He said that I’d tricked him into going down this route.

His arguments were totally unfair. I’d never lied to him and believed this was the only option left for us. Seeing his obvious distress made me painfully aware of our age gap. He might have been in his mid-30s but he was acting like a schoolboy. He simply wasn’t prepared for the grown-up conversations we needed to have around how we created our family and how we would navigate this together.

My default mode was one of frustration and anger. At the time I had no idea how to process these emotions. I was so tired from the pregnancy and trying to pull my marriage back from the brink. Mateo’s way of dealing with all of it was to avoid me. He’d leave for work first thing and come back late. This behaviour went on for a week, until he told me he didn’t want to be a father this way.

And then, nervous, he suggested perhaps we should make this (pointing to my stomach) go away and start again and see if we could do it naturally. When I asked if he meant undergoing a termination, he nodded. I was speechless, aghast he could suggest such a thing.

I asked him how he’d feel about his precious village knowing we had done that. It was then he burst into tears. Throughout the rest of the evening, he went on and on about how we shouldn’t have conceived this way and how it wasn’t right.

We slept in separate bedrooms that night, and my mind went into survival mode: should I move back to the UK, have our child and tell him where to go?

I realised this just wasn’t an option for me. I didn’t want to be a single parent. I had no long-term financial cushion. I’d read the scary headlines about the six-figure sums required to raise a child.

My parents had died and I didn’t have a vast network of supportive mum friends I could rely on back in the UK.

Despite everything, I still loved Mateo, our life together and his family and friends who had always supported me. I convinced myself we could start over and try again to conceive naturally.

I flew to London where I had a termination, away from the prying eyes of Mateo’s family. I went to the clinic on my own, although I was staying with a friend who was appalled by my predicament. As cowardly as it sounds, I dissociated from my body and the procedure. That was the only way I could get through it without breaking down. While in London, Mateo messaged me continually, checking I was OK, reassuring me how much he loved me.

Even though I’d had the termination because of him, I still wanted to get home, to my place of security and love.

However, from the moment I put the key in our front door, I found myself repulsed by him, I didn’t want him near me. On the first night, feigning sickness, I slept in a separate bedroom.

We struggled along for a few months, neither quite ready to sit down and work out what our future looked like, whether we even had one. I seethed with resentment towards Mateo and what he’d made me do and threw myself into work as a means of coping.

After returning from a week-long work trip, I discovered Mateo had moved in with his mum, a temporary arrangement, apparently. After I’d got over the shock, I realised how relieved I felt to have space.

A couple of days later I heard the familiar sound of his car on the drive. Mateo came in and explained that for his mental health he needed a break. Then, a month later, we had the conversation: he wasn’t coming back.

While a part of me, deep down, knew this was coming, I was still floored. I’d lost everything. My husband, my in-laws, our group of friends... and my chance of having children. As soon as everyone knew, the shutters went down, my in-laws would say hello to me on market days and if I bumped into them, but it was swift. I’m not sure what Mateo had told them about why our relationship had broken down and I didn’t want to know.

That winter passed by in a work-focused blur. When spring arrived with what would have been our due date, I recognised for my own emotional wellbeing, I needed to close this chapter of my life and move away.

Within weeks our house was on the market and a buyer snapped it up. Before I left, I asked Mateo what he wanted us to do with the remaining embryos. As I’d guessed he would, he said they should be destroyed. The same day I emailed the clinic to tell them of our decision, I sank into a black hole. I finally accepted that I needed antidepressants and took a low dose for the next two years.

Nine months after we split, in 2019, I returned to the UK, bought a cottage on the south coast, and have slowly rebuilt my life and myself.

Three years ago, Mateo called with the news he was married and going to be a father. Even though firmly back in the UK, the news still crushed me. I’d done so well in healing and yet I couldn’t stop crying. He deserves to be happy. But more importantly, I deserve happiness too.

I’ve been on a couple of dates in the last few years but it took me an awfully long time to grieve over the child I aborted, the husband I lost and the end of a life I thought I’d have forever.

Have I come to terms with never being a mum? No – and I’m sure every childless woman who wanted children, if they’re being honest with themselves, will know that you never ever do.

Carrie Darbyshire is a pseudonym. All names and identifying details have been changed.

For support and information visit sands.org.uk
