More than 1,000 arrested in France as protesters and police clash

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Updated on: October 25, 2026 / 8:53 PM EDT / CBS/AP

Police made 1,314 arrests in nationwide protests in France, Interior Minister Laurent Nuñez said Sunday, after demonstrations over the high cost of living and the country's education system earlier in the day remained largely peaceful.

Nuñez 53 officers were hurt in marches. He said on Saturday marches "passed off well" in cities, but police officers found themselves confronted with "an extremely elevated level of violence" from "ultra-left" groups later in the day. Nuñez said some shops and businesses had been looted, but "the scale was far smaller" than a week earlier, when some 3,000 businesses were damaged or looted.

He said police acted more rapidly and conducted more pre-demonstration checks. Businesses and shopping centers had also been better protected. Earlier Saturday, the interior minister said police had confiscated weapons including metal balls, lead pellets, fireworks, slingshots, steel balls, improvised weapons, a katana and airsoft weapons, he said.

The French Interior Ministry said about 410,000 people participated in about 298 protests around the country. Union organizers from the General Confederation of Labor put the number at around 800,000.  An explanation for the discrepancy was not immediately available.

Some confrontations were reported between authorities and demonstrators in several cities, but as of Saturday evening there had been no repeat of the major street violence and rioting that erupted amid similar protests last week.

Nuñez previously said that French authorities were preparing to maintain order this weekend by maintaining up to 70,000 officers nationwide along with assets including helicopters, armored vehicles and water cannons.

Nuñez said authorities will remain "on high alert" overnight and into Saturday.

Paris had prepared for unrest, with shops boarded up and events including those related to the NFL's first French football game canceled, but authorities described the atmosphere in the city as "calm."

Thousands gathered at the Porte d'Italie on the city's southeastern rim for an authorized demonstration. The march had originally been set for the Bastille, near the city's center, and was changed to the more remote Porte d'Italie at the last minute. Union representative Murielle Guilbert told reporters that it was "scandalous" that Macron had forbidden protesters from demonstrating "where we wish to demonstrate," but participants complied with the change.

The front of the march ended without incident. Some chanted "We are not happy" as they peeled away from the Porte de Vincennes. Police said 110,000 people participated in the event, while union members put the number at 250,000.

As the evening went on, clashes were reported between police and demonstrators, the Agence France-Presse reported. Nuñez said that "several thousand radical activists" had "gathered at the rear of the march, including around 500 individuals identified as rioters."

Clashes between protesters and police lasted about two hours, he said.

The Paris prosecutor's office said 64 people, including six minors, were arrested and 40 were placed in police custody. Most arrests concerned suspected weapons offenses or participation in a group preparing violence or property damage, the office said. More than 3,000 were stopped and checked by officers, police said.

In Orleans, thousands of people marched against a congress held by Marine Le Pen's far-right National Rally party. Le Pen is launching her campaign to succeed Emmanuel Macron as France's president. Elections will be held in six months, and surveys show Le Pen is best-placed to win. Demonstrators in Orleans protested both Macron and Le Pen.

"Macron resign. Le Pen in prison," the crowd yelled, according to the AFP. Police put their numbers at 11,000, while organizers said 26,000 took part. About 50 organizations, including labor unions, left-wing parties and student groups, had called for a protest against Le Pen's nationalist, anti-immigration party.

Some placards seen at the march referenced a shooting in Trappes, a Paris suburb, that left one person dead and several others injured across several locations. Macron called the rampage a "racist terror attack." Prosecutors are investigating the shooting as a terrorist incident.

French media reported that the protest against the congress ended peacefully early Saturday afternoon. The gathering is expected to continue through Sunday.

Demonstrations had been banned in the southeast city of Lyon, but about 150 protesters near the city's center briefly tried to block a road. Police fired tear gas to disperse the group. Tear gas was also used to break up a group of around 1,000 people gathered in the south of the city. Street fires were also reported.

A police helicopter was seen flying over the city, and security forces, armored vehicles and riot police vans were patrolling. A 21-year-old student, who spoke to the Agence France-Presse on the condition of anonymity, described authorities as "really mobile."

In the western city of Nantes, thousands of protesters broke through police lines to enter the city center, the AFP reported. Officers used tear gas and a water cannon to try and disperse them. Some on the fringes of the demonstration threw projectiles and aimed fireworks at officers.

Police said about 17,000 attended an event in Marseille. Officers fired tear gas during clashes with several dozen people near the march's endpoint. Street fires were seen in the city.

Tear gas was also deployed in Clermont-Ferrand, Bordeaux and Strasbourg, police said. Nuñez said one person was seriously injured in Clermont-Ferrand, but did not provide any details.

In the city of Toulouse, a 20-year-old man reportedly had fingers blown off, Nuñez said. He did not provide more details about the person, their condition, or how they came to be injured.

Nuñez said that in the southern cities of Cannes and Montpellier, authorities saw far-right activists who claimed they were there to protect businesses or sought confrontations with "ultra-left militants." He said it was the first time authorities witnessed this phenomenon.

In Nice, where demonstrations had also been banned, 11 people were arrested after far-right extremists fought with demonstrators downtown, regional authorities said. Water cannons were also used in the city.

One group of protesters, the Yellow Vests, said they would hold another round of protests on Oct. 31. The Yellow Vests emerged about eight years ago as a loosely organized group that staged disruptive protests against Macron.

Organizers called for rallies and a sit-in outside parliament and city halls nationwide, condemning the "authoritarian drift" of Macron's government and urging citizens to stand up for "public freedoms."

The demonstrations have been ongoing since September, when high school students in the southeast of Paris protested for better conditions. The unrest quickly spread across the country as people took to the streets to demand relief from rising living costs.

Other protests took place across Europe on Saturday, encompassing a broad range of issues including pensions, housing costs, anti-war movements, education and corruption.

Italian police said in a statement that they carried out nine arrests and briefly detained 371 people during protests in eight cities on Saturday. Of the people who were detained and taken to police stations, 110 were minors, the statement said. Police on Saturday also said they had arrested a 30-year-old man suspected of being the figure behind the "October 17 Movement," a group which had called for the protests.

Soldiers were deployed to Milan, the nation's fashion and finance capital, ahead of fresh demonstrations one week after looting and police clashes. Many stores were boarded up with wooden planks on Saturday. Others remained defiantly open after a call by the city's mayor Giuseppe Sala not to "panic."

"We want to give the message that we are not scared by these kids," said Alessandro Buhne, 30, who works at the Philipp Plein menswear shop. "Tourists are scared of this situation so we want to give the signal that you are safe if you enter our store."

Italy Premier Giorgia Meloni announced a raft of measures, including sending an additional 500 soldiers to Milan and tougher penalties for minors, who police said made up a large part of the violent protesters.

In Rome, authorities locked down luxury shopping streets and stepped up patrols around key sites. Some protesters briefly broke into the Colosseum, officials said.

Police "managed to isolate and contain the violent fringes, with balance and professionalism," Interior Minister Matteo Piantedosi said, according to Italian news agency ANSA.

"In Italy there can be no room for those who delude themselves that they can act with impunity," he said.

Several hundred students demonstrated peacefully in the historic city. Organizers of peaceful events sought to distance themselves from potential unrest, urging participants to keep their faces uncovered and their hands visible.

In Albania's capital of Tirana, riot police used water cannons to subdue large groups of protesters. Some of the demonstrators threw projectiles at police, the AP reported. A separate protest organized by the opposition outside the office of Prime Minister Edi Rama turned violent earlier Saturday, with some activists lighting fires on the street. The demonstrators are mainly high school and university students, demanding lower tuition fees, greater state investment in education and protesting corruption in the education system. A broader anti-government movement is calling for Rama's resignation.

In Spain, thousands of retirees and others marched through central Madrid to demand that public pensions aren't meddled with.

Protesters in Lisbon, Portugal, are expected to march across the city Saturday to demand affordable housing.

AFP contributed to this report.

Andy Burnham to give homeless policy £432million - despite warnings previous attempts have failed

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published: 00:47 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 03:15 GMT, 26 October 2026

Andy Burnham will today double the funding for tackling homelessness, despite warnings his previous attempts to end the problem have failed.

The Prime Minister will confirm a further £432million in cash as he hosts a 'rough sleeping summit' in Downing Street aimed at delivering on his pledge to offer a 'safe route off the streets' by Christmas.

Ending homelessness was the new PM's first pledge in office. But critics have pointed to his mixed record on the issue as mayor of Greater Manchester.

In 2017, Mr Burnham pledged to end rough sleeping in the region by 2020. But after initial falls, homeless numbers began to rise again during his tenure in the job.

A study by the Centre for Social Justice this year found that homelessness is increasingly fuelled by immigration, with foreign nationals accounting for half of those sleeping rough in London.

Pictures yesterday appeared to show a group of Eastern Europeans sleeping rough on Oxford Street, the day before the capital's premier shopping destination is pedestrianised as part of efforts to rejuvenate the area.

Homeless people have taken to sleeping rough in doorways and under arches along London's Oxford Street

Mr Burnham last night said 'every part of society' has a duty to tackle rough sleeping.

'Rough sleeping isn't a fact of life. It's a thing governments either choose to accept or don't,' he said.

'Well, I don't accept it. That's why we're doubling funding so that people aren't just offered a safe route off the streets this winter but have that same support right through the rest of this Parliament too.'

But shadow communities secretary Katie Lam accused the PM of squandering taxpayers' money on 'empty promises'.

'Burnham is throwing another £432 million at a problem he failed to solve in Greater Manchester,' she said. 'Under Labour, rough sleeping has hit record highs - yet their answer is always more taxpayers' money and more empty promises.'

Brazil election: Five economic challenges facing Brazil’s re-elected President Lula

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Leftist President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva’s (right) won Brazil’s run-off election on Oct 25 and is set to begin his fourth term in January.

BRASILIA – Brazil’s economy has beaten expectations in recent years, but mounting debt and entrenched structural problems could complicate leftist President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva’s fourth term, which begins in January.

Here are the five main economic challenges he faces.

Brazil’s public debt has surged more than 10 percentage points during Lula’s current term to over 82% of gross domestic product, driving up borrowing costs.

The root cause is years of rising mandatory spending, leaving under 8% of the federal budget adjustable.

Lula has sent mixed fiscal signals, at times dismissing debt worries as “nonsense”, while arguing strong economic growth would resolve the problem.

His team has pledged to keep the government’s current budget rules, which cap spending growth and set primary balance targets – revenues minus expenditures, excluding interest payments on public debt – and hinted at tightening them.

Hitting those targets will be harder, given Lula’s promise to raise the minimum wage faster than inflation, which also lifts pension costs, after already raising welfare benefits and promising new spending.

Even with interest rates at 13.75%, Brazil’s annual inflation of 4.6% remains above the central bank’s tolerance range of 1.5% to 4.5%.

The central bank delivered its fifth straight rate cut in September on growing signs of an economic slowdown, but markets expect inflation to remain above the official 3% target until as late as 2029.

Lula’s platform promises lower interest rates through fiscal discipline that spares social programmes, but most economists say rates will only fall faster if Brazil cuts spending, and it is unclear how he will square the two.

A record nearly 84 million Brazilians and 9.3 million businesses are behind on debt payments, according to credit bureau Serasa, as high interest rates bite.

That has fuelled public dissatisfaction despite strong indicators such as record-low unemployment and wage growth outpacing inflation.

Lula plans a new round of debt relief, this time through discounted purchases of distressed household loans from banks, plus tax debt forgiveness for some small business owners. Analysts say such measures offer only temporary relief.

The government has also discussed with the central bank ways to rein in heavy use of easy but expensive credit, such as credit cards and unsecured consumer loans, though it has not detailed any proposals.

Lula will likely struggle to push his economic agenda through Congress after voters elected the most conservative legislature since Brazil’s return to democracy in the 1980s.

Although he defeated right-wing Senator Flavio Bolsonaro in the run-off on Oct 25, Bolsonaro’s Liberal Party and its allies are set to control both chambers.

Lula’s aides have signalled he will keep pushing to curtail tax breaks, but lawmakers have repeatedly resisted measures that raise the effective tax burden.

During his current term, Lula has struggled to name nominees and win Senate approval for them at key institutions, including the Supreme Court, central bank, securities regulator and antitrust watchdog, and that is likely to be even more difficult ahead with the rightward shift of Congress.

Lula won congressional approval in 2023 for a sweeping overhaul of Brazil’s consumption taxes, and a new value-added tax is set to take effect in January.

With under three months to go, the government has still not disclosed the effective rate, which independent analysts estimate will be around 28%.

Fearing a backlash during the campaign, the administration has also held off on sending Congress a Bill to regulate a new tax on products considered harmful to health or the environment, such as cigarettes and alcohol.

That delay could push back revenue from the tax and has fuelled criticism of the government’s handling of tax policy. REUTERS

Trump critic Lula wins reelection in Brazil, securing fourth presidential term

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brazil’s President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, a vocal critic of U.S. President Donald Trump, has been reelected in a result that provides relief to critics of Trump and keeps some distance between Latin America’s biggest economy and Washington

RIO DE JANEIRO -- Brazil’s President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, a vocal critic of U.S. President Donald Trump, was reelected Sunday in a result that keeps some distance between Latin America’s biggest economy and Washington.

With 100% of the vote counted, Brazil’s electoral court announced that the soon to be 81-year-old Lula held an unassailable lead. The announcement capped an evening of chaos at polling places across Brazil, following a last-minute decision to extend voting by two hours. That decision by a Supreme Court justice may trigger legal action by opponent Flávio Bolsonaro.

His triumph bucks a trend in Latin America where increasing numbers of right-of-center presidental candidates allied with Trump have been winning, further extending the United States’ influence in the region. Lula’s victory leaves Mexico and Brazil, Latin America’s two most populous nations, with left-of-center leaders. It also positions Latin America’s largest economy, with a GDP of $2.6 trillion per year, to remain independent of the U.S., despite the pressure of increased tariffs imposed by Trump.

The announcement kicked off a celebration of supporters in and out the Sao Paulo hotel where Lula watched the count with First Lady Rosângela da Silva, allies, and friends.

Lula’s victory, if confirmed by electoral authorities, will give him his non-consecutive fourth term as president. The next term begins on Jan. 5, 2027.

It is the first time since Brazil’s military dictatorship ended in 1985 that the second-highest vote getter in the first-round elections won the presidency. Bolsonaro had 47% of the vote in the first round to Lula’s 45%, a difference of 2.2 million votes.

Lula and Sen. Bolsonaro have yet to address their supporters.

Sunday’s victory will likely be the last of the veteran leader’s political career. Lula began his political career in the 1970s as a union leader. He then helped found the Workers’ Party, which he represented as a presidential candidate three times until he finally won it in 2002. He was reelected four years later and was succeeded by one of his cabinet ministers, Dilma Rousseff, in 2010. She was reelected in 2014.

Rousseff was impeached for accounting maneuvering in 2016 amid a sprawling corruption probe, which also ensnared Lula. He tried to run for president again in 2018 but was jailed in March after being convicted.

Lula was released from prison after 580 days and saw Brazil’s Supreme Court annul his sentence after police found messages that showed the judge who convicted him was biased. He quickly became one of former President Jair Bolsonaro’s biggest critics and narrowly beat him in 2022.

Lula admitted in 2024, two years into the job, that his administration had done little that far to earn the support of his voters.

Since he lost the first round, Lula reorganized his campaign, appealed to young voters, put millions of Brazilians on the streets to defend democratic values and his legacy, raised fears of another Bolsonaro administration and boosted several government initiatives.

Lula also defended Brazil’s sovereignty and insisted that a Bolsonaro presidency would allow the U.S. to exert control over Brazil.

He joins Trump and Nicaragua’s President Daniel Ortega — all age 80 — as the oldest presidents in the Americas.

Savarese reported from Sao Paulo. Brigida reported from Brasilia.

How a movement of students brought down Serbia's Vučić

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Although it was a students' movement that won Serbia's election, no student has been elected

Theirs is a movement born from tragedy that began with students blockading streets and turned into almost daily protests and a national movement against corruption and government negligence.

In just under two years the students have succeeded in tearing up 14 years of political dominance under Aleksandar VuÄiÄ, who has seen support for his Serbian Progressive party (SNS) ebb away and ultimately fail in what became a referendum on the future of Serbia.

From Belgrade to Novi Sad and NiÅ¡, the mood was euphoric as supporters gathered in town centres or in front of Student List headquarters. In a post on social media, the students declared "Serbia is free".

Most symbolic of all was the demise of so-called Äaciland, a pro-VuÄiÄ tent camp set up 18 months ago at Pioneers Park in central Belgrade in front of the Serbian parliament. Metal fences were torn down amid shouts of "VuÄiÄ is leaving".

They have no obvious leaders and have no grounding in politics, and yet the "Student List-Students Win" movement attracted anti-government voters across Serbian society.

They fielded candidates for all 250 parliamentary seats, and yet no students were among them.

As he joined the students marching towards Belgrade last week, lead candidate and cardiologist Ilija SrdanoviÄ said history was being rewritten: "The energy is unstoppable."

"We offer our citizens the core values of civilised society, which this previous regime has taken away from our citizens for the last time," he told the BBC.

It all began on 1 November 2024, when the concrete canopy collapsed outside Novi Sad train station, in Serbia's second biggest city, killing 16 people.

The tragedy was seen as emblematic of widespread corruption. Critics linked the station's fatally flawed renovation to a president who had closely associated himself with the project.

"When the canopy collapsed that served as the last straw," says Helena Ivanov of the think tank the Henry Jackson Society.

It was an emotional moment that triggered a spark and a national campaign of marches and events, she said, that captured the hearts and minds of Serbians in a way no opposition party had been able to do.

Serbia's veteran ruler VuÄiÄ concedes election to student-led protest movement

Student movement takes Serbia's VuÄiÄ to the wire in neck-and-neck election

Aleksandar VuÄiÄ conceded victory, but said the students' movement would fail

What started as weekly student protests demanding justice on university campuses and city streets soon became a national movement, often at a local level, that encompassed far more sections of Serbian society, including farmers, actors and judges.

VuÄiÄ's SNS was clearly shaken and by January 2025 his prime minister had resigned "to defuse tensions". In March 2025 Belgrade saw Serbia's biggest ever anti-government protest and as the leaderless student movement increased in strength, they organised themselves through student assemblies or plenums.

By the time VuÄiÄ had announced snap elections last month, they were ready. "They have been able to take that emotional momentum into a proper organisation and have people learn how to protect fairness at polling stations," Helena Ivanov explained.

"The message was clear: the rule of law and not ruled by the law," said Gordana DeliÄ, head of the Balkans programme at the German Marshall Fund of the US, who said Sunday's vote was in effect a referendum.

Initially VuÄiÄ sought to praise the protesters as "well-intentioned", but ahead of the elections he accused them of trying to "bring down" Serbia. Even after conceding victory on Sunday night he made the unfounded allegation that they had the support of unnamed "European countries and the American deep state".

Ilija SrdanoviÄ topped the Student List's candidates but is not seen as a likely prime minister

One thing is clear, it will not be the students, as they were not standing for election. That was a calculated decision as the electorate would probably not voted for a list of candidates with little life experience.

Although their top candidate was Ilija SrdanoviÄ, a cardiology professor in Novi Sad, he is not really seen as a likely prime minister.

Few candidates stand out as potential leaders.

At least 135 people on the list are thought to have won seats in parliament, and they include ex-basketball star Dejan Bodiroga (24th on the list), renowned actor Tihomir StaniÄ (8th), opera singer Nikola MikiÄ (46th), former National Bank of Serbia governor Dejan Å oÅ¡kiÄ (27th), and a rather large number of professors.

Å oÅ¡kiÄ's name has been mentioned in connection with the top post. He has spoken out about reforms he would make to the economy, and he has been widely criticised in pro-government media.

The elected MPs will have the task of fulfilling what is in effect a manifesto.

In the words of the students themselves, they were looking for people who could enter institutions "do their jobs responsibly and not forget why they are there".

Under an 11-point programme, , externalthe student movement wants to:

Eradicate corruption by forming a police unit to arrest those responsible for it

Purge officials in the internal affairs ministry of anyone who broke rules on cracking down on protesters, or anyone linked to organised crime

Reform Serbia's skewed media scene and free the public broadcaster of political control

Stop recruiting party loyalists to head public enterprises.

VuÄiÄ and the SNS party deny corruption and seeking to influence the media.

Although they have not been elected, it is the students who have made the promises to the Serbian people and they will come under pressure to follow through on their manifesto.

The ordinary Serbian citizens they chose to represent their movement have "been entrusted with transferring part of the struggle we have been waging on the streets for the past year into the institutions", the students say.

In his speech on Sunday evening, VuÄiÄ told party supporters that the students had destroyed two years of life in Serbia and they would be defeated "faster than they think".

Brazil's Lula wins historic presidential election; Bolsonaro reportedly refuses to concede

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva has won an unprecedented fourth term as Brazil's president, overturning a first-round deficit to beat Senator Flávio Bolsonaro in a runoff that split the country almost evenly.

Lula took 51.8% of valid votes to Bolsonaro's 48.2%, according to the Superior Electoral Court, with nearly all ballots counted on Sunday.

The results are also historic as it's the first time since Brazil returned to direct elections in 1989 that a candidate has won the final runoff after losing in the first round.

Bolsonaro refused to concede defeat, saying he would seek the annulment of the outcome, Reuters reported. He also said a ruling by the Supreme Court Justice Cristiano Zanin to extend voting for two hours was "unacceptable," according to the report.

The election process was marred by allegations of voter suppression, with Lula's campaign reporting suspended public transportation in some key areas, prompting the court to extend voting by two hours. Bolsonaro's campaign had also requested an extension, citing heavy rains that shut polling stations in the south, a right-wing stronghold.

The victory of the 80-year-old leftist, who had trailed in the first round, blocks a rightward shift in Latin America that U.S. President Donald Trump has backed.

Trump, who had labeled Bolsonaro's lead in the first round as "a very, very big victory," would have preferred a Brazilian in closer alignment to U.S. foreign policy as Washington makes efforts to project U.S. power across the Americas, according to Daniela Portella Sampaio, senior research fellow for Latin American Security at the U.K. think tank Royal United Services Institute.

Lula, who has publicly sparred with Trump, is set to govern from 2027 to 2030, facing a Congress where Bolsonaro's Liberal Party holds the largest bench in decades, and with a substantial public debt that he would need to stabilize.

Brazil, under Lula's leadership, has grappled with worsening fiscal health and mounting public debt driven by double-digit interest rates, elevated cost of servicing the debt, and rigid mandatory spending. Gross debt reached 82.9% of the country's GDP in August, the highest since March 2021, according to the Central Bank of Brazil.

Investors are bracing for heightened volatility across Brazilian markets on Monday following Lula's victory as the right-wing Bolsonaro was seen as more likely to push for fiscal austerity and deregulation.

The iShares MSCI Brazil ETF (EWZ) plunged nearly 20% in overnight trading following election results.

Analysts had expected a rally in Brazilian assets before Sunday's election results, with Bolsonaro holding a narrow lead earlier this month. Chris Turner, Global Head of Markets and Regional Head of Research for UK & CEE at ING, had forecast both the currency and bond markets to rally if Bolsonaro won.

The new president will need to convince investors that Brazil can stabilize its debt trajectory, said Alejo Czerwonko, a fellow at the Atlantic Council. Lula's administration will likely extend the more "gradual approach," building on the existing fiscal framework while seeking to preserve public investment and social spending, Czerwonko said.

Goldman Sachs analysts expect "modest measures" to contain the government's spending growth, alongside further increases in an already high tax burden, while major structural reforms remain unlikely.

Leaders from across Latin America largely congratulated Lula's historic win.

Peruvian President Keiko Fujimori, in a post on X late Sunday, hailed Lula's victory, calling it a "democratic day" for the Brazilian people. Uruguay President Orsi Yamandu congratulated Lula over a call, calling the Brazilian president a "friend."

Lula's victory came the same day as Serbia's long-serving strongman leader, Aleksandar Vucic, conceded defeat in a parliamentary election to the student-led opposition movement, ending 14 years of leadership.

— CNBC's Matthew Tan contributed to this report.

'Having enough food to protect your family during a disaster isn't selfish': Middle-class women prepping for World War III reveal what they really stockpile amid Labour's doomsday guide

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By JESSICA GREEN, ASSISTANT LIFESTYLE EDITOR

Andy Burnham's Government has warned people in the UK to 'be ready' for a national crisis, urging households to stockpile three days of food and water and start carrying cash.

But these middle-class women need no such advice, because they are part of a growing number of professionals already preparing their families for when 'disaster strikes', be that a natural catastrophe or even the start of World War III.

Amid conflict in the Middle East and the Russia-Ukraine war, as well as cyber attacks and extreme weather, becoming a 'prepper' is a flourishing trend – and not only with middle-aged men who love cargo trousers and boast underground bunkers.

Plenty of professional ladies across Britain have now joined the movement, especially since the coronavirus pandemic. Wife and mother-of-one Ana, based in Wales, is one of those prepared women.

She has been prepping for years but stepped up her efforts during the Covid lockdowns when she couldn't find baby formula for her newborn.

The 41-year-old now has cupboards filled with flour, oats, lentils, beans and other tinned goods, ready to cook from scratch for at least two months, alongside medicine and cleaning supplies.

She welcomed the government's advice to the UK - but warned it doesn't go far enough, insisting that households should be encouraged to stockpile seven days worth, or even two weeks, of food and water.

'Having enough food to protect your family during a disruption isn’t selfish,' insisted Ana. She told The Daily Mail: 'There’s also a difference between gradually building a sensible reserve and panic-buying.

Plenty of professional ladies across Britain have now joined the prepping movement, especially since the coronavirus pandemic. Wife and mother-of-one Ana (pictured), based in Wales, is one of those prepared women

Fellow 'prepper' Dr Sarita Robinson (pictured), an associate dean in the school of psychology and humanities at the University of Lancashire, insisted: 'Prepping should not increase panic and anxiety

'In fact, if more households can look after themselves during an emergency, that could leave more support available for those who genuinely need it. Preparedness should also be about helping our neighbours and communities, not just ourselves.'

She noted how 'unfortunately, prepping still comes with stereotypes of bunkers and doomsday scenarios, when for most of us it’s simply about being a little more self-reliant,' adding: 'Households should take some responsibility for their own resilience.'

However, she called for the government to 'continue investing in the infrastructure and emergency services we all depend upon and start being a little bit more transparent of the challenges ahead, as there are still people that think that current state of the world is just happy and dandy'.

Fellow 'prepper' Dr Sarita Robinson, an associate dean in the school of psychology and humanities at the University of Lancashire, insisted: 'Prepping should not increase panic and anxiety.

'It should actually help people to feel reassured that they would be able look after their household without relying on others.'

She added: 'Building resilience in the UK like other countries around the world is important. If other countries can do it so can we. The best time to prepare for an emergency is before it starts! During an emergency it's too late.'

Sarita started prepping after living in LA for a year in 2004, and experiencing an earthquake. 'It really made me reflect on how quickly the world could change from being safe and predictable to being dangerous and chaotic.'

But it was the global outbreak of the coronavirus that finally proved to her friends and family that her prepping over the last 20 years wasn't 'bonkers'.

'Most people are a lot more on board with the prepping now,' said the 50-year-old, from Preston, who's married to a retired computer programmer with two children in their 20s.

Noting the rise of female preppers, Sarita said: 'I think since the pandemic more people are aware that things can go wrong quite quickly... the world does seem a little more unpredictable at the moment.'

She added: 'I have definitely seen friends and family taking prepping more seriously. I have even been asked for "go bags" (an emergency kit) as Christmas presents!'

Dr Sarita Robinson is just one of a growing number of women in the UK who are readying themselves and their families for when 'disaster strikes again'

Citing negative stereotypes of 'grumpy middle-aged men surrounded by cans of food or in a bunker', Ana (pictured) insisted: 'I'm not prepping for an apocalypse or zombie invasion'

Should the government be urging us to prepare for disasters, or is this fearmongering that risks causing panic?

Sarita believes that everyone should be a prepper, suggesting that if everyone who can afford to preps, then local governments' limited resources can go straight to vulnerable groups such as the elderly.

She added: 'I am hopeful that everyone will undertake some preparations for emergencies, even if it is just updating their first aid training and buying some extra canned food. In the heat of an emergency these things can make a real difference.'

Sarita has completed a range of training in order to survive a disaster – including something as simple as first aid, to the more unusual skills such as helicopter underwater evacuation, firefighting and wilderness survival.

She revealed that her cupboards are a 'little more packed than most people' and she keeps plastic storage boxes under the stairs with most of the tins and dried packets she has, having around three months of food in her house.

Meanwhile, Ana, who has at least 32,000 followers and growing across her Instagram and YouTube accounts, founded the UK Preppers Club on Facebook, which has more than 10,000 members, keeps a 'well-stocked working pantry with everyday foods, including tinned goods, dried ingredients, beans, pulses and ready-to-eat options'.

Ana, who also supports food banks, added: 'I also have a medium-term pantry with a few extras of everyday foods and items and a long-term pantry with goods that can last a very long time.

'I also grow and preserve food, which forms part of our household resilience and extra pantry supplies.'

All her vehicles have small packs of essentials inside, filled with water, power banks, warm clothing, waterproofs, snacks, a micro cooking stove and a water filter.

'I believe in making use of the space you already have rather than needing a dedicated stockpile room. Cupboards, pantry shelves and other suitable storage spaces can all be useful.

'The important thing is keeping everything organised, stored correctly and rotated. I prefer a working pantry where we regularly use and replace our supplies rather than food sitting forgotten for years.

Sarita (pictured) believes that everyone should be a prepper, suggesting that if everyone who can afford to preps, then local governments' limited resources can go straight to vulnerable groups such as the elderly

She added: 'I’d really like to emphasise that preparedness shouldn’t be a privilege reserved for people with spare money.

'Some of the most valuable things we can do cost absolutely nothing. Learning useful skills, making a family emergency plan or exchanging contact details with neighbours can all make a difference,' said Ana.

She said of the government's guidance: 'I’d like to see clearer quantities and advice on what families should do if toilets stop flushing, heating goes off or they can’t cook.

'Some information is available elsewhere on the website, but I think the main checklist could be more comprehensive.

'Start with what you already have and build gradually and you don’t need expensive survival equipment. Naturally I’d recommend keeping foods you normally eat, including ready-to-eat meals, tinned fish, beans, oatcakes, crackers, nuts and long-life milk.

'Make sure you have enough for proper meals, not just a collection of tins. Alongside food and water, I’d include a manual tin opener, torches, batteries , a power bank, radio, medication, first aid supplies, toilet roll, soap, heavy-duty bin bags, blankets and some cash.

'Most importantly, make a household plan. Ask yourself how you’d feed everyone, keep warm and manage basic hygiene if the power and water stopped tomorrow.'

Ana insisted the preppers she knows are just 'ordinary, sane people'. 'Many are women, parents, families, people who simply want stability and security. They are gardeners, cooks, teachers, office workers, nothing like the stereotypes you see in films.'

Citing negative stereotypes of 'grumpy middle-aged men surrounded by cans of food or in a bunker,' Ana insisted: 'I'm not prepping for an apocalypse or zombie invasion.

'Most of the things I prepare for are actually very ordinary. Economic pressures, price spikes, supply disruptions, storms, power cuts or simply times when life becomes more difficult financially.'

She insisted that preparedness 'is really about softening the impact of unforeseen circumstances, rather than preparing yourself for the end of the world'.

'Having a stocked pantry, knowing how to cook from basic ingredients, growing a little food and understanding where your food comes from - those things create resilience in everyday life and in my eyes that's what prepping truly is,' insisted the mother.

Ana continued: 'These are things people did for generations before supermarkets made us rely on weekly shopping. So none of it is a new concept, just a forgotten way of thinking for many.'

Likewise, Sarita, who appeared as a psychology expert in Pompeii: Out of Time with Tom Hiddleston on Disney+ recently, insisted: 'Most of the preppers I know are completely normal people.'

The mother-of-two revealed that parents of young children make up a lot of the prepping folk she knows.

In recent times, mothers across Britain have taken to prepper Facebook groups, asking where to begin when it comes to becoming a 'doomsday prepper'.

Sarita suggested buying extra food is a good place to start. 'It's just food that we would normally eat, we just buy extras. So instead of buying a small pack of rice we would buy several kilos at a time.

'The great thing is that this actually works out a lot cheaper in the long run. I think people think you have to do something special when you are stockpiling food but it just isn't the case.

'You should just buy food that you would normally eat but just have a little bit extra each week.'

Sarita – who doesn't have a bunker (a house modification some international preppers boast of), but wouldn't be averse to purchasing one if she could – also keeps a supply of over-the-counter medicines, as well as other basics such as water purification tablets, a wind-up radio, a battery pack and power banks for mobile phones.

'It's also important to keep some treats and a good supply of teabags, sugar and some UHT milk. You can recover from most emergencies as long as you have some hot, sweet tea and biscuits available,' added the professional.

Ready-to-eat meals, tinned fish, meat, beans and vegetables

Crackers, oatcakes, peanut butter, nuts and long-life milk

Dried staples such as rice, pasta and oats, if cooking is possible

Children’s favourite foods and special dietary requirements

At least three litres of drinking water per person per day

Additional water for cooking, washing and hygiene, extra two litres per person roughly

Clean, food-grade water storage containers

Water purification tablets or suitable filter as a backup

Toilet roll, soap, hand sanitiser and wet wipes

Toothpaste, toothbrushes and personal hygiene products

Heavy-duty bin bags, cleaning supplies and disposable gloves

Bucket, secure lid and liners for emergency toilet arrangements if sewage services fail

Nappies, sanitary products and other individual essentials

Torches, battery-operated lanterns and spare batteries

Fully charged power banks and charging cables

Optional portable power station for essential appliances

Blankets, sleeping bags and warm clothing

Working smoke alarms and carbon monoxide alarms

First aid kit and suitable everyday medicines

Copies of medication lists and important medical information

Backup arrangements for essential medical equipment
